After forming the conditional logarithm every differentiated connected cluster meets its designated source seed. The resulting boundary activities have jets of order \(m\) bounded by \(C m!(C'/r)^m e^{-\kappa' d_1(X)}\) in their stated charts. On the real law the first and mixed second density coefficients are the conditional first moment and conditional product moment; the mixed second logarithmic coefficient is the conditional covariance. The original unnormalized mass multiplies the resulting sourced density.

For the old copy of one such component-owned boundary activity at a receiving \(T'_k\), the admitted LFR II (1.60), (1.65) field substitution and (MR.13) give
\[
 |T'_{k,c}[bF]|\le e^{\varepsilon_c}\|b\|_{\rm old}\,(T'_{k,r}1)\|F\|_\infty .
 \tag{O.23}
\]
Here the receiving assignment and old coordinate domain are part of the assertion. This handles a mark after actual constrained regular integration and retains the compulsory mass. Middle steps also create new conditional differences; estimate (O.23) alone cannot sum those terms to arbitrary depth. First constrained boundary readout, (C.1)–(C.8), with primary clauses

### 17. Conditioning on all links outside the source region

The selected-sequence contact law also controls an exterior conditional mean. Let \(D\) be a bounded open physical region with \(\operatorname{supp} f\) compactly inside it, and let \(\mathcal E_{\rm out}(D)\) reveal every link outside its interior (including links crossing its boundary). Use either scalar placement in §15. Then
\[
 \|\mathbb E[Z_m(f)\mid \mathcal E_{\rm out}(D)]\|_2\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \left\|\mathbb E[e^{itZ_m(f)}\mid\mathcal E_{\rm out}(D)]
           -e^{-t^2 A_0\int f^2/2}\right\|_1\longrightarrow0
 \quad(t\in\mathbb R). \tag{O.24}
\]
In particular the estimate is uniform against bounded observables of all those exterior links, even when the observable varies with the cutoff.

To prove this, at each fixed side \(l\) hide only those tiling interiors meeting the fluctuating source stencil; equivalently hide the interiors of all boxes in a fixed buffer strictly inside \(D\) containing this stencil. All other links are revealed. For sufficiently fine lattices this visible sigma field contains \(\mathcal E_{\rm out}(D)\). Conditional independence and the fixed-box moment and variance estimates in §15 give the same limit \(A_l\int f^2\) for the within-box variance. By (O.17) the squared \(L^2\) norm of the visible conditional mean tends to \((A_0-A_l)\int f^2\). Project onto \(\mathcal E_{\rm out}(D)\), then let \(l\to\infty\), to obtain the first part of (O.24). The conditional characteristic function of the centered box sum converges in \(L^1\) to the constant for variance \(A_l\int f^2\), by the conditional Lindeberg argument. Adding the visible mean costs at most \(|t|\) times its \(L^2\) norm. Conditional expectation again contracts the error. Taking \(l\to\infty\) gives the second conclusion. Equation (O.20) transfers both estimates to the four-vertex source by conditional contraction. The same argument works with a shrinking deterministic buffer of width \(d_m\) around the support if \(d_m/a_m\to\infty\), since each fixed box and the placement stencils eventually fit.

The conditional mean in (O.24) still includes the factor \(a^2\beta\). Its convergence gives no \(O(1)\) rate for the unscaled insertion. The physical terminal readout in (MR.27) also retains coarse observations inside the support; that sigma field need not be contained in the exterior used in (O.24).

### 18. Bare local correlations and a growing ultraviolet window

The energy selection in §14 has a second use. It fixes the background left by a Dirichlet integration when averaging the root over its position relative to the box. The following statements concern the actual full Wilson plaquette energies on the selected sequence, at separations measured first in **fine lattice units**. Use a color basis orthonormal for \(-\operatorname{Tr}(XY)\). Let \(P\) be orthogonal projection in \(\ell^2\) of oriented plaquettes of the infinite lattice onto the closure of curls of finitely supported real link potentials. Give each of the \(d_A\) color components of a centered Gaussian curl \(G\) covariance \(P\), independently across colors. This defines its joint laws on finite sets of plaquettes.

**Fixed microscopic stencil.** For any finite set of plaquettes, the variables \(\beta s_p\) converge jointly in law and in all fixed moments to \(\tfrac12\sum_b(G_p^b)^2\). For clarity this statement needs no common gauge across different boxes. Take a uniformly translated fixed-side box tiling, independently of the configuration, and then average. By translation invariance the unconditional law of the original stencil is unchanged. On the event that it lies well inside one box with near-flat boundary, use one forest gauge for all its matrices. The conditional centered fluctuations \(\sqrt\beta(U_p-\mathbb E[U_p\mid\mathcal E_l])\) then converge jointly at fixed \(l\) and root position to the flat linear curls. This is the Taylor and uniform Gaussian moment argument of §14. Finite-dimensional Gaussian linear algebra identifies their plaquette covariance with \(P_{\rm box}\), projection onto curls of interior link variations extended by zero: in free coordinates the action Hessian is \(D^*D\) for this injective curl \(D\), giving \(D(D^*D)^{-1}D^*\). Boundary variation from the flat orbit tends to zero in probability at fixed \(l\).

To replace the conditional mean matrix by identity one must average its positive cost. In each good box sum the first terms of (O.19) over its fluctuating plaquettes. After multiplication by \(\beta\) their expected sum tends to \(d_Ah_l/2\). The total energy per box, including one period's assigned visible plaquettes, has scaled expectation tending to \(A_0l^4\). The expected sum of the second terms of (O.19) on good boxes is bounded by the difference. Random offset therefore bounds the expected squared length of the unwanted \(\sqrt\beta(\mathbb E[U_p\mid\mathcal E_l]-I)\) at any one specified good interior plaquette by \(C(A_0-d_Ah_l/(2l^4))+o_l(1)\); nonnegativity permits restricting the sum to its orientation. Here the small error tends to zero in the cutoff limit at fixed \(l\). For a finite stencil sum this estimate over its roots. The event that the stencil comes within \(r\) sites of a box wall has probability \(O((r+\operatorname{diam}(\mathrm{stencil}))/l)\). First take the cutoff limit, then take \(l\) large compared with \(r\). The scaled mean error tends to zero in probability in these ordered limits.

For boxes containing an \(r\)-neighborhood of the stencil, \(P_r\le P_{\rm box}\le P\) as quadratic forms, using any fixed inner box centered on that stencil. Increasing the inner boxes exhausts the finitely supported curls, so \(P_r\to P\) strongly; in particular all the required finite covariance matrices converge. This proves the asserted convergence in law by the continuous mapping theorem and \(s_p=\|U_p-I\|_{\rm HS}^2/2\). Uniform higher one-star moments give convergence of each product moment. The proof applies to gauge-invariant energy observables; using matrix entries before taking norms requires keeping the common local gauge choice.

**The coefficient at separated lattice sites.** Write \(e_x=\sum_{\alpha}s_{p_\alpha(x)}\) for the literal lower-vertex scalar. For fixed integer \(r\) its limiting scaled covariance is
\[
 C_{\rm G}(r)=\lim_m\beta_m^2\operatorname{Cov}(e_0,e_r)
          =\frac{d_A}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}|P_{\alpha\gamma}(r)|^2 .
 \tag{O.25}
\]
The equality follows by Gaussian contraction. At nonzero Fourier momentum the symbol of \(P\) projects onto \(\hat k\wedge\mathbb R^4\), where \(\hat k_i=2\sin(k_i/2)\), with the appropriate unit-modulus link and plaquette midpoint phases. Indeed the curl symbol is exterior multiplication by \(i\hat k\) in midpoint coordinates; the closure of its image in \(L^2\) is the pointwise image almost everywhere, by inversion away from \(k=0\) and truncation. Near zero the anchored symbol differs from the continuum projection by \(O(|k|)\), with derivative bounds \(O_j(|k|^{1-j})\). Away from zero the original anchored symbol is smooth periodic. The continuum kernel off zero is \(\Lambda^2(I-2\hat r\hat r^\top)/(\pi^2 |r|^4)\), \(\hat r=r/|r|\), by differentiating the scalar Green function \(1/(4\pi^2|r|^2)\). To bound the error, a smooth dyadic cutoff at frequency \(h\) on the difference has inverse-transform bound \(C_M h^5(1+h|r|)^{-M}\) by integration by parts. Summing yields \(O(|r|^{-5})\) for each entry. Cutting off the continuum homogeneous symbol away from zero changes its large-distance kernel by a rapidly decaying term: derivatives of order greater than four of its high-frequency part are integrable. Since the exterior-square reflection is orthogonal on the six-dimensional two-form space,
\[
 C_{\rm G}(r)=\frac{3d_A}{\pi^4 |r|^8}+O_N(|r|^{-9}) .
 \tag{O.26}
\]
The factor corresponds to the bare energy whose Gaussian limit is one half the independent-pair sum of squared normalized curls. Free coefficient and trace conventions, (UV.1)–(UV.5)

**Extent of the Wilson conclusion.** There exist integer \(R_m\to\infty\), with \(a_mR_m\to0\), for which
\[
 \sup_{|r|\le R_m}(1+|r|)^8
    \big|\beta_m^2\operatorname{Cov}(e_0,e_r)-C_{\rm G}(r)\big|\ \longrightarrow\ 0 . \tag{O.27}
\]
For each fixed integer \(j\) there are finitely many \(r\) with \(|r|\le j\). On a sufficiently late tail the supremum there with weight \((1+j)^8\) is below \(1/j\), and \(j a_m<1/j\). Choose increasing tail indices and let \(R_m\) increase by these steps. No speed of growth is obtained. Equations (O.25)–(O.27) give a dimension-eight **upper** bound for the actual two-point function out to \(R_m\) fine spacings and the coefficient in (O.26) along every \(1\ll|r|\le R_m\). In physical units the bound for the covariance of \(\beta_m a_m^{-4}e_x\) is \(C|a_mr|^{-8}\) in this range. All histories are included and no source-placement conversion enters this microscopic statement. The largest guaranteed physical distance still shrinks to zero. The missing bound from \(a_mR_m\) to fixed physical distances, the normalization at a fixed physical scale, and passage to a selected phase prevent this window from establishing either full S2 or R6.

### 19. The mean energy at a prescribed bare coupling

Here is a finite-volume pressure comparison that removes the energy-selection hypothesis of §§14 and 18. Let the four-torus of side \(n\) admit a tiling by boxes of integral side \(l\), with \(2l\le n\). Fix \(G=SU(N)\) and retain the Wilson normalization of (O.19). The constants below may depend on \(N\) and the fixed blocking factor. At large \(\beta\) choose a divisor \(l\asymp\beta^{1/1000}\). Such divisors exist on the compatible tori \(n=2L^m\) whenever \(L^m\) dominates this power, by rounding down to a power of \(L\). The same \(l\) will be used at all comparison couplings \(v\in[\beta/2,2\beta]\). Write \(r_F\) for the number of free links after tree gauge fixing on the graph of the strictly interior vertices \(\{1,\ldots,l-1\}^4\) with all its elementary plaquettes. There is a constant \(c_l\) independent of \(v,n\) such that
\[
 \left|\psi_n(v)-c_l+\frac{d_A r_F}{2l^4}\log v\right|
       \le C_N\frac{\log\beta}{l}. \tag{O.28}
\]
Related Gaussian box comparisons for free energy were developed for \(U(N)\) by Chatterjee. We give the boundary and gauge counts here for the periodic \(SU(N)\) Wilson measure used in this manuscript. [Chatterjee, *The leading term of the Yang–Mills free energy*](https://arxiv.org/abs/1602.01222)

**Bracketing.** The upper bound uses the product of free interior-box integrals \(Z_F(v)\), dropping every other nonnegative plaquette action. For the lower bound keep each link in the boundary subcomplex of the tiling within Hilbert–Schmidt distance \(\beta^{-2}\) of identity. There are at most \(C l^3\) such links per tile. Normalized Haar measure assigns each ball mass at least \(c_N\beta^{-2d_A}\). At fixed seam links all hidden interiors integrate independently. Replacing the constrained seam links by identity changes the sum of all actions by at most \(C_N l^3\beta^{-2}\) per tile, since only incident plaquettes change, and products of real unitary matrices telescope. Visible-only plaquettes at identity have zero action. Let \(Z_D(v)\) integrate the remaining links in a closed tile with boundary links fixed at identity and all its plaquettes. Boundary-only faces can be counted more than once in this last definition since each has zero action. Consequently
\[
 l^{-4}\log Z_D(v)-C_N l^{-1}\log\beta
      \le \psi_n(v)\le l^{-4}\log Z_F(v). \tag{O.29}
\]

**Uniform evaluation of the two boxes.** Choose any spanning tree of the strict interior graph. In the Dirichlet box add one link from the tree to the boundary. This fixes all interior vertex gauges; all remaining boundary vertices are fixed roots. In both boxes the remaining independent group coordinates have normalized product Haar measure, by successive leaf integrations or by Haar invariance along the tree. For the free box one residual global conjugation leaves the action invariant and has mass one. Write \(r_j\) for the number of independent links, \(j=F,D\); \(r_D=h_l\), \(r_F=4(l-2)(l-1)^3-(l-1)^4+1\), and \(r_D-r_F=O(l^3)\). A spanning tree on the connected boundary subcomplex completes the relative forest to a tree of the full Dirichlet box, all its links equal to identity.

Every independent link in the chosen slice equals the holonomy of a based fundamental loop up to inversion. The loop has length \(O(l^4)\) and a filling by \(O(l^8)\) elementary plaquette moves within its box. To see this, sort its direction letters by coordinate index, swapping successive different axes across their square, then cancel opposite steps on each remaining one-dimensional segment. Every swap is inside the coordinate box: its fourth corner takes coordinates separately from the two original neighbors. After sorting, each one-dimensional closed segment reduces by backtracking. For the real group the discrepancy in each move is at most \(\max_p\|U_p-I\|_{\rm HS}\) by unitary invariance. Linearizing the same moves bounds the largest free link by \(C l^8\max_p |(D_j x)_p|\), where \(D_j\) is the scalar curl on the sliced coordinates. It follows that \(G_j=D_j^*D_j\) has spectrum in \([c l^{-20},C]\). The upper bound follows from bounded row and column degree.

Pick \(b=\beta^{-2/5}\) and use exponential coordinates of norm at most \(b\) on each independent link. Outside this product chart some free link differs from identity by at least \(c_N b\); the filling gives total action at least \(c_N b^2 l^{-16}\). Its integral is at most \(\exp(-c_N\beta^{1/5}l^{-16})\). Inside the chart the action differs from \(\frac12\sum_p |(D_j x)_p|^2\) by at most \(C_N l^4 b^3\), by the plaquette Taylor expansion in the fixed color basis of §18. The logarithm of the Haar density differs from \(r_j\log J(0)\) by \(O_N(l^4 b)\). Both errors tend to zero after weighting the action error by \(v\). The full Gaussian integral including its constant density is at least \(\exp(-C_N l^4\log\beta)\) by the upper spectral bound. Its relative mass outside the product chart tends to zero by the coordinate Gaussian tail bound \(C_N l^4\exp(-c_N\beta b^2 l^{-20})\). These estimates apply uniformly to \(l\asymp\beta^{1/1000}\) and to all the indicated \(v\). They give
\[
 \log Z_j(v)=r_j\log J(0)+\tfrac{d_A r_j}{2}\log(2\pi/v)
              -\tfrac{d_A}{2}\log\det G_j+o(1).
\]

The independent interior-tree coordinates of the free box embed unchanged in the Dirichlet coordinates. Its strictly interior plaquette rows extend by zeros. All additional nonzero rows number \(O(l^3)\). Consequently \(G_D-\operatorname{diag}(G_F,I_{r_D-r_F})\) has rank \(O(l^3)\). The eigenvalues of the relative positive matrix obtained by conjugating \(G_D\) by the inverse square root of the padded matrix are between \(c l^{-20}\) and \(C l^{20}\), and all but \(O(l^3)\) of them equal one. This bounds the log-determinant difference by \(C l^3\log l\). Taking \(c_l\) to be the \(v\)-independent free Gaussian term divided by \(l^4\), (O.29) now proves (O.28).

**Consequences.** Since \(\psi_n'(\beta)=-\mathbb E S/V\) and \(\psi_n\) is convex, bound its derivative between the two secants with endpoints \(\beta(1\pm\delta)\). Equation (O.28) holds with the same \(c_l\) and error on both intervals. Multiplying the secants by \(-\beta\) and expanding \(\log(1\pm\delta)\) gives
\[
 \left|\beta\mathbb E S/V-A_0\right|
 \le C_N\left(l^{-1}+\delta+\frac{\log\beta}{l\delta}\right)
 \le C_N\sqrt{\frac{\log\beta}{l}},\qquad
 \delta=\sqrt{\frac{\log\beta}{l}}. \tag{O.30}
\]
Here \(d_A r_F/(2l^4)=A_0+O_N(l^{-1})\). This estimate is at the specified coupling and includes the periodic seam and all histories. The exponent in the choice of \(l\) is conservative.

With the local moment hypothesis of §14, the mean input in (O.18) and in the proof of (O.25) now holds on every such compatible weak sequence. The directional law of large numbers, the finite-stencil squared-Gaussian curl limit with all fixed moments, and the diagonal growing window (O.27) therefore extend to these prescribed couplings. The Gaussian white noise statement for macroscopic tests in §15 also uses the susceptibility upper estimate; its selection condition remains in force. Convex secants bound one pressure derivative. Identifying the second derivative pointwise, or controlling the normalized covariance at a fixed physical separation, needs a further estimate.

### 20. Susceptibility selection near a prescribed coupling

Fix any compatible weak sequence as in §19, with prescribed central couplings \(b\to\infty\), and retain the same torus when varying the coupling. In logarithmic coordinates \(t=\log v\) the exact finite-volume identity is
\[
 \frac{d}{dt}u_n(e^t)=u_n(e^t)-e^{2t}\chi_n(e^t).
\]
Put \(h=b^{-1/4000}\) and take a window of length \(h\) containing \(\log b\), including a one-sided window if desired. Equation (O.30) gives a uniform \(O_N(b^{-1/2000}\sqrt{\log b})=o(h)\) error on this window, using a common divisor sized at \(b\) in the proof. Thus the mean of \(e^{2t}\chi_n(e^t)\) on the window tends to \(A_0\), since the endpoint difference of \(u_n\) divided by \(h\) tends to zero. The uniform lower estimate in §8 applies as \(\inf e^t\to\infty\); it converts this signed mean into convergence in mean absolute deviation. The weak maximal inequality used in §15 now selects a point in the middle half where both the deviation and its maximal interval average (extended by zero off the window) tend to zero. In particular the selected coupling \(v\) satisfies
\[
 |v/b-1|=O(b^{-1/4000}),\qquad v^2\chi_n(v)\longrightarrow A_0 . \tag{O.31}
\]
For geometric embeddings with \(a^2=o(h)\) and the other hypotheses of §§13–15, all fixed real tilts in (O.22) remain in the window. The proof of the scalar contact white noise law therefore applies to this near-prescribed selection. The absolute change in inverse coupling can grow. Any application to a prescribed physical stopping scale still has to identify the stop for the selected couplings. Formula (O.31) gives no upper bound on \(b^2\chi_n(b)\) at the exact central value.

### 21. Separated higher correlations in a shrinking physical region

On every prescribed sequence covered by §19 and its local moment hypothesis, the exact bare energy has a joint mesoscopic limit for *separated* tests at all fixed orders, if the magnification scale is chosen sufficiently slowly. There exists one sequence \(R_m\to\infty\), with \(a_mR_m\to0\) and \(R_m/n_m\to0\), such that the following holds. Set \(X_x=\beta_m(e_x-\mathbb E e_x)\). For \(k\ge2\) and compact smooth profiles \(f_i\) of mutually disjoint support in \(\mathbb R^4\), the connected cumulant of the \(k\) variables \(\sum_x f_i(x/R_m) X_x\) converges to the integral of \(\prod_i f_i(y_i)\) against
\[
 \frac{d_A}{2k}\sum_{\pi\in S_k}
 \operatorname{tr}_{\Lambda^2}\!\left[
 \prod_{j=1}^k K(y_{\pi(j)}-y_{\pi(j+1)})\right],\qquad
 K(y)=\frac{\Lambda^2(I-2\hat y\hat y^\top)}{\pi^2|y|^4},
 \quad \pi(k+1)=\pi(1). \tag{O.32}
\]
The variables use the dimension-four bare normalization; the \(R_m^4\) field factor cancels the Riemann-sum cell volume. The disjoint-support condition keeps partial diagonals out of the integral.

Indeed, for fixed lattice sites the cumulants are polynomials in the joint moments of §18. The Gaussian energy field has the same cyclic formula as (O.32) with the lattice matrix \(P\) in place of \(K\). This follows by expanding the Gaussian log generating function \(-d_A\operatorname{tr}\log(I-PJ)/2\), with the six diagonal source entries at each site all equal. The trace is finite on the chosen stencil and each of the \(k!\) ordered appearances contributes its cycle. To make this comparison simultaneous, for integer \(j\ge2\) use all orders \(2\le k\le j\) and all lattice sites in the box \([-j^2,j^2]^4\). There are finitely many cumulants. On a sufficiently late tail their maximum absolute approximation error is at most \(j^{-5j}\), with \(a_m j<1/j\) and \(j^2<n_m/4\). Choose increasing tail indices and take \(R_m=j\) between consecutive indices. For fixed profiles the sum of the errors is \(O_{f,k}(R_m^{4k}R_m^{-5R_m})\). On their product support every separation is of order \(R_m\) in lattice units, uniformly bounded below by the disjoint-support distance times \(R_m\). The Fourier estimate in §18 gives \(P(r)=K(r)+O(|r|^{-5})\), so the remaining Gaussian cumulant sum converges by ordinary Riemann sums to (O.32).

In particular the scalar three-point kernel here vanishes. The matrix of a single exterior-square reflection anticommutes with the Hodge star; the trace of any odd product is zero. At the four collinear sites \(0,e,2e,3e\), \(|e|=1\), the four reflection factors in every cycle multiply to identity. The pointwise value of (O.32) is \(6d_A\pi^{-16}(1/81+1/256+1/20736)>0\), using the three undirected cycle lengths and their reverses. By continuity, small positive profiles supported near those four sites give a strictly positive connected fourth limit for the actual bare Wilson field in this window. The Gaussian curl is a linear field; its local energy is a quadratic observable with this nonzero fourth cumulant. This is at a different magnification and normalization from the coincident-contact central limit theorem in §15. The first nonzero *interacting* scalar three-point term, its separated continuum remainder and its transfer to the required phase still need proof. Source source, separated scalar cycles and interaction target

### 22. A quantitative two-point window at the prescribed coupling

There is an explicit radius in (O.27) on compatible tori. Fix \(\epsilon=10^{-6}\), take \(l=L^{\lfloor\epsilon\log_L\beta\rfloor}\mid n\), \(n\ge3l\), and assume the uniform local energy bound used in §18. Then for plaquettes with shortest relative coordinate displacement of sup norm at most \(n/3\),
\[
 \left|\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}(s_p,s_q)
        -\frac{d_A}{2}|P(p,q)|^2\right|\le C_{N,L}\beta^{-\epsilon/6}. \tag{O.33}
\]
Here the infinite-lattice entry uses the shortest lift. The rate is an absolute error. For the scalar sum over orientations the weighted error with weight \((1+|r|)^8\) therefore tends to zero uniformly for \(|r|\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\), including any bounded placement displacement. This gives the coefficient (O.26) on a specified growing interval in fine units. It gives the corresponding bare annular estimate on that interval by summing over plaquettes.

Three controls in the growing conditional boxes are needed for the rate. First, the sampled box boundary in boundary-tree gauge is within \(C l^8\beta^{-1/4}\) of identity except with probability \(C l^4e^{-c\sqrt\beta}\), by the local tail and the loop filling of §19. On this visible good event every conditional minimizer is within \(Cl^{18}\beta^{-1/4}\) of identity. This uses the identity trial links for an energy upper bound and the filling for the lower bound. The slice Hessian is \(\ge c l^{-20}I\) near the minimum, and outside the product chart of radius \(c_0 l^{-24}\) the energy exceeds its minimum by at least \(c l^{-64}\). In centered coordinates \(y=\sqrt\beta(u-u_*)\), truncate at \(|y|\le\beta^{1/32}\). The action Taylor error there is \(O(l^4\beta^{-13/32})\). The inner and outer relative tails are exponentially small even after including the dimension loss \(O_N(l^4\log\beta)\) in their exponents. The one- and two-plaquette conditional centered matrix moments through degree four match the flat box Gaussian to absolute error \(O_N(\beta^{-1/32})\). The note below gives the Hessian comparison, polynomial moment powers and Haar remainder for this particular \(l\).

Second, (O.19) summed over a randomly offset tile shows that the squared norm of the scaled conditional matrix-mean displacement per root has expectation at most \(C\beta^{-\epsilon/3}\). Use (O.30) at side \(l\) (its zero-boundary proof works for this smaller power as well), subtract the conditional centered matrix energy \(d_Ah_l/(2l^4)+O_N(\beta^{-1/32})\), and include visible-only plaquettes on the displacement side. Hölder with one \(L^2\) and three \(L^6\) factors bounds the error of deleting a mean in each fourth moment by \(C\beta^{-\epsilon/6}\). Third, the independent-box Gaussian curl projection \(P_{z,l}\) at offset \(z\) satisfies \(0\le P_{z,l}\le P\) on the infinite lattice. The expectation over offsets of the diagonal trace difference per site is exactly \(3-h_l/l^4\). Positivity and Cauchy–Schwarz bound the expected absolute difference of any one matrix entry by \(C/l\), independently of the pair separation. Wick contraction and the box-moment error now give (O.33). The two-root geometry agrees with its lift in the stated displacement range. Detailed growing-box estimates and prescribed-coupling proof, §§10–11

### 23. Further marked integrations on the constrained histories

For the restricted insertion of §16 the first regular readout also treats a distinguished bond eliminated by a blocking constraint, provided its full two-block determining region stays in the admitted small chart and misses the switch. Its value \(y\) solves the local group-average equation \(\mathcal F_c(y,x)=0\). In the flat gauge \(D_y\mathcal F_c=L^{-3}I\): the reference path and its final copy cancel in the derivative, and \(L\) of the \(L^4\) translated crossings use this bond. The two-block paths use no other distinguished crossing. The local implicit solution therefore gives the required source difference with the full determining stencil counted, including at an integration boundary satisfying the stated collar test. At the next ordinary integration the conditional logarithm of the sourced density is the old boundary logarithm at the new background plus \(\log\Pi\exp(\Delta L_z)\), where \(\Pi\) is that step's source-zero law and the difference uses its actual reconstructed old background. The mixed derivative includes the fresh conditional covariance of the first differences. B119's old-boundary localization controls one such generation on its smaller domain. Iteration requires a uniform estimate for the shrinking margins and all fresh terms.

There is also a retained history class at arbitrarily late eligible erasure. On an ordinary bond with the full switch \(t_b=1\), the B119 background relation \(e^{iH_b}=U_1(b)U_{\rm loc}(b)^{-1}\) gives the exact marked link \(e^{ig_0A'_b}U_{\rm loc}(b)\). Suppose all four bonds of a plaquette have this prescription in the retained shell, and its entire localized determining region and incident collar belong to one historical component through its first eligible erasure with \(h=k-N\ge2\). On this history the required \(A'_0,V_0,V_1\) stay in the old real integration. LFR I's annulus integrates the layers beginning at \(h\), and LFR II retains the historical operator and its constrained real measure at the receiving step. Thus the closed Wilson trace is a bounded real test there. Charging each original plaquette once gives a source factor bounded by \(\exp(2NC_L\|z\|_\infty |Z_1|_1)\) before applying the component estimate (O.23), with the actual unnormalized mass. This extends the strictly raw insertions to a class of reconstructed shell marks. The single-component and full-determining-region conditions must be verified on the history. All-history transport, including the new boundary terms generated over unbounded depth, remains open. Constrained inverse, next integration and full-switch receiving operation, (C.9)–(C.15)

### 24. The temporal spectrum seen by a single Wilson energy insertion

The two-point window also determines a positive spectral measure of the finite-volume Wilson theory at intermediate energies. Use the closed-cube scalar \(H\) of §13 at one fixed spatial anchor. Its placement averages are invariant under reflection through the middle of its time slab. In lattice units let \(C(t,z)=\operatorname{Cov}(H_{(0,0)},H_{(t,z)})\), with spatial displacement \(z\). On an even compatible torus, for \(2\le t\le n/2\),
\[
 C(t,0)=\int_{[0,\infty)}
     [e^{-(t-1)E}+e^{-(n-t-1)E}]\,d\nu_{n,\beta}(E),
 \qquad |C(t,z)|\le C(t,0). \tag{O.34}
\]
Here \(E\) is a difference of transfer energies. The finite torus is in a trace state and the sum includes transitions from excited states.

For details, the Wilson transfer matrix \(T\) on the spatial gauge-invariant Hilbert space is positive. Multiplication by the real centered cube inside a single transfer slab gives a self-adjoint insertion kernel \(J\) by slab reflection. The joint covariance with disjoint slab interiors is the trace of \(J T^{t-1} J T^{n-t-1}\) divided by \(Z=\operatorname{Tr}(T^n)\). In a positive-eigenvalue basis, group each unordered pair with \(\lambda_i>\lambda_j>0\) at \(E=\log(\lambda_i/\lambda_j)\), giving it weight \(|J_{ij}|^2\lambda_i^{n-2}/Z\). Equal-eigenvalue ordered pairs including diagonals give half that ordered weight to \(E=0\). Zero eigenvalues drop out when both time powers are positive. Positivity bounds the total mass by \(C(1,0)\) using the adjacent-slab trace; any zero-mode endpoint terms at \(t=1\) are nonnegative. The local energy moments give \(\beta^2\nu([0,\infty))\le C_N\). Translating the second \(J\) in space preserves its weighted Hilbert–Schmidt norm since translations commute with \(T\). Cauchy–Schwarz for the two weighted slab kernels proves the inequality in (O.34). [Lüscher, positive Wilson transfer matrix](https://bib-pubdb1.desy.de/record/396349/files/7611148.pdf)

With \(k_0=3d_A/\pi^4\), on the prescribed sequences of §22, take any \(R\to\infty\) with \(R=o(\beta^{\epsilon/100})\). Then for each \(s>0\),
\[
  \beta^2 R^8\nu_{n,\beta}([0,s/R])
        \longrightarrow k_0\,s^8/8!. \tag{O.35}
\]
Indeed (O.33), the finite cube-placement averages and (O.26) give \(\beta^2 R^8 C(\lfloor tR\rfloor,0)\to k_0 t^{-8}\) for each fixed \(t>0\). Bounded changes in the placement have relative error \(O_t(1/R)\) in the leading continuum kernel. Write \(\mu_R\) for \(\beta^2 R^8\) times the image of \(\nu\) under \(E\mapsto RE\). Its mass on compact intervals is bounded by (O.34) at \(tR\). The backward exponential makes a vanishing contribution: on \(RE\le1/M\) the mass is at most \(e\beta^2R^8 C(\lfloor MR\rfloor,0)\), with limsup at most \(e k_0 M^{-8}\); on its complement bound the backward integrand by the forward integrand at \(\lfloor R\rfloor\) times \(\exp[-(n/R-O_t(1))/M]\). First fix \(M\), then let it grow. Monotonicity of the forward exponential and the estimates at \((t\pm\eta)R\) remove integer rounding. The resulting Laplace transforms of \(\mu_R\) converge to \(k_0 t^{-8}\). Tails in each transform are uniformly controlled by the transform at \(t/2\). Every vague limit therefore has this transform; uniqueness follows, for example, by exponentially weighting the measures and approximating continuous functions of \(e^{-E}\) by polynomials on \([0,1]\). Its density is \(k_0 E^7/7!\). There is no atom at either endpoint of \([0,s]\), proving (O.35).

There is a direct criterion for extending the bare scalar bound to fixed physical separations. For dimensionless times \(2\le t\le t_{\max}\le n/2\), an estimate \(\beta^2 C(t,0)\le C t^{-8}\) implies \(\beta^2\nu([0,u])\le C'(u+t_{\max}^{-1})^8\) for \(0\le u\le1\), by testing (O.34) at a time of order \(\min(t_{\max},u^{-1})\) and using the total mass for the shortest times. Conversely this cumulative bound and the total mass bound imply the temporal estimate by Laplace integration in (O.34); the backward exponential is bounded by the forward one. For the cube scalar the spatial inequality in (O.34) and hypercubic permutations then give the dimension-eight upper bound in all directions in that distance range, using a coordinate of maximal magnitude. Fixed-order constants and the finitely many adjacent placements can be absorbed by the local moment bound.

In this criterion one may take \(t_{\max}\asymp d/a\) for a fixed physical distance \(d\) on growing tori. The current proof controls the spectral weight down to inverse powers of \(\beta\); it does not extend to energies of order \(a/d\). This is a specific low-energy matrix-element estimate whose proof would yield the desired separated *bare cube* upper bound. Identifying the renormalized tensor, its contact prescription, interacting coefficients and the phase would still require the corresponding estimates on that same sequence.

### 25. An exact boundary-score test for both routes

For the scalar cube on the even periodic Wilson lattice, the unknown on-axis covariance in §24 is exactly a conditional-mean variance on two physical time slices. Take the site reflection \(t\mapsto-t\) and let \(\mathcal B\) reveal the spatial links in the planes \(t=0,n/2\), with no other links revealed. Let \(H_j\) be the cube energy whose time slab runs from \(j\) to \(j+1\), at a fixed spatial anchor in the positive half. Take \(1\le j\) and \(2j+1\le n/2\). Define \(M_j=\mathbb E[H_j\mid\mathcal B]-\mathbb EH_j\). If \(\mu_{\partial,z}\) is the marginal on these two slices after a real tilt \(e^{zH_j}\) of the full normalized law, then
\[
 \left.\partial_z\log\frac{d\mu_{\partial,z}}{d\mu_{\partial,0}}\right|_{z=0}=M_j,
 \qquad \|M_j\|_2^2=C(2j+1,0)
 =\sup_{B\in L^2(\mathcal B),\ \|B\|_2\le1}
          |\operatorname{Cov}(H_j,B)|^2. \tag{O.36}
\]
To see this, condition on the two planes in the original link variables. Every remaining plaquette lies in one of the two closed halves, and the spatial plaquettes on the cuts are fixed. The product Haar law therefore splits into two independent conditional Wilson measures. Time reflection fixes each cut link and interchanges those two measures. The cube energy is invariant under its slab reversal, including the temporal face orientations. The reflected insertions consequently have the same conditional mean and zero conditional covariance. Their total covariance is the variance of that mean. Differentiating the explicit marginal likelihood ratio \(\mathbb E[e^{zH_j}\mid\mathcal B]/\mathbb E e^{zH_j}\) gives the score, and Hilbert-space duality gives the supremum. No background choice, source renormalization, phase selection or transfer-matrix eigenbasis enters this identity.

In particular, on the prescribed sequences of §22, for every \(1\ll j\ll\beta^{\epsilon/100}\) the actual boundary response has
\[
 \beta (2j+1)^4\|M_j\|_2\longrightarrow \sqrt{3d_A}/\pi^2 . \tag{O.37}
\]
The first unproved range begins beyond the growing microscopic interval of §22. To extend the bare scalar estimate it suffices to prove \(\beta\|M_j\|_2\le C(1+j)^{-4}\) up to distances of order \(d/a\) for each fixed physical \(d\) (with the corresponding logarithms if required). This already tests all square-integrable observables on both slice boundaries simultaneously. Odd separations suffice: the kernel in (O.34) is decreasing for \(1\le t\le n/2\) by pairing its forward and backward exponentials. Cauchy–Schwarz and axis permutations in §24 recover the off-axis bound. The converse for these odd times follows directly from (O.36).

The same target admits a sequence of positive conditional integrations. Keep the far plane fixed at \(B=n/2\) and successively move the near plane \(q\) from the source toward zero. Reveal the closed complement of the open temporal slab between \(q\) and \(B\), leaving only the spatial links fixed on its bounding planes. These exterior sigma fields decrease as the slab expands. By the same finite-range factorization their conditional means for the interior source equal those given just the two bounding planes. Thus each successive mean is a conditional expectation of its predecessor under the actual positive law. The norm is nonincreasing. The dimension-four estimate requires its quantitative decay with distance. This formulates one common scalar problem for the observable route (conditional means) and the source route (derivatives of normalized boundary marginals), with the same original link measure, observable placement and normalization. The torus marginal here is different from a blocked terminal marginal with observations retained inside the source region.

### 26. Recovering the intermediate-distance boundary score from the nearer slice

In a sufficiently slowly growing part of the window of §22, the entire leading scalar response in (O.36) can be approximated by a concrete observable of the spatial links of the near plane alone. More precisely, there exist integers \(R_m\to\infty\), \(R_m=o(\beta_m^{\epsilon/100})\), and centered real near-plane observables \(D_m\) such that
\[
 \left\|R_m^4\beta_m M_{R_m}-D_m\right\|_2\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \operatorname{Var}D_m\longrightarrow\frac{3d_A}{2^8\pi^4}.
 \tag{O.38}
\]
Consequently the additional score variance obtained by revealing the far cut after conditioning on the near spatial slice is \(o(R_m^{-8}\beta_m^{-2})\). This gives a physical observable realizing the leading boundary response on the prescribed Wilson laws. The fine distance grows and its physical length still tends to zero.

**The boundary observables on the lattice.** Let \(b_i\) denote the three tangential plaquette orientations \((23),(31),(12)\) in the near plane, with orientation reversal for \((31)\) if using increasing pairs. At each anchor form the traceless anti-Hermitian part of its plaquette holonomy times \(\sqrt\beta\), parallel transported to one spatial root along links of a tree in the plane. Denote the resulting color vector by \(B_i(z)\). For a real compact smooth spatial test vector \(h\) use the single-plane flux
\[
 Y_R(h)=R^{-1}\sum_{z\in\mathbb Z^3}\sum_i h_i(z/R)B_i(z).
\]
Squared color norms, and inner products at the common root, are gauge invariant. The tree for any finite set of tests need only span their anchors and the root. We will use half the sum of squares of six such fluxes and center it in the true law.

At **fixed** \(R\), paths and tests, their invariant joint moments with the scaled bulk cube energy agree in the cutoff limit with those of the corresponding linear lattice Gaussian curls and the cube sum of their half squares. To justify this extension of §18, include the finite path tree and all faces in a fixed stencil before choosing the auxiliary box. On a good interior placement the box forest slice makes the path links tend to identity in probability when the boundary tends to flatness at fixed box size. The plaquette matrices times \(\sqrt\beta\) after subtracting identity have bounded moments of each fixed order by (O.19) and the local energy bound. Thus parallel transport along these paths contributes an error tending to zero after taking invariant products. The conditional Gaussian limit and the random-offset mean-displacement estimate in §18 then apply. Common root conjugation does not alter the products. Bad interior placements disappear in the ordered limits of that section; uniform integrability follows by the same local moment bounds, since all transports are unitary. The comparison first treats each finite tree, with no uniform path-length estimate.

**Equal-time Maxwell calculation.** In the infinite lattice Gaussian comparison the three magnetic components on the plane have a matrix Fourier covariance with midpoint phases. Its continuum limit on the above flux tests has symbol
\[
 C_B(p)=\tfrac{|p|}{2}\big(I-\hat p\hat p^\top\big),\qquad p\in\mathbb R^3,
 \tag{O.39}
\]
with measure \(d^3p/(2\pi)^3\) per independent color. In fact, writing \(s_i=2\sin(k_i/2)\) and \(\omega^2=\sum_{i=1}^3s_i^2\), the magnetic block of the lattice curl projection is \((\omega^2 I-ss^\top)/(4\sin^2(k_0/2)+\omega^2)\) up to the anchored phases. Integration in \(k_0\) gives denominator \(\omega\sqrt{4+\omega^2}\) in place of the original denominator (by the elementary integral for \((A-2\cos k_0)^{-1}\)). The symbol is bounded by \(C|k|\) on the centered Brillouin zone. The Fourier transform of a smooth compact test sampled at \(z/R\) is \(R^3\) times the periodized transform at momentum \(Rk\). The bound just obtained and rapid decay give (O.39) by dominated convergence, also for products of different tests.

At time one in these rescaled coordinates the continuum cross covariance between a bulk magnetic component and the boundary test is given by the corresponding row of \(e^{-|p|} C_B(p)\). For the electric block the rows are those of \(e^{-|p|} U(p) C_B(p)\), where \(U(p)\) is the imaginary cross-product matrix \(\pm i(\hat p\times)\), with sign fixed by the time Fourier convention and orientations. These statements follow by integrating the curl projection against \(e^{ik_0}\) in continuous time: the magnetic numerator is \(|p|^2I-pp^\top\), and the electric-magnetic numerator has one \(k_0\) and one spatial momentum. Equivalently these are the spatial Fourier transforms of \(K_{A,b_i}(1,-y)\) in (O.32), which fixes every sign in choosing the real test functions. On the lattice, \(R^2\) times the cross covariance of a bulk linear curl at time \(R+O(1)\) with \(Y_R(h)\) tends to that same pairing: sum the kernel expansion \(P=K+O(R^{-5})\) over the compact test on the time-zero slice. All bounded cube-placement shifts have the same limit.

Choose the six linear flux tests representing these six cross-covariance rows in the boundary covariance inner product (O.39). In Fourier variables their multipliers are the rows of \(e^{-|p|}I\) and \(e^{-|p|}U(p)\), using the adjoint convention for test functions. This is an explicit prescription independent of any nonlinear Yang–Mills input. Both multipliers respect the reality condition. They can be approximated in the boundary covariance norm by smooth compact real-space tests: first cut off smoothly near zero and at large frequency, and then truncate the inverse Schwartz transform in real space, with convergence in \(H^1\). The cross-covariance pairings are continuous in this norm, by the stated representation.

The covariance of the six resulting fluxes per color is \(cI_6\), with \(c=1/(16\pi^2)\). For the magnetic block the angular average of \(I-\hat p\hat p^\top\) is \(2I/3\); the radial integral is \(\int_0^\infty u^3e^{-2u}du=3/8\). On the transverse plane \(U\) is unitary, so the electric block has the same covariance, and their cross block vanishes by oddness. By construction the cross-covariance matrix of the rescaled bulk curls with these six fluxes is also \(cI_6\). Thus Gaussian contraction gives both the variance of the centered half sum of the flux squares and its covariance with \(R^4\) times the centered bulk cube energy in the limit as \(3d_A c^2\). The bulk variance itself is never used in this calculation; only the separated cross contractions occur.

Take successive compact test approximations, then sufficiently large integer distances for the lattice Gaussian comparisons, and finally sufficiently late tails of the actual Wilson sequence for those finitely many test moments at each fixed distance. The tail indices can enforce \(R_m=o(\beta_m^{\epsilon/100})\) and all the tree stencils fitting inside the torus with a vanishing physical radius. This diagonal choice gives \(\operatorname{Var}D_m\to3d_Ac^2\) and \(R_m^4\beta_m\operatorname{Cov}(H_{R_m},D_m)\to3d_Ac^2\). By (O.36)–(O.37) the squared norm of \(R_m^4\beta_m M_{R_m}\) has that very limit. Since \(D_m\) is measurable on the near cut, its inner product with the scaled score is precisely the scaled bulk covariance. Expanding the squared difference proves (O.38). Projection onto the near cut can only improve the approximation. The construction gives no uniform path or nonlinear transport estimate out to \(R\) of order \(1/a\); that is required to use a one-plane readout at a fixed physical distance.

**Uniform range for the near-plane result.** In fact the variance and projection assertions (O.38) apply along *any* sequence \(R\to\infty\) with \(R\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\) and the §22 geometry. Here is the additional uniform estimate needed when the path tree and its flux tests grow with the cutoff. Fix a finite set of compact smooth profiles first. Choose shortest coordinate paths in a containing spatial cube of side \(C_h R\), using one rooted coordinate tree there. Include the bulk cube in the surrounding four-dimensional bounding box. Under random tiling, a single good \(l\)-box contains all these stencils except on offsets of probability \(O_h(R/l)\) and on bad boundaries of the exponentially small probability given in §22. On a good box the tree-gauge group links are at distance \(O(l^{18}\beta^{-1/4}+\beta^{-15/32})\) of identity on the thermal truncation \(|y|\le\beta^{1/32}\) of §22. Thus moving any one color vector along its path costs at most \(C_h R(l^{18}\beta^{-1/4}+\beta^{-15/32})\) times its norm. The original tree root contributes only a common conjugation inside the squared norms.

In each untransported second or fourth product on the good placements, delete the conditional mean displacements at cost \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon/6})\) per root using the averaged estimate in §22. The product estimate applies equally to four different roots: Hölder uses at most three uniform sixth moments, and conditional Gaussian Taylor comparison in §22 uses only four single-plaquette linear expansions with bounded coefficient norms. Compare the box Gaussian moments to the infinite ones by (38) of the heat-bath note applied to each entry in the Wick contractions. Bad offset contributions per product are \(O_h(R/l)\), by independence of offsets and uniform norm moments for each factor. On bad law events use higher local moments and their exponentially small probabilities. The total absolute coefficient weight of the quartic products in the variance of the flux squares is \(O_h(R^8)\), since one flux costs \(O_h(R^2)\). It is also \(O_h(R^8)\) for the bulk-square covariance multiplied by \(R^4\). Thus every error, including \(R^8\beta^{-\epsilon/6}\) and \(R^9/l\), tends to zero on the stated interval. The second-moment and cross-covariance limits for fixed profiles follow before sending their approximation error in (O.39) to zero. Choosing increasingly accurate profiles sufficiently slowly proves the \(L^2\) approximation for any such sequence. The profile supports may likewise be kept physically shrinking along the weak trajectory.

There is also a distributional conclusion. For a fixed finite family of the smooth profiles, the joint invariant squares of the boundary fluxes have the law of their Gaussian counterparts in the limit of this interval. One way to see this without summing uncontrolled high-moment errors is to compare the fluxes themselves in the good box chart. Deleting the scaled matrix means costs at most \(C_h R^2\beta^{-\epsilon/6}\) in averaged \(L^2\) on good placements, by the triangle inequality. Path transport errors tend to zero in probability by the preceding bound with coefficient cost \(O_h(R^2)\). At fixed good exterior the entire law of \(y\) is within vanishing total variation of the Gaussian of covariance \(G_*^{-1}\), by the relative density and tail estimates in §10.1 of the heat-bath note. On this comparison the centered linearization error of each matrix is \(O(\beta^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{poly}(l))\) in \(L^2\), using the quadratic Taylor remainder and the Gaussian fourth-moment bound for \(|y|\); the truncation and density error in its mean is bounded by \(C\beta^{-1/3}l^{24}\). The identity-centered derivative differs from the flat curl by \(O(l^{18}\beta^{-1/4})\). One can couple the two Gaussian coordinate vectors by applying \(G_*^{-1/2}\) and \(G^{-1/2}\) to the same standard normal. Their operator difference is at most \(C l^{30}\|G_*-G\|\), by the integral formula for the inverse square root and the lower spectral bounds in §22. All these errors still vanish after the flux coefficient cost \(O_h(R^2)\). Finally realize the independent-box and infinite-lattice Gaussian curls as \(P_{z,l}W\) and \(PW\) on a common plaquette white noise \(W\). Their squared discrepancy per root averaged over offsets is \(O(1/l)\), because these are nested projections and the diagonal deficit is controlled in §22. The resulting flux discrepancy is \(O_h(R^2/\sqrt l)\) in \(L^2\). This proves the asserted comparison in law for the invariant flux squares. The Gaussian fluxes themselves converge by (O.39). Centering constants converge by the moment comparison above.

For the six limiting optimal profiles this yields explicitly
\[
 R^4\beta\big(\mathbb E[H_R\mid\mathcal B]-\mathbb E H_R\big)
       \ \Longrightarrow\ \frac{\chi^2_{6d_A}-6d_A}{32\pi^2}.
 \tag{O.40}
\]
Indeed at fixed test approximation the centered half sum has the corresponding Gaussian quadratic law; sending the approximation error to zero gives the displayed independent-component chi-square law. The \(L^2\) score comparison controls its characteristic functions uniformly in this passage for each fixed frequency. Its second moment converges by (O.37). The same distributional result holds for the score conditional on only the near slice, by projection contraction and (O.38). This score concerns a single cube conditioned across a distance of \(R\) lattice spacings.

### 27. A positive receiving operation for an integrated boundary source

There is a further source-transport result when an entire first-step integration island is captured. Let a connected regular region \(D\) be separated from other regular unknowns by enough fixed bonds to split every Wilson plaquette, axial block, averaging constraint and active selector stencil. Suppose that the required original and coarse data on \(D\) and its determining collars remain real in the next protected region, and all are held by the same historical component until the eligible erasure of §23. This allows partial switches as well as genuine integration of links of a marked plaquette. The constrained first minimum splits on these conditions because the action, the averaging constraints and the gauge choices have separate variables on the separated regions. Its unique local representative and the local comparison minimum in \(D\) depend only on those determining data. Hence also the partial-switch link \(e^{ig_0A'_b}e^{i(1-t_b)H_b}U_{\rm loc}(b)\) depends on real old data on this branch.

Write \(I_D\) for the unnormalized real conditional integral on the integrated part of \(D\). For the first-step boundary marks of §16 put \(d_D=I_D(1)\) and \(M_D(z)=I_D(\exp\sum_p z_p s_p)/d_D\) on positive-mass fibers, and \(M_D(z)=1\) otherwise. Then \(|M_D(z)|\le\exp(2N\sum_p|z_p|)\) and the original density carries \(d_D M_D(z)\) on every fiber. The first source derivatives are the conditional insertion means; the second derivative of the density is the conditional product moment and that of its log is the conditional covariance. Subsequent regular integrations outside the determining region carry the whole real function \(M_D\) into its component operation. This supplies a receiving bound also for boundary sources whose original links have actually been integrated. The proof uses the full boundary-source sum with its mass. Counting anchors touching the original historical collar gives the source reserve in §23, including mixed insertions owned by that component. The separated-island, collar and ownership hypotheses specify the history class; weighting the complementary histories with penetrating coarse backgrounds is still required. Positive conditional kernel and receiving bounds, (C.16)–(C.18)

For a crossing whose connected regular region reaches beyond the captured data, B119 supplies a local comparison with the full first minimum under a buffer condition. Take a protected native cube about the source with its enlarged collar inside the admitted first regular region. B119 after (1.19) gives \(\|U_1 U_{1,\square'}^{-1}-I\|\le C\epsilon_1 e^{-cLM_2 R_1}\) on the smaller collar in a common axial gauge. The physical current in B109 (1.8) uses a finite stencil there. Replacing the full minimum and this physical current by their local values in short source carriers costs the same exponential up to the fixed Cauchy margin; carriers running out of that collar pay the rooted tree decay of §16. For a single crossing the resulting old-variable function \(b_\circ\) satisfies
\[
 |I_1(z)-I_1(0)e^{b_\circ(z)}|\le C I_1(0)|z|e^{-c_1 R_1}
 \tag{O.41}
\]
on the real determining fields in the indicated small source disk. Keeping the vacuum factor \(I_1(0)\) at the actual exterior is essential. Positive subsequent integrations preserve this mass-relative error on the protected histories. For an independently varied complex current, an estimate on its physical relation to the links does not give a transverse derivative bound. At one fresh ordinary step the old source anchor and the new integration or switch cell are separated by the protection layers. Counting their common carrier in current \(M\)-cube units pays \(e^{-c_2R_2}\) while retaining a smaller tree decay. The copy of the old coefficient persists at its original norm. Actual seam comparison and first new carrier, (C.19)–(C.21)

### 28. The first unresolved scale change for the scalar boundary score

The matched observable from §26 gives a benchmark at the starting scale of a proposed iteration. Here is the difficulty in passing to a larger slab by positivity alone. Fix a bulk cube and the remote cut, move the near cut outward as in §25, and write \(\mathcal F_j\) for the nested exterior fields. For any chosen boundary approximation \(P_j\) to the conditional mean \(M_j\), set \(e_j=M_j-P_j\). The contribution of this error at the next distance \(Lr_j\) is the true conditional projection \(\mathbb E[e_j\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]\). Jensen's inequality alone bounds its norm on the dimension-four scale by \(L^4\) times the preceding normalized error. Orthogonality to finitely many boundary predictors cannot remove that factor in general, even on the actual finite-volume Wilson probability space. Indeed the further retained exterior contains a plaquette with a non-atomic trace law under the strictly positive original density. Its bounded functions span an infinite-dimensional space in both exterior sigma fields. Choose a nonconstant function orthogonal to the constants and the two finite predictor spans. Its conditional projection is itself. This tests an operator-norm shortcut; it makes no claim that the specific Wilson insertion error equals this exterior function.

Thus a scale iteration must estimate the projection of the *particular* transported source or its transfer spectral weight at lower energies. The fresh connected contributions in §27 carry an exponential geometric gain on the stated histories. The copied conditional means have no such gain from that estimate. This identifies the point at which combining the source calculation with positive history transport still requires a new bound. Normalized slab reductions and near-plane comparison, §12

### 29. The time-slice test for all 21 bare tensor components

Use the actual site-centered trace-completed clover \(\widetilde q\) of Annex E, and a symmetric six-by-six coefficient matrix \(H\). Put \(A_H(x)=a^4 H:\widetilde q(x)\), so \(A_{I/2}=a^4 w_a\). Set \(S=\Lambda^2\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)\) for time reflection. The signed clover definition and the four-vertex Wilson trace give exactly \(\theta A_H(x)=A_{SHS}(\theta x)\). With the two cut planes and their sigma field \(\mathcal B\) from §25 define
\[
 m_j(H)=\mathbb E[\beta A_H(j,0)\mid\mathcal B]-\mathbb E[\beta A_H(j,0)] .
\]
For a vertex with its stencil strictly on one side of the planes and its reflected partner, the exact covariance identity is
\[
 \langle m_j(H),m_j(K)\rangle
    =\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}\big(A_H(j,0),A_{SKS}(-j,0)\big).
 \tag{O.42}
\]
Time reversal of the tensor labels in this formula includes the pseudoscalar sign. The real conditional-independence proof is the same as (O.36). It applies also to two different anchors on opposite sides of the plane, where their centered means have inner product in place of a squared norm.

On the prescribed sequences of §22, for any \(R\to\infty\), \(R\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\), the joint law in the 21-dimensional space of coefficients and its covariance obey
\[
 \begin{split}
 R^4 m_R(H)&\ \Longrightarrow\ c\sum_{b=1}^{d_A}
       [Z_b^\top H Z_b-\operatorname{tr}_6 H],\qquad
       c=1/(16\pi^2),\quad Z_b\sim N(0,I_6)\text{ independently},\\
 R^8\langle m_R(H),m_R(K)\rangle&\ \longrightarrow        2d_Ac^2\operatorname{tr}_6(HK).
 \end{split} \tag{O.43}
\]
Equivalently the limiting random symmetric matrix is a centered Wishart matrix times \(c\). Its covariance form has rank 21 for each \(N\ge2\), even when a realization of the uncentered matrix has smaller rank. This does not alter the rank-20 result in §11, which concerns a spatially summed quadratic form at zero momentum. Here the marks are at separated reflected points. Taking \(H=I/2\) gives the normalization and law of the scalar result at the vertex placement of the actual field family.

To check (O.43), in the Gaussian comparison the traceless part of \(H\) multiplies the based clover Gram matrix and the trace part multiplies the four-vertex Wilson energy by \(\operatorname{tr}H/3\). Each clover is a signed average of rooted plaquettes for its orientation. On the separated cross contractions all bounded displacements have the same leading kernel and each averaging weight has total one after orientation. The trace part consequently restores \((\operatorname{tr}H/6)I\) in the quadratic coefficient, giving \(G^\top H G\) per color. At temporal separation \(2R\) the rescaled cross kernel is \(cS\). Wick's two cross contractions with the reflected coefficient \(SKS\) give the second line of (O.43). The uniform four-matrix error of §26 applies to these fixed stencils, after multiplication by \(R^8\). All plaquette products in each clover are based at its vertex, and the projection onto traceless matrices leaves the leading Lie algebra curl intact.

For the law take the six near-plane fluxes of §26 and form the centered invariant polynomial \(D_R(H)=\sum_{A,B}H_{AB}\langle Y_R(h_A),Y_R(h_B)\rangle-\mathbb E[\cdot]\), with the expectation taken of the preceding uncentered sum. For limiting optimal profiles its variance and its covariance with \(R^4\beta A_H\) both tend to \(2d_Ac^2\operatorname{tr}(H^2)\). This follows from the two covariance matrices \(cI_6\) in §26 and the same two Wick contractions. The estimate there for fixed finite profiles controls the Wilson errors. By (O.42), projection onto the two slices and onto the near slice alone are both approximated on this scale in \(L^2\) by this near-slice observable. Take the test approximations sufficiently slowly for the 21 members of one basis simultaneously. The Gaussian flux comparison of §26 and linearity give the first line of (O.43) jointly in all 21 components.

For the **full bare separated two-point upper bound** on these lattice representatives there is now a finite list of positive tests at a fixed physical separation. For a real basis \(H_i\) bound \(\|m_j(H_i)\|_2\) by \(C(1+j)^{-4}\), with allowed logarithms, through \(j\) of order \(d/a\). For a displacement with a largest coordinate of size \(t\), use that coordinate as time and put a site cut between the two anchors. Choose their distances \(\lfloor t/2\rfloor,\lceil t/2\rceil\). Once their stencils miss the cut, conditional independence and Cauchy–Schwarz bound their covariance by the product of the two boundary-score norms, with the reflection acting on the second label. Spatial translation and the finite hypercubic action only change its basis coefficients by fixed constants. This works for even and odd \(t\); bounded stencil separations are already controlled by local moments. Conversely the reflected diagonal entries give (O.42) and hence those same tests. This is an equivalence for the specified bare two-point *upper bound*. The new window proves the limiting Gram matrix there, but leaves the physical-distance bounds, running field mixing, coincident contacts, higher hierarchy and interacting phase identification outstanding.

The same contraction gives the directional leading kernel on this interval. For increasing lattice separation \(r\), \(|r|\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\), set \(S_{\hat r}=\Lambda^2(I-2\hat r\hat r^\top)\). Then
\[
 |r|^8\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}(A_H(0),A_K(r))
 -\frac{2d_A}{\pi^4}\operatorname{tr}_6(H S_{\hat r} K S_{\hat r})
 \longrightarrow 0 .
\]
The error is uniform in direction by the kernel estimate of §18 and the order-four comparison used for (O.43). This gives the covariant angular dependence for the stated bare two-point functions in this shrinking physical interval.

### 30. Joining the two halves with their original masses

There is a form of the time-slice test that uses unnormalized source integrations directly. Split every cut-plane plaquette weight equally between the two halves. Let \(u,v\) be all spatial link variables on the two planes, let \(K(u,v)\) be the real half-torus integral, and let \(J_F(u,v)\) be the same integral with a real insertion \(F\) on the interior of that half. With normalized product Haar integration \(du\,dv\), the full partition function is \(Z=\int K^2 du\,dv\) by reflection and the boundary law is \(K^2du\,dv/Z\). Therefore
\[
 \big\|\mathbb E[F\mid\mathcal B]-\mathbb EF\big\|_2^2
      =\frac1Z\int |J_F(u,v)-(\mathbb EF)K(u,v)|^2\,du\,dv .
 \tag{O.44}
\]
All three kernels here use the original action and the same cut-plane convention. The two factors of the boundary density cancel the denominator in the squared conditional mean exactly. In particular the target can be expressed as an estimate on unnormalized physical half-integrals even at boundary configurations of small probability. In a positive decomposition of one half into histories the centered kernel is the sum of \(J_{F,h}-(\mathbb EF)K_h\). The single full-law centering must be retained when the historical mean varies. The identity does not supply a component estimate for the half-space RG with fixed real cuts.

**One class controlled at physical separations.** If \(E_r\) requires a connected path of microscopic bad cubes from the mark to a cut at distance \(r\) fine spacings, the actual Wilson bad-bridge estimate of Annex A and §4.4 gives \(\mathbb P(E_r)\le C e^{-c r}\) under its thresholds and on its compatible sequence. For the scaled site mark \(F=\beta A_H\), the local Wilson moment bound gives uniform \(L^4\) norm. Conditional contraction and Hölder yield
\[
 \left\|\mathbb E[(F-\mathbb EF)\mathbf1_{E_r}\mid\mathcal B]\right\|_2
 \le \|F-\mathbb EF\|_4\,\mathbb P(E_r)^{1/4}\le C_H e^{-c r/4}.
\]
For a half-space path use a root near the bounded stencil and stop it at its first visit to the cut; the same anchored full-law path count bounds that event. Thus even after multiplication by \(r^4\) this term vanishes at a fixed physical separation when \(r\asymp a^{-1}\). This removes the actual microscopic bridging sector from all 21 tests in (O.42). A coarse history can carry a conditional mean to the other side without containing such a microscopic bad path.

For sources entering solely as the first boundary family there is also an arbitrary-depth estimate on consecutive ordinary steps before the first erasure. Select crossing marks by a local birth rule, perturb only the boundary interactions on a fixed small source polydisk, and retain all unmarked kernels, couplings, and selectors. The ordinary-step input theorem of B119 pp.245, 262 and the smallness of the old-boundary variations at p.277 allow the same boundary allowance and fixed source disk at each step on the stated hypotheses. The homogeneous extraction at zero boundary interpolation parameter sees the original interior input. Fresh activities inherit their source anchors and the post-integration tree margin; the old copy is counted once at its birth. For one original root the resulting derivative sum is bounded by \((C/r_z)(1+\sum_{j\ge2}e^{-c R_j})\), where \(r_z\) is the source radius and \(R_j\) are the protection widths. On a weak corridor \(g_j^{-2}\ge G+c(K-j)\) this sum is bounded, since \(R_j\ge(\log g_j^{-2})^s\) with \(s>1\). This treats ordinary evolution before any intervening erasure and makes no claim of a uniform marked norm after repeated erasures. In particular the preliminary integration of an old retained real fluctuation changes an argument held fixed by the ordinary step; its variation needs a separate bound. Fixed source radius through consecutive ordinary steps, (C.22)

For smeared scalar sources there is a weaker sufficient physical-frequency test with an explicitly controlled part. Let \(J_h(t)\) be the actual time covariance of a cube slab weighted by a real spatial profile \(h\) on both ends, and put \(D_h(q)=\tfrac32\widehat J_h(0)-2\widehat J_h(q)+\tfrac12\widehat J_h(2q)\). This equals \(\sum_t J_h(t)(1-\cos qt)^2\). The reflected transfer representation gives positive \(J_h(t)\) at separated times. Averaging over lattice frequencies \(|q|\lesssim R^{-1}\) with a sufficiently large fixed constant bounds the covariance tail beyond \(R/2\); monotonicity then bounds each \(J_h(t)\) for \(t\ge R\). Consequently \(D_h(q)\le M_a\|h\|_2^2 R^{-4}\) on those frequencies suffices for \(J_h(t)\le C M_a\|h\|_2^2 R^{-5}\) through half the time circumference. At \(R\asymp a^{-1}\) the two temporal smearing sums cost \(O(a^{-2})\) and spatial profiles have squared norm \(O(a^{-3})\). This gives the unscaled \(O(M_a)\) bound for physical separated test functions in this placement.

For the single-box conditional covariance in the heat-bath decomposition the temporal Fourier polynomial has degree \(O(l)\) and supremum \(O(\beta^{-2}l^4\|h\|_2^2)\). Oversampling on the torus bounds its continuous supremum by the same order. Reflection makes it even, and the fourth difference cancels its degree-zero and degree-two Taylor coefficients. The trigonometric Bernstein derivative bound yields \(|D_h^{\rm box}(q)|\le C\beta^{-2}l^8 |q|^4\|h\|_2^2\); at the side \(l\asymp\beta^\epsilon\) of §22 this coefficient tends to zero. Thus at *physical frequencies* the part still requiring an estimate is the fourth difference of the covariance of surviving conditional means. Positivity in the tail argument is needed only for the full Wilson time covariance. Smeared spectral bound and the controlled conditional term, (43)–(46)

### 31. A rate for separated smooth physical tests on the selected sequence

The physical support separation in (O.17) can be kept fixed while giving a rate for its scaled remainder. Let \(\epsilon=10^{-6}\) as in §22, assume the compatible weak geometry with \(a\) smaller than every inverse power of \(\beta\), and use a near-prescribed selection as in §20. The selection can be made so that its susceptibility deviation and the maximal logarithmic-window error of §15 are \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon})\). Indeed the growing-box lower bound gives \(v^2\chi_n(v)\ge A_0-C b^{-\epsilon}\) uniformly in the narrow window about \(b\) in §20. The signed window mean differs from \(A_0\) by \(O(b^{-1/4000}\sqrt{\log b})\) using (O.30) at box side comparable to \(b^{1/1000}\). Both tile sizes are available on the stated geometry. These two estimates bound the mean absolute deviation by \(C b^{-\epsilon}\). The weak maximal inequality at a sufficiently large constant times that threshold leaves a point in the middle half, as in §15.

Let \(F_f\) denote the sum of closed-cube energies with smooth compact weights \(f\) of §13. On that selection, for fixed smooth profiles of disjoint support,
\[
 |a^4\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}(F_f,F_g)|\le C_{f,g}\beta^{-\epsilon}.
 \tag{O.45}
\]
Use the actual box integration of side \(l\asymp\beta^\epsilon\). Its conditional covariance between the two marks vanishes once their supports are farther apart than a box and its stencil. On a single mark the expected conditional variance in the normalization \(a^2\beta\) is \(A_l\int f^2+O_f(\beta^{-1/32}+a l^3)\). To see this, freeze the spatial weights at each box anchor. For the frozen mark, the conditional variance of \(\beta A\) on a good box is \(d_Ah_l/2+O(\beta^{-1/32})\) by §22, and the probability of a bad box beats any inverse power. On a bad box the compact action is bounded by \(C l^4\), so the expectation of its discarded conditional variance is negligible. The Riemann sum of the squared frozen weights has error \(O_f(al)\). In the difference of the two within-box centered sums the plaquette weights have magnitude \(O_f(al)\). Its squared norm after normalization is at most \(C_f a^4(al)^2 (a^{-4}/l^4)l^8=C_fa^2l^6\), using conditional independence and the unconditional local second moments. Cauchy–Schwarz now gives the displayed variance comparison. The chessboard upper bound is \((A_0+O(\beta^{-\epsilon}))a^4\sum f^2\). Subtracting the within-box variance leaves an \(O_f(\beta^{-\epsilon})\) bound on the variance of the scaled conditional mean since \(A_0-A_l=O(l^{-1})\). Applying Cauchy–Schwarz to the two conditional means proves (O.45).

For the actual four-vertex scalar one has a slightly weaker quantitative bound by placement alone. At any prescribed compatible coupling, (O.33) and square summability of each row of \(P\) imply
\[
 \operatorname{Var}\left[\beta a^4\sum_{p} h_{\alpha_p}(x_p)s_p\right]
       \le C_h(a^4+\beta^{-\epsilon/6}) \tag{O.46}
\]
for smooth compact plane weights on a growing physical torus. The Gaussian pair term costs \(O_h(a^4)\) after summing over the first root, and the uniform absolute error times \(a^8\) costs \(O_h(\beta^{-\epsilon/6})\) after summing both roots. For sufficiently large side all their shortest displacements are admitted in (O.33). Consequently (O.20) bounds the centered placement difference in \(a^2\beta\) units by \(C_f(a+\beta^{-\epsilon/12})\) in \(L^2\). The cube variances in those units are uniformly bounded on the selection. For \(F_f^v=a^4\sum_x f(x)w_a(x)\) and disjoint supports this yields \(|a^4\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}(F_f^v,F_g^v)|\le C_{f,g}\beta^{-\epsilon/12}\). Both bounds concern the actual full law at fixed physical support separation. The unscaled estimates still contain \(a^{-4}\); an inverse power of \(\beta\) does not pay for that factor along the weak trajectory.

For disjoint supports the four-vertex bound improves to the exponent of (O.45). Keep the same pressure selection and choose the conditional side \(l\asymp\beta^{2\epsilon}\), rounded to a compatible power of \(L\). The estimates of §22 use polynomial factors at most \(l^{500}\) against \(\beta^{-1/8}\) for the moments through degree four, and both Gaussian tail exponents still dominate \(O(l^4\log\beta)\) at this side. The per-root squared conditional-mean displacement is now \(O(\beta^{-2\epsilon})\): its three inputs are the global mean error from (O.30) at the larger pressure side, the conditional thermal error \(O(\beta^{-1/32})\), and the trace deficit \(O(1/l)\). Thus the four-factor comparison underlying (O.33) has error \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon})\), giving that rate in (O.46) as well. On these conditional boxes the normalized four-vertex minus cube difference has variance \(O_f(\beta^{-\epsilon}+a^2)\). Its projected mean has at most that variance. The conditional mean of the normalized cube mark also has variance \(O_f(\beta^{-\epsilon})\) by the argument for (O.45) at this slightly larger side. Since the conditional covariance of two disjointly supported four-vertex marks vanishes, only these projected means contribute. Their Cauchy–Schwarz bound gives
\[
 |a^4\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}(F_f^v,F_g^v)|\le C_{f,g}\beta^{-\epsilon}.
 \tag{O.47}
\]

### 32. Early connected insertions already pay their physical scale on ordinary histories

Consider two specified microscopic anchors \(p,q\) admitted at birth under the boundary-only crossing rule of §30 on a frozen history. Follow consecutive ordinary integrations through scale \(k\) before its first erasure. Keep the exterior arguments fixed and take the mixed derivative of the **conditional logarithm**. Its contribution born at scale \(j\le k\) has an antecedent from both roots: differentiating a term whose old inputs omit either root gives zero. The resummation by unions and the fixed outer source polydisk in (C.22) therefore give
\[
 \sum_{j\le k}\sum_X
  \left|\partial_{z_p}\partial_{z_q} B^{(j)}(X;0)\right|
 \le C r_z^{-2}\sum_{j\le k}
  \exp\!\left[-c\,\frac{d_{\rm fine}(p,q)}{M L^j}\right] .
 \tag{O.48}
\]
The summands on the left are the distinct generations, with the surviving old copy counted once. For the first scale use the birth bound (C.6). At later scales two Cauchy derivatives use the same fixed source disk. A participating carrier at that scale spans both roots up to the bounded current-cube enlargement. Split its tree decay between the rooted entropy sum and their separation, retaining a positive fraction for each. This estimates the connected conditional source jet, including the fresh covariance terms; a product of marginal first derivatives can have disjoint carriers and does not obey this argument.

In particular if the supports have fixed positive physical separation, \(a=\ell_0L^{-K}\), and \(k\le K-\lceil\log_L K\rceil-C_0\), the right side is at most \(C k e^{-c' L^{C_0}K}\), with \(c'\) depending on that separation. For any specified power \(a^{-b}\) choose \(C_0\) sufficiently large to pay it and any fixed polynomial in \(\beta=O(K)\). This also pays the \(O(a^{-8})\) pairs in two bounded smooth supports. Thus the early connected part of these actual ordinary histories is controlled even at fixed physical separation. The last logarithmically many scales, histories having an earlier erasure and the covariance of conditional means under the full law still need estimates. Uniform pre-erasure source bound and retention of both anchors, §10

A rare history sector can also be propagated beyond a cut in the *normalized* full-law covariance for these tensors. The relevant event must have the asserted probability in the original positive path law of Annex N, or in a joint lift with the same marked marginals. Let \(p\) be this probability, \(X,Y\) any two centered marks \(\beta A_H\) with bounded tensor coefficients, and initialize the first cut field by the posterior numerator of \(X\mathbf1_E\), the second by the posterior of \(Y\). Then the absolute sum of all subsequent conditional covariance defects plus the absolute terminal covariance is at most
\[
 C p[1+\log(1/p)]^3. \tag{O.49}
\]
To prove it, the finite-stencil local energy bound and \(|\operatorname{tr}(C_A C_B)|\le (\|C_A\|^2+\|C_B\|^2)/2\) give both marks a uniform exponential moment. Truncate both at \(b=C_0\log(e/p)\), with large fixed \(C_0\); each discarded part has \(L^2\) norm \(O(p^2)\). For the bounded terms (N.4) gives \(C b^2 p(1+\log(1/p))\). For the other terms conditional Jensen contracts the initial \(L^2\) norms. Cauchy–Schwarz for each bracket, then for the sum of the martingale increments and the centered terminal variables together, bounds the absolute total by the product of the two initial standard deviations. This yields \(O(p^2)\) for the discarded terms, uniformly in the number of later operations. For bare \(A_H\) undo the factor \(\beta^2\); for the physical \(H:\widetilde q\) also multiply by \(a^{-8}\). This applies to an original crossing insertion even after an earlier integration of its links, because its contribution at the cut is a posterior numerator with its probability retained. The rare-event bounds in Annex N may be used on their specified ancestry classes. Their extension to general surviving exterior terms is still required.

### 33. The complete preliminary integration on a single successful component

For the boundary-only input of (C.22) a first erasure can also be bounded on a **whole-torus exterior chart**, when the history has just one terminal old component, that component takes the successful branch, \(h=k-N\ge2\), and no earlier erasure intervened. The bound retains its unnormalized mass through all \(N\) preliminary integrations. It uses a volume estimate for the marked test and consequently does not yet localize its output to a polymer.

At preliminary step \(j\), write \(q_j(v;y)\) for the actual source-zero fluctuation integrand of LFR I (1.60), with unchanged real integration coordinates \(y=(A_j,B_j)\), and \(Q_j(v)=\int q_j(v;y)dy\). Keep inactive external factors outside. On the admitted smaller mixed chart its total variation relative to \(|Q_j|\) has log bounded by \(C(1+N+|\log g_j|)(1+|Z_{j+1}|_{j+1})\) on nonempty steps. For the numerator keep all active coordinates on their real contours in the absolute Gaussian comparison. The determinant and bounded-potential costs count the active annulus and its collars. For the denominator use the connected **source-zero** fluctuation logarithm of (1.60)–(1.63), whose carriers meet that same annulus; common pure Gaussian factors cancel. The successful branch and the native connected expansion are essential to this comparison. Empty steps cost zero in the log.

The input is holomorphic in the transported background and independent current and may depend measurably on the retained *real* fluctuation coordinates. The original \(V_j\) reconstructed from \(B_j\) and a varying background can itself become complex; arbitrary measurable dependence on that reconstructed variable would not qualify. The boundary input above has the native chart dependence, with its old real \(A_0\) protected by \(h\ge2\). LFR I p.191 maps the new mixed chart into the previous one for both background and current. Thus one can insert the test into each normalized integral as \(Q_j^{-1}\int q_j F\) and compose the bounds. For the admitted \(N\le CR_k\) and comparable widths, the sum of the log costs is bounded by \(C\sum_j R_{j+1}|Z_{j+1}|_{j+1}\le C H(\mathfrak h)\) on this history. The first-layer source anchors already cost at most another fixed multiple of \(H(\mathfrak h)\). LFR II (1.65) admits the final whole-chart test in the original component operation. The historical reserve (MR.8)–(MR.14) absorbs \(\exp(C H(\mathfrak h))\) before integration and history summation. The historical reference denominator is still the unmarked one and the real component mass still multiplies the test. The same source disk controls its first and mixed density jets. This proves a receiving estimate after the complete preliminary integration in this single-component sector. Applying it to several components, or reusing the output as a local activity through another erasure, requires a bound with the inherited spatial support restored.
