Whole-region Gaussian comparison, chart substitution and retained normalization, (C.23)–(C.25)

The whole-chart bound extends to a fixed family consisting entirely of simultaneously successful terminal components with a common frozen exterior and the same admitted incoming history class. In (1.60) count the union of the actual active annuli. The total preliminary log cost is bounded by a fixed constant times the sum of the component history budgets. At (1.71)–(1.72) the product of source-zero component integrations can act on one joint bounded test: factorization is needed only for a positive majorant. All source-zero factors involving several components remain inside the historical sums. Their absolute tree bounds cost at most a fixed constant times the incident terminal volumes, as in §34. Thus the unnormalized global estimate keeps every real component mass, without assigning a potentially nonlocal marked test to an individual owner. This gives a different conclusion from the localized distance estimates below. Joint whole-region bound for successful components, (C.28)

### 34. Distance between two locally owned marks at an erasure

The mixed component factors in LFR II also give a distance estimate for locally owned marks in a real conditional law. Fix the admissible real exterior and the family of components at an erasure. Include every one-owner factor in that owner's positive reference measure, which can include its local history variables. Keep exterior-only weights as constants. On the physical positive disintegration of (1.71)–(1.72) the remaining interactions are \(\exp V_Y\), with owner set \(I_Y\) of size at least two. The historical sums in (1.71) still act on all factors involving that history. Gauge-unfixing is used only with its stated eventual integration and gauge-invariant observables. If a chosen summand is written in complex notation while the full physical weight is positive, taking real parts termwise preserves its product. The argument here concerns the real conditional measure. Let \(f_i,g_j\) be bounded real marks of owners \(i,j\) alone. Strictly raw marks and the captured conditional means in §27 qualify when all their determining data stay with those owners. A bounded global test of §33 need not qualify.

There is an exact probability bound on the interaction graph. Give each multicomponent hyperedge \(Y\) an independent Bernoulli variable of probability \(p_Y=1-\exp[-2\operatorname{osc} V_Y]\), where the oscillation is over its real owner data at the fixed exterior. Under this independent law let \(i\leftrightarrow j\) denote connection by active hyperedges. Then
\[
 |\operatorname{Cov}(f_i,g_j\mid\text{exterior and component family})|
 \le \tfrac12\operatorname{osc}(f_i)\operatorname{osc}(g_j)
               \,\mathbb P_{\rm ind}(i\leftrightarrow j). \tag{O.50}
\]
For each interaction expand \(e^{V_Y}=e^{\inf V_Y}+[e^{V_Y}-e^{\inf V_Y}]\) and call the second, nonnegative term active. Given all component variables these activity indicators are independent and each activation probability is at most \(1-e^{-\operatorname{osc} V_Y}\). Use two independent replicas of the full decorated measure. The union of their active edges is dominated by the independent law above, by using independent uniforms for the two sets of conditional indicators. In the two-replica identity for covariance the integrand is \((f_i-f_i')(g_j-g_j')/2\). If the union does not connect the two owners, swap both the owner variables and the replica activity labels on the entire cluster of \(i\). An edge meeting both the cluster and its complement is inactive in both replicas, with a constant weight. The swap preserves the full measure, keeps the union fixed, and changes the sign of that integrand. This proves (O.50). The normalized inequality can equivalently be multiplied by the square of the **actual** real conditional mass to bound the unnormalized two-source determinant. Zero-mass branches contribute zero to that determinant.

For two owners the right-hand connection probability is at most \(\sum_{Y:\,i,j\in I_Y} p_Y\). For many owners a sufficient spatial estimate uses the coarse distance \(d_Z\) in which motion inside one component costs zero and exterior \(M\)-cube steps cost one. If
\[
 q=\sup_i\sum_{Y:\,i\in I_Y} p_Y (|I_Y|-1)
                e^{m\operatorname{diam}_{d_Z}(I_Y)}<1
\]
for some \(m>0\), a union bound over simple hyperedge paths gives \(\mathbb P_{\rm ind}(i\leftrightarrow j)\le e^{-m d_Z(i,j)}q/(1-q)\). On a simple path edges are distinct, so the probability is bounded by their product. Summation over a next owner costs at most the displayed row sum; the triangle inequality pays the endpoint weight.

For example this yields uniform decay in the following specified native geometries. Each eligible component is contained in a cube of side \(100MR_k\). Suppose the exterior separation between distinct owners, measured in \(M\)-cube units, is \(s\), and the tree-decay reserve is large enough for the coarse lattice tree count below. The bound (1.68) gives \(|V_Y|\le\alpha e^{-(1+2\beta_{\rm geom})\kappa d_{k,Z}(Y)}\) for the mixed factors; (1.69) concerns a one-owner term already included in its measure. A mixed connected carrier starts at one of \(O((1+R_k)^4)\) incident cubes of a specified owner. A geometric tree of length \(t\) in \(M\)-units meeting all its exterior cubes lies in a connected coarse cover of \(C(1+t)\) cubes: traverse its edges in both directions and cover that path in successive unit steps. The possible covers rooted at that port number at most \(C_1^{1+t}\). Choosing exterior cubes within the cover and adjoining incident components at fixed geometry adds at most another exponential in \(1+t\). Each touched component meets an incident exterior cube, so the owner count and the contracted diameter are at most \(C(1+t)\); a mixed interaction between separated owners has \(t\ge c s-C\). Spending part of the tree exponent on entropy and choosing \(m\) small enough therefore gives \(q\le C\alpha(1+R_k)^4 e^{-c' s}\). In particular separations larger than a sufficiently large multiple of \(1+\log(2+R_k)\), with the admitted small \(\alpha\), suffice independently of the number of components. The estimate is per incident cube before the factor for the size of the owner. A uniform such separation has not been assumed for all native histories. The covariance of the two exterior conditional means after this fixed-family calculation is still present in the physical Wilson covariance. Component bounds and positive real operations, LFR II pp. 376–379 · Two-component determinant and relative-tree count

For the **unnormalized** determinant one can remove the separation hypothesis by using the actual historical size budget. Let \(P_i\) count the incident coarse cubes of owner \(i\) (including a fixed collar). The rooted tree count above, now without a lower cutoff on \(t\), gives \(\sum_Y p_Y e^{m\ell_Y}\le C\alpha\sum_i P_i\) for \(\ell_Y=\operatorname{diam}_{d_Z}I_Y\). On a connecting path the sum of \(\ell_Y\) over *all* active hyperedges is at least \(d_Z(i,j)\). Its exponential moment under the independent law factors exactly. Thus
\[
 \mathbb P_{\rm ind}(i\leftrightarrow j)\le
    \exp\!\left[-m d_Z(i,j)+C\alpha\sum_b P_b\right]. \tag{O.51}
\]
Let \(K\) be the full real source-zero mass at this exterior and family. The absolute two-source determinant \(|K(f_i g_j)K-K(f_i)K(g_j)|\) is bounded by (O.51) times \(K^2\operatorname{osc}(f_i)\operatorname{osc}(g_j)/2\). This can be large relative to \(K^2\); the extra positive cost is paid inside the joint history estimate. In particular \(K\) is at most the product of the source-zero one-component masses times \(\exp(C\alpha\sum_bP_b)\), up to any retained common exterior factor. For original anchors owned within the component collars one can also use the uncontracted coarse grid distance: a shortest contracted path need visit each component at most once, and lifting its passages across an eligible rectangle adds at most its diameter plus a fixed collar. Both the total added distance and the incident cube count are bounded by a fixed multiple of the total terminal occupied volume. The \(j=k\) term of (MR.4) includes this volume on each contributing microscopic history, using the cube-volume comparison of (MR.5) at that layer. One therefore inserts the extra size factors into the positive history integrations before using the coefficient-stable bound, keeping the two copies of the mass and every participating component. This gives exponential decay in the **actual uncontracted distance in units of \(ML^k a\)** for the full unnormalized conditional determinant with two locally owned marks, with the absolute history weights retained. It gives no uniform normalized conditional decay for crowded families, nor a bound on the connected correlation after mixing all exterior fields and geometries. Original size reserve, (MR.4) and (MR.8)–(MR.10)

### 35. All connected orders for locally owned marks at the same real erasure

The unnormalized two-component estimate extends to every connected order with one factorial, without a separation assumption on the other owners. Work in the positive fixed-exterior, fixed-family disintegration of §34. Let \(m\ge2\), let \(f_1,\ldots,f_m\) be bounded real observables each local to its indicated owner, and let \(\kappa\) be their connected cumulant under the normalized component measure. Several insertions may use the same owner. Let \(\tau_Z\) be the length of a shortest tree joining the indicated owners when travel inside components costs zero. With the entropy and decay constants of §34 there exist fixed \(b,c,C>0\) for which
\[
 K^m|\kappa(f_1,\ldots,f_m)|
 \le K^m C^m m!\,\prod_{u=1}^m\operatorname{osc}(f_u)\,
        \exp\!\left[-b\tau_Z+c m\alpha\sum_i P_i\right]. \tag{O.52}
\]
Here \(K\) is the **unmarked real mass** of the full component integration. Multiplication by \(K^m\) turns the cumulant into the usual polynomial in the unnormalized jet integrals and \(K\). In applying its historical bound, use one replica of the kernel for each factor of \(K\).

Take \(m\) independent replicas of the decorated probability measure of §34, indexed by \(v=0,\ldots,m-1\), and put \(\zeta=e^{2\pi i/m}\), \(Y_u=\sum_v\zeta^v f_u(\sigma^v)\). There is an exact identity
\[
 \mathbb E\prod_{u=1}^m Y_u = m\,\kappa(f_1,\ldots,f_m).
\]
Indeed \(\log\mathbb E\exp(\sum t_uY_u)\) is the sum over replicas of the one-replica log generating function with every source multiplied by \(\zeta^v\). The coefficients of degrees one through \(m-1\) vanish, while those of degree \(m\) are multiplied by \(m\). Exponentiating back leaves those degree-\(m\) coefficients unchanged; take the mixed derivative at zero. This is a finite derivative identity for bounded variables.

Form the union of the active hyperedges in all replicas. If the marked owners are disconnected in this union, take the cluster of the first marked owner, which contains \(j\) of the insertion labels with \(1\le j<m\). Cyclically permute the replicas of all variables in that cluster and of its internal activity labels. Boundary edges are inactive in every replica and have constant weight. The measure and the cluster are preserved while \(\prod Y_u\) acquires a factor \(\zeta^{\pm j}\). On each class with that \(j\) its expectation is zero. Consequently the entire expectation comes from configurations in which the union connects the marked owners. Subtracting any constant from \(f_u\) leaves \(Y_u\) unchanged, so \(|Y_u|\le m\operatorname{osc}(f_u)\).

Use independent uniform random variables for the conditional edge draws in all replicas. The union is dominated by independent hyperedges with probabilities \(p_Y^{(m)}=1-e^{-m\operatorname{osc}V_Y}\le m\operatorname{osc}V_Y\). Give each edge the length \(t_Y\) of a connected tree in the contracted coarse graph joining **all** owners in \(I_Y\). The tree in (1.67), its bounded-cell cover and the incident exterior cubes give \(t_Y\le C(1+d_{k,Z}(Y))\); the construction uses its entire connecting tree, including possible many-owner edges. If the marked components are connected, the sum of lengths over active edges is at least \(\tau_Z\). Exponential Markov and independence bound the connection probability by
\[
 e^{-b\tau_Z}\prod_Y [1+p_Y^{(m)}(e^{bt_Y}-1)]
 \le \exp[-b\tau_Z+C m\alpha\textstyle\sum_i P_i] .
\]
Use the rooted exterior-tree count of §34 with fixed small \(b\); the constants are independent of \(m\). Together with the replica identity and \(m^{m-1}\le e^m m!\), this proves (O.52).

To restore the tree length in actual coarse coordinates, adjoin one spanning tree per visited connected owner and its fixed collar. The total added length is bounded by a fixed multiple of their occupied terminal volumes, regardless of how many labels visit each owner. These costs, the component-port exponential and the bound of \(K^m\) by \(m\) copies of the product source-zero masses are all charged in the original positive history sums. Their total positive coefficient is at most \(C m\) times the one-replica terminal volume. Each of the \(m\) replicas has the fixed present owner geometry and its own past history, with that terminal volume included in (MR.4). Assigning a fixed cost to each replica therefore preserves an order-independent coefficient in the reserve. The factorial in (O.52) is the insertion-order cost. The result gives a spatial tree bound at this erasure scale for unnormalized connected jets of locally owned marks. It does not transfer a generally nonlocal marked output of §33 to a local owner or normalize the cumulant after summing over exterior data. Those steps are needed for the full higher-order theorem.

If several original sources lie in one captured integration island, one must retain their joint conditional moments. For (O.52) enlarge its owner variable by a draw of the original integrated island links from the positive conditional law \(I_D/d_D\) in §27. The mixed factors ignore these auxiliary coordinates, the owner keeps its original mass, and products of the actual bounded plaquette marks under this enlarged measure give the true joint jets. Other owners are unaffected. This avoids replacing the conditional expectation of a product by the product of its first conditional moments.

There is a version for local source-density factors themselves. Give each of the \(m\) source labels to a single owner. Suppose \(F_i(t_{I_i};\sigma_i)\) is that owner's true holomorphic density factor, equal to one at zero, with a fixed disk for the combined source and modulus bounded by a historical reserve \(\exp(C H_i)\). Put \(G(t)=K(\prod_i F_i(t))/K\). In place of the product of the \(Y_u\), take the coefficient of \(t_1\cdots t_m\) in
\[
 \mathbb E\prod_{v=0}^{m-1}\prod_i F_i(\zeta^v t_{I_i};\sigma_i^v).
\]
It equals \(m[t_1\cdots t_m]\log G\) by the same cancellation of degrees. On a disconnected cluster containing \(j\) source labels, rotating its replicas acts as multiplication by \(\zeta^{\pm j}\) on that squarefree coefficient. This handles the true local mixed jets without a choice of factorization inside an owner. Cauchy on radii \(r_0/m\) for the individual labels stays in a prescribed disk for the combined source, even with repeated directions. Its cost \((m/r_0)^m\) is bounded by \((e/r_0)^m m!\). After multiplication by \(K^m\), the absolute-bound factors \(\exp(C H_i)\) can be inserted in the original positive one-owner integrations of each replica; any reweighting used in domination has already been paid for there. The conditional activation bounds stay uniform in the owner variables. The same tree estimate therefore holds with this Cauchy prefactor and the corresponding historical costs. The hypothesis of a genuinely local density factor is essential.

### 36. Comparing two real exteriors without differentiating a cutoff

For locally owned marks one can also test which **exterior** data change their means. Work with two admitted real exteriors \(u,u'\) on the same component geometry, at which the positive disintegrations of §34 apply. Suppose they agree outside a specified *change set* \(D\) of exterior cubical data; all comparisons include the actual determining collars of the operations. In the fixed local representation a one-owner kernel is identical if its determining data have not changed. A mixed interaction can change only if its determining carrier meets \(D\). Single-owner factors that otherwise depend on a distant exterior patch may instead be left as one-owner interactions with that exterior patch, provided they have the same small, localized bound (1.68). Pure exterior weights drop out of normalized expectations. Compare an owner mark \(f_i\) with the same function on both sides, as is the case for a strictly raw local gauge invariant. For a captured density readout also require its own determining data at the owner to agree, or retain its direct change separately.

Decorate one replica in each measure, using for each mixed interaction the *common* inactive constant \(\exp(\min(\inf V_Y(u),\inf V_Y(u')))\). Treat owners whose single-owner reference kernels can change as pinned. For an interaction which can change, its activation in either replica connects every one of its owners also to a changed determining cell in that carrier. An owner incident to several active unchanged factors has the usual component connections. If the union cluster of \(i\) reaches neither a pinned owner nor any such activated changed interaction, interchange the two replicas on that entire cluster. Every one-owner measure there is identical in the two integrals. Every internal active factor has identical interaction on the two sides, and all crossed inactive factors have common constant weight. The map preserves the union event and cancels \(f_i(\sigma_i)-f_i(\sigma'_i)\). Thus the absolute change in its conditional mean is at most \(\operatorname{osc}(f_i)\) times the probability of reaching the change set or a pin. This bound allows the measures at the pins to differ arbitrarily.

Given both sets of owner data, union activations are dominated by independent hyperedges with probabilities bounded by \(C\alpha e^{-c d_{k,Z}(Y)}\), using the sup bound (1.68) at both exteriors. Attach a changed cell as a terminal vertex when needed. Its distance from a touched owner in the contracted coarse graph is paid by the same carrier tree, counting the determining collar. In the sparse real families of §34 the simple-path sum now starts at \(i\) and stops at its *first* pin or active edge meeting changed data. Intermediate weighted rows sum to \(q<1\), and the last weighted edge sum is bounded by the per-port estimate, including one-owner small interactions if present. This gives exponential decay in the contracted distance to the union of the pinned owners and changed determining cells, with a per-port prefactor at the terminal step if no uniform terminal row bound is available. On arbitrary families the total-length bound in (O.51) gives instead a factor \(\exp(C\alpha\sum_i P_i)\). Multiplication by \(K(u)K(u')\) retains both actual masses; the same terminal-volume charge restores uncontracted distance to these sets. This is a real finite-difference estimate at fixed geometry. It gives no variation control across a change of history class, and it does not extend a global marked test to independent complex current data. Local determining carriers and bounds (1.67)–(1.72), LFR II pp. 376–379

### 37. A fixed source disk for sparse real component families

For sparse families one can bound the **normalized** connected jets in (O.52) uniformly in the number of components, without the terminal-volume factor. At fixed admitted real exterior let every locally owned bounded real mark enter as \(\exp(\sum_{u\in i}z_u f_u)\) in its component. This includes the latent-variable realization of a captured island in §35. Assume the mixed factors of §34 have enough decay for the following strengthened row sum, with \(t_Y\) its contracted tree length:
\[
 \delta=\sup_i\sum_{Y:\,i\in I_Y}
     (4e)^{|I_Y|} e^{b t_Y}\|e^{V_Y}-1\|_\infty\le\log(3/2).
 \tag{O.53}
\]
The per-port count in §34 bounds this by \(C\alpha \sup_iP_i e^{-c s}\) when a fixed part of the native tree exponent pays the displayed owner entropy. A sufficiently sparse fixed family with the admitted parameters therefore satisfies the hypothesis. All one-owner terms, however large, are in their original positive reference measures. Any positive reference mass can be normalized here and restored on taking the partition ratio.

Choose \(r_0>0\) small enough that \(e^{r_0}/(2-e^{r_0})\le2\). On the source domain \(\sup_i\sum_{u\in i}|z_u|\|f_u\|_\infty\le r_0\), each normalized one-owner source factor has integral within \(e^{r_0}-1<1\) of one, and its tilted measure has total variation at most two. Expand only the mixed factors as \(1+(e^{V_Y}-1)\). A connected nonempty set \(\gamma\) of selected hyperedges, connected by overlap of owners, is one hard-core polymer with owner set \(I_\gamma\). Its activity is the product of those edge functions integrated in the tilted product measure on its owners. Disjoint sets of owners factor, so this is an exact finite product expansion. The bound for its activity \(w_\gamma\) is \(2^{|I_\gamma|}\prod_{Y\in\gamma}\|e^{V_Y}-1\|_\infty\), throughout the source disk.

For clarity, the row condition (O.53) implies
\[
 \sum_{\gamma:\,i\in I_\gamma}|w_\gamma|
             e^{|I_\gamma|+b\sum_{Y\in\gamma}t_Y}\le\tfrac12 .
\]
Root a connected family at \(i\), choosing a spanning exploration in its incidence graph by a fixed ordering. At each reached owner choose an unordered set of incident edges, then give each owner of each chosen edge its own subsequent subtree. Allowing repeated owners or edges in different subtrees only overcounts the original connected families. If the generating sum including an empty subtree is at most two at depth \(d\), at depth \(d+1\) it is at most \(\exp(\sum_{Y\ni i}(2e)^{|I_Y|} 2^{|I_Y|}e^{bt_Y}\|e^{V_Y}-1\|_\infty)\le3/2\). The factors \((2e)^{|I_Y|}\) cover both total variation and owner weight. Start at depth zero and subtract the empty term. Thus the sum over polymers incompatible with any given polymer is bounded, with these weights, by its owner count. The Kotecký–Preiss theorem applied to the absolute majorants gives the convergent logarithm and a uniform bound on its absolute cluster sum anchored at one owner, with the spatial weight retained. For the rooted bound one may adjoin a test polymer of zero activity supported on that one owner. [Kotecký and Preiss, *Cluster expansion for abstract polymer models*](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01211762)

The full source ratio is zero-free throughout the interior of this disk and has an analytic logarithm chosen to vanish at zero. It includes the individual one-owner logarithms in addition to the mixed polymer log. A term contributing a derivative in all the labels must contain every marked owner; its cluster connects them and \(\sum t_Y\ge\tau_Z\). The one-owner log contributes only when all labels have that owner. On circles of radii \(r_0/(2m\|f_u\|_\infty)\) the same absolute bounds apply. Hence the normalized connected derivatives satisfy
\[
 |\kappa(f_1,\ldots,f_m\mid\text{exterior and component family})|
     \le C^m m!\prod_u\|f_u\|_\infty\ e^{-b\tau_Z}. \tag{O.54}
\]
A zero mark gives zero directly. This is a disk for the total strength at each owner, with bounds independent of how many other sparse owners are present. It is not a fixed independent source radius for an unbounded number of original marks concentrated in a single owner. The all-history physical logarithm also includes the change of outside weights and nonlocal source terms.

### 38. Joint dependence of a connected source response on its exterior

The preceding bounds also control the change in an entire connected jet between two *real* exteriors satisfying the common-geometry and determining-set conditions of §36. Let \(\tau_D\) be the least contracted length of a tree connecting all the marked owners and reaching either a pinned owner or a changed exterior cell in a determining carrier. For a pin, count distance to that owner; to include a changed cell outside its owner add the actual determining collar or keep the pin as endpoint. Take \(m\) decorated replicas at each of the two exteriors. Write \(Y_i^u,Y_i^{u'}\) for the corresponding root-of-unity sums of §35. Then \(m(\kappa_u-\kappa_{u'})=\mathbb E[\prod_iY_i^u-\prod_iY_i^{u'}]\) for common locally owned marks.

Use the union of all \(2m\) sets of active *owner* hyperedges. If the marks are split among several owner clusters, rotate the replica numbers on the first cluster **within each exterior separately**. Both products acquire the same nontrivial phase, even when the cluster meets changed data. Thus this part vanishes. If the marks are in one owner cluster which contains no pin and no active interaction touching changed data, exchange the two sets of replicas pairwise on that cluster. All crossed inactive weights coincide by the common-constant choice of §36, and the difference of the two products changes sign. This part also vanishes. A contributing configuration therefore has a genuinely connected source cluster reaching a change. No path through two distant pieces of the change set is used to connect the marks.

For each changed active edge choose an incident changed cell in its determining carrier and include it when counting that edge's length. The relative tree and its bounded collar pay this extension in the same rooted sum; a single changed cell is sufficient. On the surviving event the total length of active edges bounds \(\tau_D\). The independent domination now has edge probabilities at most \(C m\alpha e^{-c d_{k,Z}(Y)}\). Taking the exponential moment as in (O.51) gives
\[
 |\kappa_u(f_1,\ldots,f_m)-\kappa_{u'}(f_1,\ldots,f_m)|
 \le C^m m!\prod_i\operatorname{osc}(f_i)
       \exp[-b\tau_D+C m\alpha\textstyle\sum_j P_j] . \tag{O.55}
\]
When using this estimate without a sparseness restriction, multiply by the original masses \(K(u)^m K(u')^m\) and charge the cost in the \(2m\) positive historical integrations as in §35. With arbitrary many marked locations in the same owner, traversing its actual connected volume is still paid only once in restoring the coarse spatial metric.

For the sparse families of §37 one obtains the corresponding **normalized** difference directly from the two absolutely convergent cluster logarithms. Use (O.53) with the stronger edge length \(C(1+d_{k,Z}(Y))\) needed to reach changed determining cells with a sufficiently small exponential rate. The same separation and entropy check applies. A term which omits some marked owner gives zero after the mixed source derivative. A term containing all marked owners cancels between the two exteriors unless it involves a changed base measure at a pin or a changed mixed interaction in its cluster. In the surviving cluster the unused spatial weight pays \(e^{-b\tau_D}\). The uniform anchored estimate and the same source circles as in §37 give the right side of (O.55) with \(\prod\|f_i\|_\infty\) and without the port-volume exponential. Local one-owner logarithms obey the same rule, with zero tree length if that owner is pinned. These are finite differences for the stated real joint component integration; comparison of an arbitrary globally reconstructed old source and continuation in independent complex current directions remain separate estimates.

### 39. Two distant fluctuation regions in one physical preliminary integration

Fix the new real background, successful geometry, and all other active variables in a preliminary integration of LFR I (1.25), (1.53)–(1.60). Let \(x,y\) be the independent old and new fluctuations on two distinct components, including their axial determining corridors, and \(D\) their separation in current \(M\)-cube units. Work in smaller regularity charts where the four corners and the separate interpolations of the new determining fluctuations remain admitted, uniformly up to the native margin factors. For the bound stated with the history volume \(H\), assume a persistent charged component from the first layer through the current scale \(k\). Put the sharp successful fluctuation cutoffs in the positive product reference. The remaining conditional density \(q\) then obeys
\[
 \left|\log\frac{q(x,y)q(0,0)}{q(x,0)q(0,y)}\right|
       \le C\exp(CH-cD). \tag{O.56}
\]

The calculation uses the effective action before its Gaussian Taylor splitting. At fixed new data the gauge factors, eliminated block-average bond variables and Haar densities are block local. The old Gaussian cross-component terms are already included in the effective boundary action by the separation after B119 (2.21). The sharp constraints on the remaining free coordinates in LFR I (1.9), (1.55) factor by component. These facts allow the four-corner quotient to retain every nonlocal term in the effective action, including earlier preliminary terms.

For a localized term on \(X\), changing a fluctuation region \(S\) which misses all its explicit old real arguments changes only its regular reconstructed field. The constrained minimum derivative at nonzero admitted fluctuations in CMP102 (190), block-local restoration of axial gauge, and a Cauchy estimate on the smaller chart bound this oscillation by its native carrier weight times \(\exp(-c\,\mathrm{dist}_{k,M}(X,S))\), up to fixed powers of \(L^k\), inverse coupling, and support size. If an explicit real argument is touched, use twice the native supremum at distance zero. For the Wilson action use its individual plaquettes. The four-corner difference is bounded by twice the smaller of the two one-region oscillations. The two distances to \(X\) plus its diameter pay for \(D\); keeping part of the initial exponential allows the remaining carriers and their locations to be summed. The logarithm of the resulting prefactor is at most \(C[k+|\log g_{\min}|+\log(2+\sum_S|S|_k)]\), which is charged to the specified persistent history. The argument and the original chart references are given in the real conditional-measure calculation.

After normalization, the law on the two regions differs from the product law with weight proportional to \(q(x,0)q(0,y)\) by at most \(C\min(1,e^{CH-cD})\) in total variation. This follows by bounding the normalized likelihood ratio between \(e^{-2\|\Delta_x\Delta_y\log q\|_\infty}\) and \(e^{2\|\Delta_x\Delta_y\log q\|_\infty}\). It bounds the conditional covariance of two separate bounded functions with the corresponding product of norms. For transported marks one must also bound their off-region replacement errors, any mixed incoming density jet, and the covariance over the newly conditioned background. The last term survives even if the two-region law factorizes exactly on every fixed background.

### 40. An unconditional decay estimate across physical time slices on the selected sequence

The scalar boundary score of §25 admits a weaker decay exponent all the way to half the time circumference. Let \(H_{t,z}\) be the closed-cube energy of §13, and \(J_h(t)\) the covariance between spatial sums with the same real weights \(h(z)\) at times zero and \(t\). Apply (O.15) with the same \(h\) on all \(n\) time slices and divide by \(n\). Translation invariance gives
\[
 \sum_{s=0}^{n-1}J_h(s)\le\chi_n(\beta)\|h\|_{\ell^2}^2 .
\]
Every summand is nonnegative. At \(s=0\) it is a variance. At \(1\le s\le n-1\) the reflected one-slab insertion for \(h\) gives the sum of squared matrix entries with nonnegative transfer powers as in (O.34), including adjacent slabs. Also \(J_h(s)\) decreases from \(s=1\) through \(n/2\), by the paired transfer exponentials; a zero-eigenvalue endpoint term can only increase the value at \(s=1\). Therefore \(tJ_h(t)\le\chi_n(\beta)\|h\|_{\ell^2}^2\) for \(1\le t\le n/2\).

In particular the susceptibility-selected stopped sequences of §§13 and 20 satisfy, with a constant uniform in lattice spacing and volume,
\[
 0\le\beta^2 C(t,0)\le C/t,\qquad
 \beta\|M_j\|_2\le C(2j+1)^{-1/2},\qquad
 \beta^2\nu_{n,\beta}([0,u])\le C(u+n^{-1})\quad(0\le u\le1). \tag{O.57}
\]
For the last estimate test (O.34) at a time comparable to \(\min(n/2,1/u)\), with \(u=0\) tested at \(n/2\); use the total-mass bound at short times. The same column bound holds in any lattice direction, and spatial translation Cauchy–Schwarz followed by a coordinate permutation bounds \(\beta^2|C(x)|\) by \(C/\|x\|_\infty\) in periodic lattice distances for separated cubes. Hence the score of the actual scalar cube, conditioned on both time slices, has norm \(O(\sqrt{a/d}/\beta)\) even at fixed physical separation \(d\). This estimate includes all exterior histories on that sequence. The required score exponent in (O.36) is four; (O.57) gives one half there, and the power eight in the spectral target is still required at energies of order \(a/d\).

### 41. Keeping the weight of a first failed fluctuation test

There is a comparison across the two alternatives of LFR I (1.55). Work on its common real parent before that split, under the other conditions in §39. Require the fixed exterior test, the primitive group-link cutoff (1.27), and the old-fluctuation requirement (1.28) to hold. The native implication (1.29) applies before (1.55). On this parent the only hard restrictions on the active variables are the component conditions (1.9), (1.27); the positive gauge weights are kept. In particular (1.54) already bounds the logarithmic free-field chart on the entire parent. The interpolation for (O.56) can use the center \(V_Z^{(j)}\) from (1.26): shrink the eigenphases of each starred free link relative to that center, linearly shrink the old fluctuation coordinates, and reconstruct the eliminated bond by its block constraint. This preserves the primitive group cutoff. Both endpoints of the later decision (1.55) can thus be tested on the same admitted smaller regular charts.

Let \(E\) be success or failure of (1.55) in the second component, \(f\) a bounded function of the first, and \(K\) the original unnormalized integral on this parent. At each fixed second-component value, the conditional likelihood in the first component differs from one common corner law by a factor between \(\exp(-2\|W\|_\infty)\) and \(\exp(2\|W\|_\infty)\), where \(W\) is the four-corner logarithm in §39. Mixing over the original second-component distribution restricted to \(E\), and also over its full distribution, gives
\[
 |K(f\mathbf1_E)K(1)-K(f)K(\mathbf1_E)|
 \le C\,\operatorname{osc}(f)\min(1,e^{CH-cD}) K(1)K(\mathbf1_E). \tag{O.58}
\]
This comparison retains the entire moderate-failure event with its actual mass. It applies to a local replacement of a transported mark up to twice its uniform replacement error in the two conditional means. A selector already fixed by the exterior, such as \(\chi_k^{(n+1)}\) at (1.25), can change deterministically between exteriors. Equation (O.58) compares random alternatives inside one parent. Common parent, admissible paths and event weights

The failed component in §41 undergoes an exact change of free variables in (1.59). At the fixed parent it is confined to those bonds. Carrying the mark, event and Jacobian together therefore preserves (O.58), including its event mass. For a physical free link the substitution has the form \(e^{ig_j B_{\rm new}}e^{i(1-\gamma(B_{\rm new}))H_Z}V_Z^{(j)}\); full switching cancels the middle factor. For a Wilson trace on finitely many partially switched bonds, telescoping in the real unitary norm costs at most \(C\sum_b\|H_Z(b)\|\) if those middle factors are dropped. Equations (1.57)–(1.58) bound each by \(C\delta'_j e^{-R_j}\). A merely measurable old mark can always be pushed forward exactly, but this last approximation requires a modulus of continuity. Comparison of two different post-failure backgrounds still requires their own common representation or a weighted finite difference.

### 42. Varying a distant coarse background through successful preliminary steps

For a run on a fixed geometry let \(Z\) contain the actual successful components and determining collars. On one native inner chart give a tangent perturbation \(\xi\) of the base field and independent current the norm \(\sup_x e^{-\mu d_k(x,Z)}|\xi_x|\), using terminal cube distances. A perturbation of raw supremum norm one at distance \(D\) thus has norm at most \(e^{-\mu D}\). A normalized preliminary map \(P_j F=\int q_j(v;y)F(\Phi_j(v;y);y)\,dy/\int q_j(v;y)\,dy\) has the exact derivative
\[
 D(P_jF)[\xi]=P_j[(DF)(\Phi_j)D\Phi_j[\xi]]
       +P_j[(F(\Phi_j)-P_jF)\,D\gamma_j[\xi]],\qquad
 \gamma_j(v;y)=\log q_j(v;y)-\log q_j(v;0). \tag{O.59}
\]
Here the successful fluctuation cutoffs use their fixed real free coordinates. The subtracted zero-fluctuation action can be large and has zero contribution to the second term. For \(t_j\) the total variation of the normalized source-zero kernel, \(a_j\) the weighted operator norm of \(D\Phi_j\), and \(b_j\) the dual weighted norm of \(D\gamma_j\), the new differential norm is bounded by \(t_j a_j\|DF\|+t_j(1+t_j)b_j\|F\|_\infty\).

The independent-base walk expansion in B116 (1.2)–(1.29) and the local current formula B109 (3.10)–(3.13) supply the following application **on quantified inner charts**. A carrier contributing to the derivative of the minimizing correction relative to zero fluctuation must reach the observed bond, the differentiated data and the fluctuation support. The anchored decay can absorb the exponential weight above. The same connection applies to the difference of the two background values of an old localized potential, using two inner margins for its second derivative. Explicit real fluctuation arguments stay fixed in this differentiation. Let \(\rho_*\) be a lower bound on the local raw perturbation radii for the projected fields, independent currents and localized potentials along the run, and \(V_*\) the largest active region volume in its own scale units, including the terminal union. If there are \(N\) preliminary integrations, a sufficient budget is
\[
 N[1+k+|\log g_{\min}|+\log(1+\rho_*^{-1})+\log(2+V_*)]\le C H .
\]
With the actual source-zero bounds of §33, the recurrence then gives \(|DF_{\rm term}[\xi]|\le e^{C'H-\mu D}\|\xi\|_\infty\) for variations supported at distance at least \(D\) from \(Z\), on a fixed inner source disk. It also bounds real differences along admitted paths with their sup-norm length. This is one distant variation relative to the union of the charged components. Uniform raw link radii for every irregular projected chart of LFR I (1.64)–(1.69), and switches of the determining geometry, are further requirements. Weighted derivative, chart test and proof, (C.29)–(C.34)

### 43. Angular and momentum dependence of the scalar bound at physical distances

On a susceptibility-selected sequence define the spatial convolution operator \(K_t\) with kernel \(\beta^2 C(t,z)\). Its quadratic forms are \(\beta^2J_h(t)\), so §40 gives \(0\le K_t\le (C/t)I\) for \(1\le t\le n/2\). Consequently
\[
 \sum_z|\beta^2 C(t,z)|^2=(K_t^2)(0,0)
      \le (C/t)K_t(0,0)\le C^2/t^2.
\]
Cover the periodic cube sphere \(\|x\|_\infty=R\) by its eight coordinate faces. Its size is comparable to \(R^3\), also at \(R=n/2\). Applying the last bound with each normal coordinate in turn yields
\[
 \left(\frac{1}{|\{\|x\|_\infty=R\}|}
       \sum_{\|x\|_\infty=R}|\beta^2 C(x)|^2\right)^{1/2}
      \le C R^{-5/2}. \tag{O.60}
\]
This bounds the mean absolute covariance over actual pairs at fixed cubical radius. A signed smooth average on a four-dimensional annulus has size \(O(R^{-4})\) after division by \(R^4\). Indeed partition into smooth patches on which one coordinate \(|t|\) is comparable to \(R\). At each fixed \(t\) the sampled smooth weight \(w(t/R,z/R)\) has spatial Fourier norm \(n^{-3}\sum_p|\widehat w_t(p)|\le C_w\) by rapid Fourier decay and periodization. Thus its pairing with \(K_t(z)\) is \(O_w(1/|t|)\), and there are \(O(R)\) time slices. Take weights supported in the fundamental domain or periodize their boundedly many images. The signed estimate allows cancellations that the root-mean-square estimate does not use.

There is also a simultaneous energy and momentum bound. Diagonalize the transfer matrix together with spatial translations. In (O.34) attach to each ordered energy transition the lattice momentum difference of its two states; symmetrize the momentum sign when grouping forward and backward terms. Call this positive measure \(\nu(dE,dp)\), with its original spatially local slab normalization. For every real spatial weight \(h\), inserting its translated slab multiplies each transition weight by \(|\widehat h(p)|^2\); this is even in \(p\). Finite translation orthogonality fixes this multiplier with the convention \(\widehat h(p)=\sum_z h(z)e^{-ipz}\). By §40 and the same short-time test as in (O.57),
\[
 \beta^2\!\int_{E\le u}|\widehat h(p)|^2d\nu(E,p)
    \le C(u+n^{-1})\|h\|_{\ell^2}^2,\qquad 0\le u\le1. 
\]
At short times use the adjacent-slab positive bound for the weighted transition mass. Choose a nonnegative smooth bump of spatial width \(r\), total weight one and squared norm \(O(r^{-3})\), with \(1\le r\le n/8\). Its Fourier modulus exceeds a fixed constant on \(|p|\le c/r\). Modulate the bump by a lattice momentum \(p_0\); the sum of the two tests given by its real and imaginary parts bounds half the squared modulus of the shifted bump. Therefore
\[
 \beta^2\nu\{E\le u,\ |p-p_0|\le c/r\}
       \le C(u+n^{-1})r^{-3}. \tag{O.61}
\]
Near zero momentum the fixed-physical, separated-test estimate improves the cylinder bound. Choose \(r\asymp a^{-1}\), \(u\asymp1/r\), and normalize a fixed smooth spatial bump at that width. Use two nonnegative smooth unit-mass time averages, each supported on \(O(r)\) slices, with every pairwise time difference between \(c_1r\) and \(c_2r\) for \(0<c_1<c_2\). The spacetime weights are fixed smooth physical tests times \(O(a^4)\) normalization constants. On the selected sequence (O.45) therefore bounds the resulting scaled bare covariance by \(C\beta^{-\epsilon}a^4\). Every term is \(J_h(s)\ge J_h(\lceil c_2r\rceil)\) by §40. Testing its transfer representation there gives
\[
 \beta^2\nu\{E\le C_0a,\ |p|\le c_0a\}
       \le C\beta^{-\epsilon}a^4 \tag{O.62}
\]
for fixed radii, by choosing the bump width and time placements accordingly. Equations (O.60)–(O.62) reach physical separations or momenta on one real sequence. They leave open the full \(O(a^8)\) low-energy estimate of §24 with unrestricted spatial momentum and the field normalization needed for the continuum two-point function.

### 44. Fourier covariance of the bare cube at the prescribed coupling

Use the compatible prescribed couplings and box estimates of §22. Write \(S_\beta(q)=\beta^2\sum_x C(x)e^{-iqx}\), and \(S_{G,n}\) for the same cube placement and normalization in the finite-torus Gaussian curl field. Both are nonnegative. For every torus momentum,
\[
 S_\beta(q)\ \ge\ S_{G,n}(q)-C\beta^{-\epsilon},\qquad
 n^{-4}\sum_q |S_\beta(q)-S_{G,n}(q)|\ \le\ C\beta^{-\epsilon/6}.
 \tag{O.63}
\]

For the lower bound use the sum of the actual conditional variances over the boxes of side \(l\asymp\beta^\epsilon\). A unit-modulus plane wave on cube origins gives plaquette coefficients of modulus at most one through the face average. In a good box Taylor-expand the marked action about the actual minimum in thermal fluctuation coordinates. Minimize the squared residual over *all affine functions* of these coordinates. This gives a lower bound on the conditional variance and removes any large linear term about that minimum. On the ball \(|y|\le\beta^{1/32}\) the true density dominates \(1-O(\beta^{-1/10})\) times the flat Gaussian density, by the Hessian comparison and density estimates of §22. The quadratic term there differs from \(\frac12\langle dy,M_qdy\rangle\) by \(O(l^{18}\beta^{-1/4}|y|^2+l^4\beta^{-1/2}|y|^3)\), where \(M_q\) multiplies by the plaquette coefficients. For complex coefficients take the squared norms of real and imaginary parts. The centered flat Gaussian ball is symmetric, so the best linear part for the quadratic is zero; its variance differs from the full Gaussian quadratic variance by an exponentially small tail. The norm of the quadratic after centering is \(O(l^2)\), since its squared norm is bounded by a constant times the number of bonds. These bounds, and the exponentially small chance of a bad box, leave an error \(O(\beta^{-1/10})\) per unit lattice volume after allowing the powers of \(l\).

In the flat comparison let \(P_l\) be the projection onto the direct sum of the box curl images and \(P_n\) the full torus curl projection. They are nested, and \(\operatorname{rank}(P_n-P_l)=O(n^4/l)\) by the box dimension and \(\operatorname{rank}P_n=3(n^4-1)\) per color. The Gaussian variance is proportional to \(\|P M_q P\|_{\rm HS}^2\). In the orthogonal splitting \(P_n=P_l+(P_n-P_l)\), the squared norms of the three blocks involving the difference cost at most \(3\operatorname{rank}(P_n-P_l)\|M_q\|^2\). This proves the uniform lower bound. For the second inequality in (O.63), the average of the signed Fourier difference is precisely the difference of the zero-displacement variances. Its modulus is \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon/6})\) by (O.33) and the finite face placement. The finite-torus and infinite Gaussian diagonals at fixed offsets differ by \(O(1/n)\): the symbol of the curl projection is bounded and has first derivative \(O(1/|p|)\), giving this Riemann-sum error by integrating cellwise away from the origin. The negative part of the Fourier difference is everywhere bounded by the first inequality. These conclusions use the prescribed bare coupling, independently of susceptibility selection.

There is a pointwise consequence away from the four coordinate-zero momentum planes. At fixed transverse momentum, take the corresponding real sine and cosine spatial weights and add their temporal transfer forms. For each nonzero transition energy put \(b=e^{-E}<1\). The Fourier transform over \(s=1,\ldots,n-1\) of the two exponentials in (O.34) is
\[
 2(1-b^n)\frac{\cos\omega-b}{1-2b\cos\omega+b^2}-2b^{n-1}.
\]
It increases with \(\cos\omega\); a possible \(b=0\) adjacent-slab term has the same property. A zero-energy term gives a positive spike at \(\omega=0\) plus a constant, and the \(s=0\) term is independent of \(\omega\). Coordinate reflections of this cube make its two-point function even separately in all coordinates. Thus \(S_\beta(q)\) decreases in each \(|q_i|\) in the principal Brillouin zone. The free multiplier is uniformly Lipschitz: its first spatial moment is bounded by the second moment of the free cube covariance plus its mass. For the latter second moment, the \(n^{-4}\) mean square of a one-mesh frequency difference of the curl projection is \(O(n^{-2})\) by the same derivative bound and dimension four. Parseval and \(n|\sin(\pi x_i/n)|\ge 2|x_i|\) in periodic representatives bound the weighted squared curl rows, hence the scalar second moment by Wick contraction.

If every \(|q_i|\ge\eta\gg 1/n\), average \(S_\beta\) on the inward coordinate rectangle of side \(\eta\) ending at \((|q_i|)\). Monotonicity bounds \(S_\beta(q)\) above by this average. Its excess over \(S_{G,n}(q)\) is \(O(\eta+\beta^{-\epsilon/6}\eta^{-4})\), by (O.63) and the free modulus. Taking \(\eta=\beta^{-\epsilon/30}\) gives uniform \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon/30})\) agreement on that region, together with the one-sided inequality elsewhere. This localization of the remaining Fourier error still permits narrow peaks near coordinate-zero planes, including the physical low-energy sector.

### 45. Pointwise decay in each coordinate of the physical displacement

Let \(r_i=1+|x_i|\) in periodic lattice representatives. For the original closed-cube scalar at any coupling with the §13 geometry,
\[
 |C(x)|\le C\,\chi_n(\beta)\prod_{i=1}^4 r_i^{-1}.
 \tag{O.64}
\]
On the susceptibility selections of §31, for \(x\ne0\) this sharpens to
\[
 \beta^2|C(x)|\le C\left(\prod_{i=1}^4 r_i^{-1}\right)
           \left(\beta^{-\epsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^4 r_i^{-2}\right).
 \tag{O.65}
\]
In particular, for fixed physical separations with all four displacement components nonzero, the scaled bare covariance is \(O(a^4(\beta^{-\epsilon}+a^2))\). The angular mean of its absolute value on the entire cubical sphere of lattice radius \(R\) is \(O(R^{-4}\log^3(2+R))\) by (O.64) and the one-dimensional harmonic sum on each face. The dimension-eight estimate requires controlling displacements along the coordinate planes as well as improving the power in the interior of these angular sectors.

For the proof normalize the covariance multiplier by \(\chi_n(\beta)\); a zero susceptibility gives zero covariance directly by (O.15). Hold three allowed real momenta fixed. For one temporal transition write \(b=e^{-E}\). The formula in §44, after subtraction of its value at \(\omega=\pi\) and normalization by the jump from \(\pi\) to zero, gives the kernel
\[
 \phi_b(\omega)=\frac{(1-b)^2(1+\cos\omega)}
                     {2(1-2b\cos\omega+b^2)},\quad 0\le b<1 .
\]
Thus the normalized multiplier is its value at \(\pi\) plus a positive mixture of these kernels and a positive spike at zero from equal energies. The mixture and spike together have mass at most one by (O.15). This argument holds in every coordinate by hypercubic symmetry. On a complex disk of radius \(c|\omega|\) around a real principal \(\omega\ne0\), both factors of \(1-2b\cos z+b^2\) have modulus bounded below by \(c'(1-b+|\omega|)\), with a fixed sufficiently small \(c\). It follows that the interpolated multiplier off zero has \(m\)-th derivative bounded by \(C_m|\omega|^{-m}\). The spike at zero is retained in the low-frequency cell.

For (O.65) use instead \(G(q)=\beta^2\chi_n-S_\beta(q)\). On the selection its grid values satisfy
\[
 0\le G(q)\le C(\beta^{-\epsilon}+|q|^2).
\]
Indeed (O.63) supplies the lower bound on \(S_\beta\), the selected susceptibility and the free rank formula give \(\beta^2\chi_n-S_{G,n}(0)=O(\beta^{-\epsilon})\), and the free second moment in §44 and evenness bound the change of \(S_{G,n}\) by \(C|q|^2\). For fixed transverse momenta, the extension of \(G\) off \(\omega=0\) is the sum of its nonnegative grid value at zero, the possible positive spike mass, and a positive mixture of \(1-\phi_b\). The identity
\[
 1-\phi_b(z)=\frac{(1+b)^2(1-\cos z)}
                   {2(1-2b\cos z+b^2)}
\]
shows that its modulus on the same disk is at most \(C(1-\phi_b(\omega))\). Consequently \(|\partial_\omega^m G|\le C_m |\omega|^{-m}G(\omega)\) for real \(\omega\ne0\). At a nongrid frequency bound \(G(\omega)\) using the next higher \(|\omega|\) on the grid, by monotonicity of the extension. All estimates use one complex variable at a time.

To invert the Fourier series, choose a smooth dyadic partition in each coordinate with bottom width comparable to \(\min(1,A_N/r_i)\) and subsequent widths \(Q_i\) comparable to \(2^{s_i}/r_i\) up to fixed \(N\)-dependent constants. Take \(s_i=0\) for the origin cell. A high cell of width \(Q_i\) has \(|q_i|\) comparable to \(Q_i\), with derivatives of its cutoff bounded by \(C_m Q_i^{-m}\); the final cutoff is periodic across \(\pi\). If \(r_i\) is small use a single bottom cell. For a product cell put \(s=\max s_i\). When \(s>0\), choose an index attaining it and sum by parts \(N\) times in that single grid coordinate. Its high cutoff kills the spike at zero. The \(N\)-fold difference costs \(C_N(n Q_i)^{-N}\), its support has grid fraction \(O_N(Q_i)\), and the periodic Fourier denominator has modulus at least \(4|x_i|/n\) before taking powers. The other coordinates supply their ordinary grid fractions. Choosing fixed \(A_N\) sufficiently large covers the \(N\)-mesh support enlargement even on the smallest high cells. The cell contribution is bounded by
\[
 C_N\Big(\prod_i Q_i\Big)2^{-Ns}
\]
for the susceptibility-normalized multiplier, with the direct bound for \(s=0\). For \(G\) there is the additional factor \(C(\beta^{-\epsilon}+\sum_i Q_i^2)\) from the derivative estimate and the direct bound on the bottom cell. The number of cells with maximum \(s\) is \(O((s+1)^3)\), \(\prod Q_i\le C_N 2^{4s}\prod r_i^{-1}\), and \(Q_i^2\le C_N2^{2s}r_i^{-2}\). Any fixed \(N>6\) suffices to sum these estimates. The inverse transform of the constant \(\beta^2\chi_n\) vanishes for \(x\ne0\), proving (O.65). [Positive Wilson transfer matrix](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01614090) · Fourier and angular proof on the selected sequence

For arbitrary lattice sources this gives a quadratic-form statement about the scalar contact. Parseval applied to the bound on \(G\), and the comparison of \(|q_i|^2\) to \(|e^{iq_i}-1|^2\) in the principal zone, give
\[
 0\le\beta^2[\chi_n\|f\|_2^2-\operatorname{Var}(\mathcal H_f)]
 \le C\beta^{-\epsilon}\|f\|_2^2+
       C\sum_{x,i}|f(x+e_i)-f(x)|^2 .
\]
The estimate is simultaneous in the source on the selected sequence. After the homogeneous contact subtraction it bounds the remaining form by a local gradient form and the stated cutoff-dependent multiple of the identity. For a physical smooth test the latter still costs order \(a^{-4}\beta^{-\epsilon}\) before contact rescaling.

### 46. An admissible uniform radius for the distant-background variation

The radius test in §42 can be verified on a class of irregular native charts. Take a represented field \(\mathbb U=e^{i\eta A'}\bar U\) in LFR I (1.64)–(1.69) with a real representative, fixed relative inner margins, frozen real historical coordinates and fixed determining geometry. On any first-order averaging stencil reaching outside the lowest regular domain, require the real inducing curvature and the represented links to stay in their small regular tube. The physical first-layer collar of B119 (1.10), (2.10) supplies this at the first erasure with the original lower layer retained, including the \(h\ge2\) case of LFR I (1.11)–(1.16).

On that domain there is a raw link and independent-current perturbation radius
\[
 \rho_*\ge c\min(1,\alpha_*)C^{-(k+1)},\qquad
 \alpha_*=\min_{i,m}\widetilde\alpha_{i,m},
 \tag{O.66}
\]
for simultaneous sup-norm directions from the smaller chart into the outer chart, uniformly in its volume. To see this, an order-\(r\) average uses its endpoint blocks, and a plaquette uses the corresponding four blocks. With the intersecting-bond convention an endpoint block may cross the order-\(r\) boundary. The admissible collar contains the stencil at the preceding scale, so the allowed real curvature and raw link radii lose at most factors \(L^2\) and \(L\), respectively. B119 (2.13)–(2.15) uses the local determining order at a trimmed boundary. The first-layer case is precisely the collar assumption above. CMP98 (158)–(164) and its symmetric version in CMP109 then give a common raw disk \(cL^{-k}\) on which the determining averages stay in the regular tube of their real values. CMP102 (172)–(173) and Proposition 9 bound the corresponding projected minimum in Landau gauge. The product returning to the block axial gauge and its inverse have norm at most \(C^{k+1}\), by the local real-reference products in CMP98 (104)–(108), (159)–(163). Hence the projected plaquettes and their local current stencils are bounded by a fixed exponential in \(k\) on the disk. One-variable Cauchy in each simultaneous direction and the fixed relative gaps in (1.64)–(1.69) give (O.66), including the independent-current direction.

B119 (2.28) bounds \(\log(1+\alpha_*^{-1})\) by \(C(1+k+|\log g_{\min}|)\) on the stated weak corridor. Thus the historical budget in §42 pays for these raw radii on the continuously surviving first-layer runs described there. Their distant exterior derivative now follows without an additional radius assumption. This yields a pathwise comparison for the actual first-erasure histories with these collars. Independent complex outer links without a represented collar, varying protected real data, and comparisons of different determining sets require separate estimates. Chart construction and primary references, (C.35)

### 47. Why diagonal reflection does not rotate the bound on the periodic torus

On a lattice cut along \(x_1=x_2\), a crossing Wilson plaquette splits into two reflected length-two paths. Its Boltzmann weight has a positive kernel in their holonomies. This gives diagonal reflection positivity in geometries whose cut actually separates reflected halves with the required boundary identification. The usual local argument is recorded in [JHEP 03 (2025) 099, §3.2](https://doi.org/10.1007/JHEP03(2025)099).

On the even periodic cubic torus of this manuscript there is a global obstruction. The reflection \(\theta(x_1,x_2)=(x_2,x_1)\) fixes the vertices with \(x_1-x_2=0\bmod n\). On the opposite seam \(x_1-x_2=n/2\bmod n\) it translates each vertex by \((n/2,n/2)\). For \(n\ge4\) take a plaquette \(p\) in the spectator directions \(3,4\) on this seam. The plaquettes \(p,\theta p\) are distinct. The real gauge-invariant test \(F=s_p-s_{\theta p}\) is in the algebra of the closed half and satisfies \(\theta F=-F\). Hence \(\langle F\,\theta F\rangle=-\langle F^2\rangle<0\): the finite-coupling density is strictly positive relative to Haar and the test differs from zero on an open set. In a diagonal transfer description the opposite seam inserts the half-period tangential translation in the trace. The positive transition weights used for the coordinate directions do not follow in this geometry. This test addresses the full diagonal positivity argument; it leaves open a direct estimate for the scalar cube alone.

A torus with the first two period vectors \(n(e_1+e_2),n(e_1-e_2)\) has correctly fixed opposite diagonal seams. Transferring a diagonal estimate from that sequence to the cubic sequence would require agreement of the relevant infinite-volume two-cube correlations, or a quantitative boundary comparison. Differentiability of the homogeneous pressure in \(\beta\) controls the one-point energy and supplies neither assertion about the pair correlation. The directional result of §45 therefore uses only the four coordinate reflections on the original torus.

### 48. Two transported sources through an entire successful preliminary history

There is a two-source extension of §§39 and 46 at a first successful erasure. Fix the final admitted field, final external selector, older retained \(T_h,V_h\) data, and all preliminary histories except those of two specified components. On the common successful branch, vary the two *entire* sets of free variables \(y^a=(A_j^a,B_j^a)_{h\le j<k}\) in their actual cutoffs. Include their complete determining corridors in \(S_a\) and let \(D=\operatorname{dist}_{k,M}(S_1,S_2)\). Use the represented smaller charts and bottom collars of §46, with an ordinary prefix carrying the two original first-layer crossing marks as in §30.

On the positive fiber the density of the joint preliminary variables is a product of the successive actual conditional densities at their respective, recomputed backgrounds. Each earlier sharp test is removed by the next successful implication (1.29). The remaining cutoffs (1.9), (1.55) have common product support. Denote by \(p\) the joint density relative to the free-coordinate measures. Then
\[
 |\Delta_1\Delta_2\log p|\le C e^{CH-cD},\qquad
 |K K_{12}-K_1K_2|\le C K^2 e^{CH-cD}. \tag{O.67}
\]
Here the rectangular difference compares two values of each complete array, \(K\) is their true conditional mass, and the subscripts are derivatives of the actual density with respect to the two original sources. There is one historical cost \(H\) for the run.

For a change at owner \(a\), the direct forcing in the minimizing field is supported on \(S_a\). Its derivative at real nonzero admitted fluctuations has exponential decay by CMP102 (190). The independent-base derivative from §§42 and 46 has summable weighted rows. In the norm \(\sup_b e^{\mu d_k(b,S_a)}|\dot v(b)|\), each earlier field substitution therefore costs its own bounded row factor and direct forcing. The product of those factors is at most \(e^{CH}\) on the stated run. Thus the whole change at bond \(b\), including the changed current, costs \(e^{CH-\mu d_k(b,S_a)}\). The old real \(A_i\) occur explicitly on their native carriers. Holding them in the common axial frame avoids differentiating an arbitrary measurable function of them.

For the joint density, restore extracted factors in each source-zero input and output \(W_j,W_{j+1}\). The transition density \(\omega_j W_j/W_{j+1}\) telescopes across adjacent stages on each evaluated array. This keeps the old \(A_j\) precision, the coarse-bond constraint Jacobians and the weights of the retained histories. Their native field-dependent logarithms are localized by their actual carriers or propagator walks; the old precision has the component restriction in B119 (2.19)–(2.22). Twice the smaller of the two one-owner oscillations of each term, summed as in §39, proves the first bound. For the source logarithm \(L\), the incoming term \(\partial_{12}L\) has a carrier containing both anchors. Each \(\partial_a L\) has a replacement by a function of its own array, at error \(O(e^{CH-cD})\), since its rooted carriers must reach the other change or see its exponentially small field variation. Applying the first bound to the normalized covariance of the replacements and retaining the incoming \(\mathbb E\partial_{12}L\) proves the second bound. This is a mixed derivative after integrating both full histories on the specified fiber. Further averaging over other histories also contributes the covariance of their conditional source means. Full joint reconstruction and two-source subtraction, (C.36)–(C.39)

### 49. What remains after conditioning on nested box boundaries

For a fixed aligned tiling by boxes of side \(l\mid n\) with even block grid, let \(S_B\) be the sum of all closed-cube energies whose origins index the block \(B\), and \(Y_B=\mathbb E[S_B\mid\mathcal E_l]\) the mean conditional on all boundary links of the tiling. The closed-cube face weights place all the corresponding plaquettes in the closed block. Conditional independence of its interior gives \(Y_B\) as a function of the boundary of its own block, including the contributions of visible faces. These functions are carried into each other by the coarse coordinate reflections. The block chessboard estimate of §13 now gives, for arbitrary real block weights \(f_B\),
\[
 \operatorname{Var}\sum_B f_B Y_B\le
     l^4\chi_l^{\partial}\sum_B f_B^2,\qquad
 \chi_l^{\partial}=n^{-4}\operatorname{Var}\mathbb E[S\mid\mathcal E_l].
 \tag{O.68}
\]
This is the actual susceptibility of the residual boundary means. For nested tilings it decreases by the law of total variance. Under the quantitative thermal box and susceptibility estimates of §31,
\[
  \beta^2\chi_l^{\partial}
   =\frac{4d_A}{l}-\frac{3d_A}{l^2}+\frac{d_A}{2l^4}
       +O(\beta^{-\epsilon})
\]
at admitted box sizes in the weak window. Indeed the conditional variance block term per lattice site is \(d_Ah_l/(2l^4)+O(\beta^{-1/32})\), where \(h_l=(3l+1)(l-1)^3\), and \(\beta^2\chi_n=A_0+O(\beta^{-\epsilon})\). In the unweighted block sum the conditional action is the true box action up to fixed boundary terms, so its thermal expansion has no linear term at the minimum. The leading \(1/l\) decrease is resolved while \(1/l\) exceeds the error.

There is an exact Fourier description at *every* aligned box size, including larger ones for which that asymptotic has not been shown. Conditional innovations in distinct boxes have zero covariance. Hence the coarse spectrum of \(S_B\) is the spectrum of \(Y_B\) plus the constant \(\mathbb E\operatorname{Var}(S_B\mid\mathcal E_l)\). For a coarse momentum \(P\), write \(w_l(q)=\prod_i|\sin(lq_i/2)/(l\sin(q_i/2))|^2\), using the continuous value at zero. The fine momenta with \(lq=P\bmod 2\pi\) have \(\sum_q w_l(q)=1\). Fourier transformation of the block sums and (O.68) imply
\[
 0\le\sum_{q:\,lq=P}w_l(q)[\beta^2\chi_n-S_\beta(q)]
      \le \beta^2\chi_l^{\partial}. \tag{O.69}
\]
On the primary momentum cell \(|q_i|\le\pi/l\), \(w_l(q)\ge(2/\pi)^8\). Thus the same bound up to a fixed factor holds for its individual deficit. Moreover at separated block times the covariance of \(Y_B\) is exactly that of the true block sums. Their self-adjoint insertion in a slab of thickness \(l\) gives the nonnegative squared transition weights of §24, so (O.64) applies on the block grid to the averaged energy \(S_B/l^4\) at distinct blocks with prefactor \(\chi_l^\partial/l^4\). A single microscopic placement disseminates its own orbit under coarse reflections and has a different susceptibility. Nested conditioning gives the exact decreasing sequence (O.68); further uniform decay past the weak window needs the conditional variance removed at the subsequent scales.

### 50. Replacing retained rough links and integrating the other continuous histories

At a fixed accepted first-erasure geometry the two arrays in §48 may include the terminal rough links \(V_h\), retained continuous \(T_h\) variables and terminal small fluctuation in each of the two owners. Fix the outer coarse data, regional reference, determining sets, gauge graphs and source assignment. In LFR II (1.52)–(1.53) the separated exterior minimum is independent of the changed rough links; the separated interior minima use their respective owner data. Thus the joined reference field and current change only inside the changed owner and its incident stencils. In restoring the true minimum by (1.51), (1.54)–(1.58), a walk can sense this change only when its determining carrier reaches that owner. This argument compares two allowed rough inputs even when they cannot be interpolated.

In subsequent substitutions a localized holomorphic coefficient \(f_Y\) with outer sup bound \(b_Y\) obeys \(|f_Y(v)-f_Y(w)|\le C b_Y\min(1,\rho_*^{-1}\|v-w\|_Y)\) on the two represented real inner inputs with their common local geometry and real arguments. Close endpoints admit a short interpolation on the determining carrier by §46; for distant endpoints the two sup bounds suffice. Summing the weighted rows of these finite differences gives the same spatial profile as in §48 at each reconstructed field. The unmarked measure then has a product one-owner reference with its own hard supports, the original mixed real factors from LFR II (1.72), and the unfolded conditional densities from §48. The terminal selectors are absorbed by their actual owner rules after LFR I (1.89). Mixed terminal factors themselves have the localized estimate (1.68). Hence (O.67) also holds when the two owners' rough and retained continuous data are integrated, with all other owner histories held fixed. Terminal separation, local finite differences and original weight, (C.40)–(C.42)

One can integrate **all** the continuous histories on this fixed geometry under a quantitative bound on the number and sizes of its owners. Here are the sufficient hypotheses and the estimate. Let \(m\) be the number of owners, and let \(S_i\) contain all supports and collars used by owner \(i\). Their ordinary terminal lattice cell multiplicity is bounded, and the sum of the sizes of connected trees spanning them is at most \(CH\). Write \(p\) for the *preliminary conditional-density product* in (C.42), leaving the original mixed terminal weights as separate factors. Assume the common support and inner-chart hypotheses hold on the full Cartesian product of actual one-owner supports, including all intermediate substitutions by references. For this product, retain the estimate \(|\Delta_i\Delta_j\log p|\le C e^{B-c\,\mathrm{dist}(S_i,S_j)}\) uniformly in the other continuous variables. Here \(B\) is the logarithm of the prefactor before the absorption into \(CH\) in §48. The weighted row proof there bounds \(B\) by \(C(1+N)[1+k+|\log g_{\min}|+\log(2+V_*)]\), using §46. Require
\[
       m(1+B)+\sum_i (1+|S_i|_k)\le C H .
\]
This holds for boundedly many owners on the persistent histories of §48 with bounded support inflation. The condition is stated for inspection on larger families. It does not follow merely from persistence of two tagged roots among arbitrarily many recently born owners.

To see the consequence, order the owners and choose one reference in each positive support. Write \(\log p\) as the sum of its one-owner increments relative to those references, turning on the owners in order. In the increment at owner \(i\), first retain only earlier owners at ordinary distance at most \(R_0=\lceil C_0(1+B)\rceil\) from \(S_i\), setting the other earlier owners to reference. Add successive integer distance shells. The term at radius \(R>R_0\) is a sum, by a second telescoping, of rectangular differences between \(i\) and the owners newly included in that shell. Its sup bound is \(C |S_i|_k (1+R)^4 e^{B-cR}\), using bounded cell multiplicity to count newly met owners. Its whole determining set consists of \(i\) and owners within radius \(R\), hence has diameter at most \(2R+O(1)\) in the graph contracting the sets \(S_j\). This produces an exact real density decomposition. No smallness of the initial radius-\(R_0\) term is needed.

In two replicas declare the \(m\) initial neighborhoods connected deterministically and expand each additional shell weight into inactive and positive active parts as in §34. The deterministic hyperedges have total contracted diameter \(O(mR_0)\). For sufficiently small \(b>0\), the sum of the remaining two-copy activation probabilities times \(e^{b(2R+O(1))}-1\) is at most \(C\sum_i |S_i|_k\), by the shell bound and the choice of \(C_0\). The original mixed terminal interactions, still kept separately, have the same exponential moment with their own port cost by (O.51). The positive replica swap and exponential Markov bound now suppress a connection across contracted distance \(D\) by \(\exp(CH-c'D)\). A shortest connection lifts to ordinary distance at cost at most the sum of the spanning-tree sizes above. All those costs are incurred once per geometry.

For two genuinely transported crossing sources, let \(D\) initially be the contracted distance between their anchors. Replace in their separate incoming scores all owners outside their respective \(D/4\) neighborhoods by references. The single-owner replacement bound of §48, summed over at most \(m\) changes, costs \(C e^{CH-c'D}\) in sup norm. Their incoming mixed log term has the same estimate from its two-root carriers. Unless a replica cluster connects the two neighborhoods, swapping both replicas on the union of all clusters touching the first neighborhood cancels the covariance of the local replacements. The same mass-weighted determinant (O.67) consequently holds for the whole fixed-geometry continuous integral under the displayed budget. Arbitrary positive one-owner reference masses, including their hard supports, remain in that integral. Discrete changes to the earlier determining partitions, summing over outer geometries, and transporting the result to the next erasure still require further estimates.

### 51. Changing just the Wilson weights that couple to the box boundaries

The macroscopic residual in §49 can be tested by varying an actual collar of Wilson couplings. Write \(\eta\) for the skeleton links of the fixed box tiling and \(V=n^4\). Choose a fixed-width collar \(\mathcal C\) of closed cubes containing all four incident cube placements of every plaquette that uses a skeleton link. Its size is \(O(V/l)\). Put \(D=\sum_{c\in\mathcal C}H_c,\ B=S-D\). In the two-parameter law proportional to \(e^{-\beta B-tD}\), \(B\) is independent of the skeleton *as a function of the links*. At \(t=0\) the marginal density \(p_{\beta,0}(\eta)\) is identically one relative to product Haar. The diagonal \(t=\beta\) is the original Wilson measure.

Differentiating the two positive finite integrals and integrating in \(t\) gives the following identity at fixed \(\beta\):
\[
 \left.\partial_\beta\log p_{\beta,t}(\eta)\right|_{t=\beta\ {\rm after\ differentiation}}
  =\int_0^\beta
      [\operatorname{Cov}_{\beta,s}(B,D\mid\eta)
                      -\operatorname{Cov}_{\beta,s}(B,D)]\,ds .
 \tag{O.70}
\]
The derivative on the left varies only the coefficient of \(B\). The missing diagonal derivative varies \(D\) and is the negative of its centered conditional mean. In the physical endpoint law its squared \(L^2\) norm is at most \(\operatorname{Var}_\beta D\le C\chi_n V/l\) by (O.15). The squared norm of the full diagonal score is exactly \(V\chi_l^\partial\). Thus an endpoint bound \(O(\sqrt{\chi_n V/l})\) for the right side of (O.70) is sufficient for \(\chi_l^\partial=O(\chi_n/l)\), and is also necessary up to a constant for that rate.

At every \(s\), the conditional law given \(\eta\) has independent box interiors. The conditional covariance in the integrand is a sum of within-box, bulk-to-collar covariances on the actual Dirichlet fibers. Their sum is evaluated with the random skeleton from the physical endpoint. A surface bound for the change in free energy controls the pressure of the interpolation; it does not estimate the squared variation of that centered sum in the endpoint marginal. Even a proof of the stated \(1/l\) residual rate would still require conversion to the full normalized two-point and contact estimates.

### 52. Which discrete histories can be included in the source comparison

Two classes of discrete labels can be summed in §§48 and 50. First, include any owner label that occurs only in its positive reference measure and leaves the determining sets, frames, source placement and successful outer geometry fixed. It is an ordinary discrete integration in that reference. Second, keep the derived domains \(\Omega_i,\Lambda_i\), their covers and interpolants fixed through every stage, and vary earlier switch sets \(S_i\), \(i<h\), within an owner on an admitted Cartesian family. B119 (2.40)–(2.42) makes an old boundary activity depend on \(S_i\cap X\) on its carrier \(X\). The original sourced insertion reads the switch on its own stencil. Each later connected term keeps the union of its input carriers on this fixed partition. Therefore a remote change of switch either leaves the marked term the same or requires a connecting carrier or the background change already bounded in §50. The primitive selector weights stay in the true reference. This verifies the same two-source subtraction after summing these switch alternatives.

For different determining partitions the linear comparison has a published basis. CMP99 (3.87)–(3.89) constructs each propagator ingredient on an enlarged local cube using the indicated portions of the two partitions. Theorem 3.14, (3.154), cancels the walks that stay in a common regular region when subtracting the operators on two domains. The mixed construction has the same primitive cancellation on enlarged stencils where the actual inputs, cutoffs and partitions agree. To compare the full sourced ordinary integrations one must also align the old source eligibility, the free-variable masks, local Gaussian integrals, overlaps, and output carrier assignments. B116 (2.5)–(2.14) and B119 (3.44)–(3.47) give equality for matched rooted terms. Equality of the primitive bad plaquette lists on a neighborhood does not ensure that the later rectangular fills give the same masks there. The general discrete sum still depends on this compatibility and on the original admissibility of the combined histories. Scope of the switch sum and cross-partition comparison

### 53. The contact of a third scalar with two separated bare energies

Write \(H_x=\sum_\alpha\sum_u m_\alpha(u)s_{x+u,\alpha}\), with finite face weights of total one in each plane. Take the lower-vertex placement \(m_\alpha=\delta_0\), the closed cube, or the four-vertex scalar \(H_x=a^4w_a(x)\). For any of these, \(\sum_xH_x=S\). Fix \(y=Re_1\) and \(k_0=3d_A/\pi^4\). On the prescribed weak sequences of §§18–22 one may choose a sufficiently slow \(R\to\infty\), \(aR\to0\), such that for every continuous compactly supported real \(h\),
\[
  R^8\beta^3\sum_z h(z/R)\,
       \kappa_\beta(H_0,H_{Re_1},H_z)
       \ \longrightarrow\ k_0[h(0)+h(e_1)].
 \tag{O.71}
\]
This is a three-source contact with two distinct macroscopic anchors in the magnified coordinates. The leading separated odd scalar cycle vanishes.

In the lattice Gaussian curl model let \(M_x\) multiply by the oriented face weights of \(H_x\), and \(P\) be the curl projection. Wick contraction gives the third cumulant as \(d_A\operatorname{Tr}(P M_0 P M_y P M_z)\); the two cycle orientations agree by transposition. Summing \(M_z\) over \(z\) gives the identity. Consequently the full spectator sum is exactly twice the Gaussian pair covariance by \(P^2=P\). The sum is absolutely convergent at fixed \(R\) because two of its curl legs go to \(z\).

There is uniform absolute summability after the \(R^8\) rescaling. For \(2\le r=|z|\le R/3\), expand each curl entry about the corresponding common site-separation matrix \(K\) of (O.32), including finite face shifts in the \(O(|x|^{-5})\) error. In the leading term the face weights in each orientation sum to one and the trace of the three exterior-square reflections is zero. The remaining absolute rescaled cumulant is bounded by \(C r^{-4}(r^{-1}+R^{-1})\). The region \(S_0\le r\le R/3\) therefore costs \(O(S_0^{-1}+R^{-1}\log R)\), and the same holds near \(y\). Where both distances are comparable to \(R\) on a bounded rescaled region the sum of the subleading terms is \(O(R^{-1})\). Beyond \(|z|\ge M R\), \(M\ge2\), the absolute three-leg bound alone costs \(O(M^{-4})\). These bounds concentrate the Gaussian answer on the two endpoints in a number of lattice spacings that can be sent to infinity *after* the large-\(R\) limit.

The limiting microscopic profile at either endpoint is explicit. At a fixed displacement \(s\) from the endpoint, the two long Gaussian legs compose to \(\pi^{-4}I\) after rescaling, since the square of the exterior-square reflection is the identity. The profile \(b_m(s)\) is the inverse lattice Fourier transform of
\[
  \widehat b_m(p)=
    \frac{d_A}{\pi^4}\sum_{\alpha=(i,j)}
      |\widehat m_\alpha(p)|^2
      \frac{\widehat p_i^{\,2}+\widehat p_j^{\,2}}
           {\sum_\nu\widehat p_\nu^{\,2}},\qquad
       \widehat p_i=2\sin(p_i/2).
\]
It is even in \(s\) for the stated placements. The preceding absolute tail estimate makes it summable, and its total is \(k_0\): each weight equals one at zero and every direction appears in three two-planes. For the lower-vertex placement the symbol is constant, so the entire limiting microscopic contact at either endpoint is \(k_0\mathbf1_{s=0}\). The other two placements spread it according to their actual face weights. Finite-stencil moment convergence through degree six at the prescribed coupling transfers this calculation to the actual Wilson law on an increasing region \(|z|\le R^2\). Choose the tail index for each successive integer \(R\) so that the total rescaled absolute comparison error on this finite region tends to zero, as in §21. The choice is independent of \(h\), and proves (O.71).

In fact the compact-spectator result holds for the whole quantified range \(R\to\infty,\ R\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\) on the §22 geometry. To check the additional moment estimate, retain the boxes \(l\asymp\beta^\epsilon\). In a product of six scaled plaquette matrices delete each conditional mean displacement with its \(L^2\) bound \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon/6})\) and the uniform \(L^{10}\) bounds for the other five factors from the local energy moments.
