On good boxes the centered plaquette Taylor remainder is \(O(\beta^{-1/2}|y|^2)\); every fixed Gaussian norm moment of \(|y|\) is bounded by \(C_m l^{12}\) in \(L^m\) by (36) of the heat-bath note. The relative thermal-density error on the truncation is \(O(\beta^{-1/3})\) and the discarded tails beat any fixed polynomial. Replacing the covariance square root costs at most \(Cl^{30}\|G_*-G\|\le C l^{48}\beta^{-1/4}\) in operator norm, as in §26. These bounds in a six-factor product cost at most \(C l^{200}\beta^{-1/4}+C l^{100}\beta^{-1/3}\), allowing also the errors of its centering constants and using the Gaussian moment bounds before expanding the product. Conditional independence applies to different boxes. In the resulting Wick products the averaged difference of each box curl covariance entry from the infinite one is \(O(1/l)\) by §22; all such entries are uniformly bounded. Consequently the absolute error of each scaled scalar third cumulant is \(O(\beta^{-\epsilon/6})\), uniformly for triples in the indicated region. There are \(O(R^8)\) possible spectators with \(|z|\le R^2\). Even after the \(R^8\) amplitude factor their total error is \(O(R^{16}\beta^{-\epsilon/6})=o(1)\). This also transfers the two endpoint profiles in total absolute error on that growing region.

A quantitative version of this last statement has total error \(O(R^{-1/2}+R^{16}\beta^{-\epsilon/6})\) on \(|z|\le R^2\) for replacing the scaled cumulant by \(b_m(z)+b_m(z-y)\). In the Gaussian comparison, within \(S_0=\sqrt R\) of either endpoint the two scaled long legs differ from their limiting matrices by \(O((1+r)/R)\), giving summed error \(O(S_0/R)\) against the short leg of size \(O((1+r)^{-4})\). The unweighted microscopic profile has absolute tail \(O(1/S_0)\) by the preceding bound after taking the large-\(R\) limit. For the true Gaussian cycle between \(S_0\) and \(R/3\) use the absolute estimate above. Outside both endpoint regions and within \(2R\) the odd continuum cancellation gives total \(O(R^{-1})\). Beyond \(2R\) the same cancellation bounds the scaled lattice remainder by \(C R^4|z|^{-8}(R^{-1}+|z|^{-1})\), again summable to \(O(R^{-1})\). This describes the two ultraviolet contacts in microscopic coordinates as well as their limit in (O.71).

The unrestricted Wilson spectator sum can also be tested, with an additional coupling average. For each compatible \(n\) hold \(R,n\) fixed while integrating \(\log\beta\) from \(\log b\) to \(\log b+d_0\), with \(d_0>0\) fixed. The same slow diagonal works uniformly there as \(b\to\infty\) under the uniform local moment hypotheses. Differentiation of the exact Wilson measure gives
\[
 \partial_{\log\beta}[\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}_\beta(H_0,H_y)]
   =2\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}_\beta(H_0,H_y)
           -\beta^3\sum_z\kappa_\beta(H_0,H_y,H_z).
\]
The normalized two-point term \(R^8\beta^2\operatorname{Cov}(H_0,H_y)\) tends uniformly to \(k_0\) on the interval. Integrating the identity yields \(2k_0\) for the limit of the **signed, unrestricted, logarithmically averaged** rescaled spectator sum. Thus the two contacts exhaust its leading averaged value. A pointwise bound on the remote spectator tail and the nonzero interacting coefficient with all three insertions separated remain open.

### 54. A dynamical sufficient condition for the physical box-boundary estimate

There is an alternative exact criterion for §51 on the original compact Wilson measure. Let \(L=\sum_b L_b\) be the positive reversible link Langevin generator, \(L_b=-\Delta_b+\beta\nabla_bS\cdot\nabla_b\), and set \(f=\beta(S-\mathbb ES)\). At finite volume solve \(Lu=f\) with zero mean. Compact ellipticity gives a smooth solution; the estimate below concerns its norm uniformly as the volume and cutoff change. Integration by parts on each hidden link gives \(\mathbb E[L_bu\mid\eta]=0\) for every hidden \(b\). Hence the physical boundary score is the projection of \(\sum_{b\ {\rm visible}}L_bu\).

For distinct links the derivatives commute, so another integration by parts gives
\[
 \langle L_bu,L_cu\rangle=
     \mathbb E\|\nabla_b\nabla_cu\|^2+
     \beta\,\mathbb E[\operatorname{Hess}_{bc} S(\nabla_bu,\nabla_cu)] .
\]
The mixed Hessian vanishes unless the links share a Wilson plaquette, and is bounded uniformly on such pairs by compactness. Summing the negative parts of these inner products over ordered pairs therefore costs \(C\beta\,\mathbb E\sum_b|\nabla_bu|^2=C\beta\langle f,L^{-1}f\rangle\). Now average over translated, rotated copies of the skeleton, whose common single-link density is \(\alpha_l=O(1/l)\). Each positive pair is retained with probability at most \(\alpha_l\); the sum over all pairs before masking is \(\|f\|^2\). Projection onto the visible sigma field contracts the norm, and by symmetry all the translated and rotated skeleton norms are equal. This proves
\[
 \beta^2\chi_l^\partial\le
 \alpha_l\left(\beta^2\chi_n+
                  \frac{C\beta}{V}\langle f,L^{-1}f\rangle\right).
 \tag{O.72}
\]
The extra quantity is the integrated Langevin autocorrelation of the total energy times \(\beta/V\). In the Gaussian transverse model its value per this normalization is proportional to \(V^{-1}\sum_{p\ne0}|\widehat p|^{-2}\), which is bounded in four dimensions. Uniform control for the Wilson energy on the selected trajectory remains an additional input. It concerns the inverse moment of the spectral measure of one specified observable. Full-law Poisson identity and mixed-link estimate

### 55. The part of the Wilson energy autocorrelation controlled by weak boxes

For \(A=L/\beta\), \(f=\beta(S-\mathbb ES)\) and \(e=\beta^{-\epsilon}\), on the selected sequences of §31 the actual finite-volume generator satisfies
\[
 V^{-1}\langle f,(A+e)^{-1} f\rangle\le C,\qquad
 V^{-1}\|{\bf1}_{[0,u]}(A)f\|_2^2\le C(u+e).
 \tag{O.73}
\]
In particular the integral of \(s^{-1}\) against the energy spectral measure per unit volume on \([e,\infty)\) is uniformly bounded. This is a dynamical estimate for the original compact link law. The generator describes artificial Langevin time; the transfer energies of §24 describe Euclidean lattice time and have a different spectral measure.

In each good conditional box of side \(l\asymp e^{-1}\), let \(x\) be the displacement from the minimum in its free tree-gauge logarithms, and take the vector field \(X=\phi(x)x/2\) with a smooth cutoff on the thermal ball of §22. Its conditional divergence score \(g_i=\beta X\cdot\partial S_i-J^{-1}\operatorname{div}(JX)\), with the actual Haar-coordinate Jacobian \(J\), has conditional mean zero. Set this score to zero on a bad boundary. The radial derivative matches the conditional quadratic energy minus half the dimension. The cubic Taylor and log-Jacobian terms are small on the thermal ball; the region where the cutoff changes and the bad boundaries have superpolynomially small probability with the required moments. Conditional independence and (O.68) thus give \(\|f-g\|_2^2\le CeV\) for \(g=\sum_i g_i\).

For its inverse norm, first remove the flat vertex gradient from the zero-extended tree-coordinate vector in the box. On the complement of relative vertex gradients the flat covariance trace is \(O(l^4)\): the relative one-form Laplacian in component \(j\) has eigenvalues \(4\sum_i\sin^2(\pi k_i/(2l))\) with \(0\le k_j<l\), \(1\le k_i<l\) for \(i\ne j\). The inverse sum is bounded in four dimensions after division by \(l^4\). Subtract the *actual infinitesimal vertex gauge variation* with the same vertex potential, zero on the boundary. This leaves the pairing with invariant test functions unchanged. The Dirichlet bound on the vertex potential costs at most \(Cl|x|\); the difference between the true and flat gradient maps also carries the small factor \(O(l^{18}\beta^{-1/4}+\beta^{-1/2+1/32})\) on the support of \(X\). The weak-box density and covariance estimates then give an expected squared norm \(O(l^4/\beta)\) in the original link metric. The boxes have disjoint hidden-link supports. Integration by parts against an invariant test and Cauchy–Schwarz give \(\langle g,A^{-1}g\rangle\le CV\); gauge averaging extends the dual bound to arbitrary tests. The residual squared norm costs \(CeV\), paid for by \(\|(A+e)^{-1}\|\le e^{-1}\), proving (O.73).

The unresolved part of the sufficient condition (O.72) is now precisely the inverse spectral moment over \(0<s<\beta^{-\epsilon}\). The centered scalar excludes nontrivial gauge and center-character sectors by symmetry. Center-neutral observables depending on torus holonomies can still have spectral weight there. Controlling the mass of that interval alone does not control its inverse moment. Original-link divergence construction and relative Hodge estimate, (64)–(65)

The energy spectrum also has a finite-frequency limit at the *prescribed* coupling. With normalized Brillouin measure \(dp\), put \(\omega(p)=4\sum_i\sin^2(p_i/2)\), \(\nu_G=(3d_A/2)(2\omega)_*dp\) and \(\nu_\beta(ds)=V^{-1}\langle f,{\bf1}_{ds}(A)f\rangle\), where the latter notation denotes the spectral measure. Then \(s\,d\nu_\beta\) converges weakly to \(s\,d\nu_G\) on every prescribed weak sequence of §22. Indeed for each positive integer \(m\), integration by parts expresses \(\langle f,A^m f\rangle\) as a sum of local expectations \(\beta^{-1}\langle\nabla_b f,\nabla_b A^{m-1}f\rangle\) supported in stencils of fixed size depending on \(m\). Taking the link derivatives before evaluating in a common local gauge, the first scaled derivative of \(\beta s_p\) tends to the incidence derivative of the quadratic curl energy, its second to the incidence Gram matrix, and the further derivatives go to zero. The fixed-stencil Wilson moment limit gives the Gaussian value for each of these moments. Positivity, a higher moment for tightness, and the compact support of the limiting measure determine the weak limit. On the susceptibility selection the zeroth moment converges as well.

The same box divergence then gives the actual limit
\[
 \frac1V\langle f,(A+\tau)^{-1}f\rangle
 \ \longrightarrow\ \frac{3d_A}{4}\int_{\mathrm{BZ}}\frac{dp}{\omega(p)}
 \quad\text{if }\tau\downarrow0,\quad e/\tau\to0
 \tag{O.74}
\]
on that selection. For the upper bound keep the leading relative Hodge trace in the proof of (O.73): after removing the exact gradients it is the four one-form sums minus the vertex Dirichlet sum, converging after volume normalization to \(3\int dp/\omega\). The dilation \(x/2\) gives the factor \(1/4\), and the corrected squared link cost has vanishing relative error. The residual inverse norm costs \(O(e/\tau)\) and its cross term tends to zero by Cauchy–Schwarz. A lower bound follows from the positive finite-frequency limit on compact intervals and then their exhaustion. Formula (O.74) identifies the finite-frequency contribution while leaving the unregularized lower tail in (O.72) open. Moment argument and sharp regularized limit, (66)–(67)

### 56. Which measurements on the physical box boundaries already satisfy the surface bound

For any deterministic set \(\mathcal D\) of visible plaquettes let \(\Pi_{\mathcal D}\) be the orthogonal projection onto the linear span of their centered individual Wilson energies. For an interval \(I\) of normalized Langevin frequencies write \(f_I={\bf1}_I(A)f\). At the prescribed weak couplings,
\[
 V^{-1}\|\Pi_{\mathcal D}f_I\|^2
       \le C\,\frac{|\mathcal D|}{V}\nu_\beta(I)^2. \tag{O.75}
\]
For the physical-size box skeleton on the susceptibility selection this bounds the linear projection of the full energy by \(C/l\) per unit volume, and the projection of its part below \(u\) by \(C(\beta^{-\epsilon}+u)^2/l\). It requires no bound on the inverse moment below \(\beta^{-\epsilon}\).

Indeed the actual plaquette Gram matrix has a volume-uniform lower bound
\(\operatorname{Var}(\beta\sum_p h_ps_p)\ge c\sum_p|h_p|^2\).
Use a translated tiling of fixed side at least six. On each good box, the quadratic Gaussian comparison after projecting off constants and linear terms (§44) is uniform for weights on the finite-dimensional unit sphere. In the flat relative complex the quadratic variance is proportional to \(\|P M_h P\|_{\rm HS}^2\), which bounds \(\|d^*M_h d\|_{\rm HS}^2/\|d\|^4\) from below. For any deep plaquette \(p\), two of its opposite parallel links belong together to exactly that plaquette; their matrix entry is \(\pm h_p\). The distinct pairs, the uniform chance under translation that \(p\) is deep, and the vanishing bad-box error give the Gram bound after averaging. Translations and coordinate permutations preserve \(f_I\). Since the sum of centered scaled plaquettes is \(f\), each has covariance \(\nu_\beta(I)/6\) with \(f_I\). Duality on \(\mathcal D\) proves (O.75).

An exact nonlinear part of the boundary projection can also be isolated. Choose a set \(W\) of visible links tangent to two intersecting skeleton planes and contained in their intersection, with no plaquette touching two links in \(W\). Every plaquette incident to such a link is wholly visible. Given the other skeleton links, the original one-link conditionals for \(W\) are independent of each other and of the hidden interiors. Therefore the difference between the conditional means of \(f\) on the full skeleton and on the skeleton without \(W\) is exactly \(\beta\sum_{b\in W}(S_b-\mathbb E_b S_b)\), where \(S_b\) sums incident plaquette energies and \(\mathbb E_b\) is the original one-link conditional. Its squared norm is at most \(C|W|\) by conditional independence and the uniform local energy moments. This controls all square-integrable tests of the visible variables with zero conditional mean after these links are removed. On a single plane away from intersections a normal plaquette contains hidden links; its staple then affects the one-link law. The general nonlinear score on the ordinary faces, including holonomies using many boundary links, remains unestimated at the required rate. Plaquette Gram bound and projection on the Wilson energy spectrum

In fact these intersecting-plane fluctuations give a lower bound for the *original* residual susceptibility at all compatible box sizes. Take the positive 3-direction links with first and second coordinates divisible by \(l\), and select one parity of the sum of their three transverse coordinates. No plaquette contains two such selected links, and for even tori and \(l\ge2\) their number is comparable to \(V/l^2\). By the single-star conditional weak limit of §3, the expected squared contribution per link tends to \(d_A/2\), uniformly in its position. The normalized squared difference of the two boundary projections in the preceding paragraph is their sum, so on the prescribed compatible weak sequence
\[
             \beta^2\chi_l^\partial\ge c_N/l^2 .
\]
Thus a faster-than-inverse-square target for the *entire raw skeleton susceptibility* would exclude actual local Wilson fluctuations already present where its planes intersect. Their contribution has to be respected when using block means to estimate a separated covariance.

The intermediate-distance time-slice calculation shows why testing individual visible energies misses part of the actual response. Put the closed scalar cube at distance \(R\) from the near spatial cut of §25 and project its centered, \(\beta\)-scaled value onto the span of all individual spatial plaquette energies in a cut-plane patch of radius \(R^2\) around the normal footpoint. The Gram lower bound above gives a projection norm squared at most \(C\) times the sum of squared covariances with these scaled individual energies. By (O.33) each covariance is bounded by \(C[(1+R+|z|)^{-8}+\beta^{-\epsilon/6}]\) at spatial displacement \(z\), including the finite face placements. Summing over this three-dimensional patch gives
\[
 \|\Pi_{\rm patch}\,\beta(H_R-\mathbb EH_R)\|_2^2
       \le C(R^{-13}+R^6\beta^{-\epsilon/3}).
\]
For \(1\ll R\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\) this is \(O(R^{-13})\), whereas the squared response to the full cut is asymptotic to a positive constant times \(R^{-8}\) by (O.37). The individual-plaquette linear span on this growing patch therefore sees a vanishing fraction of that response. The successful near-plane tests of §26 contain products of fluxes from different plaquettes in a common transported frame. This comparison is for a localized bulk cube on the ultraviolet interval; (O.75) estimates the separate total-energy boundary projection at physical box sizes.

### 57. A source comparison across locally compatible changes of partition

The switch comparison of §52 extends to derived partitions on a specified joint family. Use a fixed grid, common post-erasure data, the same ordinary prefix with no previous erasure, and an actual Cartesian family of successful histories. When replacing one owner's discrete labels require its derived masks and coordinate sets to change only inside that owner and the native incident collars. Require the original source eligibility, common exterior frame, controlled first-layer collars, and admitted real inner charts on every combined history. Test these conditions after the rectangular fills.

For this existence construction fix one local choice of the spatial Wilson cutoff in B119 (1.27), (2.24)–(2.25). Smooth the indicator of the part of its domain at distance at least \(2\delta\) from the complement with a fixed nonnegative kernel of radius \(\delta\), a small fixed fraction of the native collar width. This is supported inside the domain and equals one on its required shrinkage; its value reads only a \(3\delta\) neighborhood. The change to the spatial Wilson coefficient is canceled pointwise by its partner in the renormalized regular action. B119 fixes the homogeneous running coefficient separately. Thus this prescription preserves the physical density and the scalar coupling used in this comparison. Keep local overlap functions and parent covers consistent and make any whole-component completion depend only on its own data. This is one allowed prescription; individual boundary summands may differ in a different splitting.

On these hypotheses the equality of the sourced coefficients far from a changed owner follows by induction. A marked birth reads its actual localized stencil. Each ordinary step uses the matched elementary Dirichlet walks of §52, their nonlinear solution on the visited region, the same local free variables and Gaussian integrals, and the same rooted cluster exclusions and parent carriers. The additional neighborhood for reading geometry through age \(i\) has width \(C\sum_{j\le i}MR_j L^{j-k}\) in terminal units. A term that fails to match has a carrier reaching that neighborhood of the change; its original exponential bound pays for that distance. Changes in the number of independent Gaussian variables also change their absolute normalization. Pad absent coordinates of the gapped precision by the identity and use the integral resolvent difference for the diagonal of its logarithm. The two uniformly localized resolvents contribute an integrable \((1+t)^{-2}\) factor and must reach the difference of the two precision kernels; the dimension term is a sum of one-bond constants. For a changed separating mask in the terminal reconstruction, start from the shared outer field and use (1.58), (1.57) before pasting the rough interior at (1.53); the exterior intermediate field may itself respond. The local finite-difference estimate in §50 propagates that response.

The conclusion (O.67) survives on this family with the ordinary point-distance cost \(B\) from §50 replaced by \(B+C(1+N)R_k\). The two-source shell argument integrates all the allowed owner histories if its original budget holds with this larger cost, uniformly on every supported combination. The native terminal reserve pays the added \(O((1+N)R_k)\) per eligible owner; the underlying budget involving \(B\), the compatibility of the hybrid histories, and the final exterior average are still substantive requirements. Local splitting and absolute Gaussian normalizers · Rooted comparison through the varying derived masks

### 58. The normalized source disk on native rectangular erasure components

For the ordinary first-erasure geometry there is a stronger way to check the row condition (O.53), using the precise tree in LFR II (1.67)–(1.69). Work on a cofinal torus large enough to lift each eligible rectangular component \(X_i\) and its one-layer collar into a box. The components are unions of terminal \(MR_k\)-cubes and have diameter at most \(C MR_k\) by B119 (2.3) and LFR I p.177. Take the actual one-owner terminal incidence of LFR II pp.376–379. A mixed carrier \(Y\) includes the **whole enlarged component** of every owner it touches. The tree in (1.67) has ordinary geometric length and must meet every \(M\)-cube of \(Y\setminus Z\), including those between \(Z\) and its one-\(M\)-layer enlargement. The remainder in (1.68) is tested on \(Y\setminus\widetilde Z\). These are distinct complements.

In \(M\)-cube units, one exterior face strip of each incident rectangle already contains at least \(cR_k^3\) cubes. Across an exposed coarse face the neighboring \(MR_k\)-cell cannot belong to a different component of \(Z\), since a shared face would connect them. Each exterior face cube is counted with bounded multiplicity across the incident owners, by its bounded number of adjacent interior cells. A tree of length \(t\) meets at most \(C(1+t)\) different unit cubes, as follows by traversing the tree in both directions and covering the path in unit segments. Thus the actual \(t=d_{k,Z}(Y)\) pays at least \(cR_k^3-C\) for any mixed carrier, and \(C(1+t)\) also bounds the sum of its incident owner diameters. Using this gain in the rooted count of §37 gives
\[
 \sup_i\sum_{Y\ni i}(4e)^{|I_Y|}e^{b t_Y}
                 \|e^{V_Y}-1\|_\infty
       \le C\alpha(1+R_k)^4 e^{-c R_k^3} . \tag{O.76}
\]
Here \(b>0\) uses a small fraction of the available native tree exponent, with the same entropy reserve as in §37. The \((1+R_k)^4\) factor counts possible starting ports. Smaller \(R_k\) are covered by the admitted small \(\alpha\). In particular the normalized zero-free disk of §37 and its factorial bounds for genuinely locally owned sources hold on these native geometries with arbitrarily many components. No additional spacing assumption on the owners is needed for this conclusion.

Furthermore, fix one ordinary lattice location in each distinct marked owner. Choose one exterior port on an exposed face of each owner; every incident tree meets its port cube. Join successive trees through that port at bounded cost and connect each marked location to it at cost \(O(1+\operatorname{diam}X_i)\). The preceding count pays for all these additions from the incident edge lengths. Consequently connected jets involving at least two distinct owners have exponential decay in the **ordinary** tree distance between the chosen representative locations, with a reduced fixed rate, in the normalized fixed-exterior law. If arbitrarily many distinct marks occupy the same owner this statement uses one representative of that owner. Its own internal fluctuations and the outside distribution retain their separate roles. For a source not localized to an owner, the transport estimates in §§48–57 are still needed before applying such a source disk. Native component bounds and the ordinary tree definition

### 59. Keeping a successful tagged component unintegrated

At each real successful preliminary step Fubini permits integrating the untagged components first, with the tagged free variables fixed. The conditional Gaussian part is exact: if \(E\) denotes the integrated variables and \(T\) the retained ones, its mean shift is \(-K_{EE}^{-1}K_{ET}b_T\) and the remaining precision is \(K_{TT}-K_{TE}K_{EE}^{-1}K_{ET}\). Keep the marginal weight and the original cutoffs. CMP99 following (3.158) gives a positive gap and exponential rows for the current-scale independent precision. Thus on geometries with a full \(MR_k\) gap between the determining supports, \(|(K_{ET}b_T)(e)|\le C p_j e^{-c L^{k-j}MR_k}\) on \(E\), using a valid sup bound \(p_j\) for the retained coordinate. Summing the current-unit lattice row incurs no factor for the number of owners. The original hard cutoffs need not be translated to make this estimate. This controls a genuinely mixed quadratic source, while the interaction at the new background and the later tagged cutoffs still depend on how the field is represented.

For a real semi-local comparison a changed hard success indicator has a quantitative bound *after original integration*. On the common successful parent let \(r_j\) be the actual free-coordinate radius in LFR I (1.55), and let \(D_j\) bound the radial derivative of the smooth conditional log density in a tagged bond on its ball. A replacement for the success-test center at raw distance at most \(t_j\) can use the reduced radius \(r_j-C t_j\). The lost region on the accepted chart is in an inner annulus of thickness \(C't_j\). Comparing its radial integrals with interior intervals of length \(\min(r_j/2,(1+D_j)^{-1})\), including the radial volume factor in the fixed Lie-algebra dimension, gives a conditional probability at most \(C t_j(D_j+1+d_A/r_j)\), capped at one. This uses the native radius in the coordinates of the printed selector, separately from any conservative upper bound \(p_j\) used in the precision estimate. With bounded real marks the absolute unnormalized loss is bounded by the true parent mass times the sum of these bounds over tested bonds and steps, times the mark sup bound. Integrate the earlier normalized kernels to one before comparing each annulus.

At the first step the physical tagged link and all future determining data may be fixed so that its (1.55) test is independent of the current untagged integration. At the next step the old center uses the newly integrated variables. A fixed raw group link relative to an arbitrary independently continued background need not lie in the same small chart, even if the background is flat. The annulus estimate supplies an integrated real error; using the full unmarked analytic erasure theorem with arbitrary successful tags still requires a compatible covariant representation of that source and the old tests at successive independent backgrounds. Original success sheets, marginal weights and annular comparison

### 60. A second integration with a fixed raw source in the interior of its cutoff

Fix the real data after step \(h+1\) on one admitted successful geometry. Keep the tagged determining links at both steps and the next tagged innovations fixed. Assume a full ordinary terminal \(MR_k\) gap from the untagged variables to the averaging stencils of the tagged links. LFR I (1.56)–(1.58) compare the center of the original \(h\)-test with its regional center uniformly in the allowed real untagged innovations. If the tagged regional logarithms are at distance greater than \(C t_h\) from the edge of the success ball, the old tests are identically one over this next real integration. Here \(t_h\) bounds the shift in the coordinates of the printed (1.55), so the physical link displacement is divided by \(g_h\). The accepted mass omitted by this restriction has the annular bound in §59 with the actual conditional density.

For the smooth factors one can continue in the untagged \(h+1\) fluctuations alone. Let \(z\) denote these variables, \(V_c(z)\) the old tagged link center obtained by the actual next reconstruction and average, and \(c^0=(ig_h)^{-1}\log(V_TV_c(0)^{-1})\) at the fixed raw link \(V_T\). In the original \(h\)-integrand insert
\[
 b_{h,T}(z)=(ig_h)^{-1}\log\!\left(e^{ig_h c^0}V_c(0)V_c(z)^{-1}\right).
 \tag{O.77}
\]
The baseline and the real integration cube must be admitted at the common later background. CMP102, Proposition 9 (190), applied along the complex segment from zero to \(z\), bounds the dimensionless change of the averaged link by \(C\epsilon_{h+1} L^{4(k-h-1)}e^{-cMR_k}\). The scale factor of the output cancels in the native axial average. The displayed power bounds the number of source cells per terminal cell before the exponentially decaying row is summed. Require this bound to fit the old disk margin and the real tags to lie inside that disk. The exponential-chart Haar factors then transform by the analytic ratio of the two Jacobians, by right invariance on the real section. The bounded measurable raw insertion is constant in \(z\). Integrating the remaining smooth old density over its original untagged balls gives a holomorphic function of these fluctuations at this fixed background. Its real values give the second partial integration with the actual normalization. Two-level substitution and the original Haar factor, (72)–(73)

For a fixed number of tagged owners the omitted mass can be made small on the original all-success law at these two steps. Put \(x_j=\log g_j^{-2}\). Read literally, the success radius in (1.55) after (1.53) is \(r_j=\delta'_j=g_j A_1x_j^{p_1}\). The weaker line of (1.57) already gives \(t_j/r_j\le C\epsilon_j x_j^{-p_1}\exp(x_j-R_j)\), small uniformly at a sufficiently weak ceiling with \(R_j\ge x_j^r\), \(r>1\). The density in this ball also matters. On the original preintegration step, holding the real old variables fixed, take the derivative of its smooth *log density* in a current fluctuation bond. The prefactor outside the integral and the zero-fluctuation terms have zero derivative. The gapped Gaussian has an exponentially summable row. For the remaining source-free fluctuation terms use the localized preintegration expansion of CMP116 (1.41)–(1.43), (2.18)–(2.20), as applied in CMP119 (3.43) and LFR I (1.60). Only expanded terms containing the varied bond contribute. Bound the quadratic part by its summable row at the physical cutoff and differentiate the bounded part by Cauchy in that one fluctuation coordinate on its native margin. A carrier counted at scale \(m\) can cover at most \(CL^{4(m-j)}\) current cubes per \(m\)-cube, with \(j\le m\le k\). The sum over carriers rooted at the bond has the remaining tree decay. There is no earlier preliminary activity at \(h\), and the single original activity at \(h+1\) uses the overlap bound on LFR I pp. 191–192. This yields a radial slope bounded by a fixed polynomial in cutoff parameters, \(g_j^{-1}\) and \(1+k-h\), times \(e^{C(k-j)}\) on these admitted original fluctuation disks.

Each tested rectangular owner contributes at most \(CR_k^4 L^{4(k-j)}\) tagged free bonds. In the stronger line of (1.57), the exponential of \(-cM(R_k+\sum_{i=j+1}^{k-1}R_i)\) pays for this count, the slope and the inverse radius on the comparable weak corridor, leaving a positive fraction of the decay by increasing the fixed \(M\) and decreasing the weak ceiling. The union bound of §59 thus gives a small fraction of the original successful mass for a fixed number of tags, uniformly in the total depth. At a fixed weak endpoint the fraction is small at that endpoint and need not tend to zero with depth. This estimate averages over the raw tags with the original kernels. Conditioning on a specified raw tag on the cutoff face changes that question.

A passive real gauge preserves a tagged microscopic Wilson plaquette when it fixes the tails of its frozen algebra variables and the endpoints of its frozen group links, with the corresponding block centers pinned for projected stencils. In the loop product the internal transformations telescope and the trace removes the basepoint conjugation. The earlier success test also survives this change of frame exactly: the relative raw link and its varying center have the same gauge-invariant distance. Thus the moving boundary is a physical condition on the later data. A localized output valid over the independent background domain and an iteration controlling the losses through the whole history remain open. Gauge identity for the microscopic insertion and its pins

### 61. What Fourier agreement and coordinate reflection positivity allow at physical distance

On the selected Wilson sequence let \(S_\beta\) be the multiplier of the centered, \(\beta\)-scaled cube and \(A_\beta=S_\beta(0)\to3d_A/2\). Choose an integer physical-distance separation \(R\asymp a^{-1}\ll n\), \(b=e^{-1/R}\), and \(d=e^{-\sqrt\beta}\). Consider the auxiliary scalar field with multiplier
\[
 S_*(q)=(1-d)S_\beta(q)+\frac{d A_\beta}{4}
       \sum_{i=1}^4\frac{(1-b)^2}{1-2b\cos q_i+b^2}.
 \tag{O.78}
\]
It is realized by scaling the physical centered cube by \(\sqrt{1-d}\) and adding four independent centered Gaussian fields, one for each lattice direction. The field for direction \(i\) consists of independent periodic Gaussian chains on the parallel lattice lines. Its precision on a line is a positive multiple of \((1+b^2)I-b(T+T^*)\), where \(T\) is translation by one. In its own direction the transition kernel is a Gaussian convolution between positive one-site weights and hence is a positive operator. In each transverse direction different slices are independent. The resulting scalar law has the same hypercubic symmetries and coordinate reflection positivity used for the cube. Each summand in the normalized momentum average lies between zero and one and equals one at zero. Consequently \(S_*(0)=A_\beta\), \(0\le S_*\le A_\beta\), and \(\|S_*-S_\beta\|_\infty\le d A_\beta\). This perturbation is smaller than every fixed inverse power of \(\beta\). Fixed-order moments at finitely many sites have the same accuracy up to a constant depending on the order, by independence, centering, the Gaussian moment formula and the bounded scaled Wilson moments.

At displacement \(Re_i\) the Wilson contribution is nonnegative by its slab transfer representation. The added contribution from the \(i\)-chain alone is
\[
 \frac{d A_\beta}{4}\frac{1-b}{1+b}
             \frac{b^R+b^{n-R}}{1-b^n}\ \ge\ c\,d/R.
\]
This follows by periodizing the inverse Fourier coefficients of the one-dimensional kernel. The other three added fields have zero covariance across this displacement. On the matched weak trajectory \(\log R\asymp\beta\), so this lower bound exceeds \(R^{-8}\) even with any fixed power of \(\log R\). Thus the scalar susceptibility, coordinate transfer positivity, and even uniform Fourier comparison to better than every algebraic order leave room for a physical long-distance contribution. This example modifies the scalar law. A stronger conclusion for the actual Wilson cube must use information that distinguishes its conditional law or some comparably strong physical input. The time-slice variance in §25 tests precisely that contribution.

### 62. The response to all exterior links around a microscopic Wilson insertion

Center a box of radius \(r\) lattice spacings at the cube insertion. Reveal every link except the links strictly inside the box, and denote this exterior by \(\mathcal E_r\). On the prescribed weak sequences of §22 put \(F=\beta(H_0-\mathbb E H_0)\). For \(1\ll r\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\) there are constants independent of the volume, \(r\) and \(\beta\) such that
\[
       c r^{-8}\ \le\ \|\mathbb E[F\mid\mathcal E_r]\|_2^2
                    \ \le\ C r^{-8}. \tag{O.79}
\]
The upper bound includes arbitrary nonlinear measurements of the complete exterior. For the lower bound put one spatial cut just outside the box at distance \(r+O(1)\) and its partner half a torus away. Both are visible. Their projection has squared norm asymptotic to \((3d_A/\pi^4)(2r+O(1))^{-8}\) by (O.33), (O.36). Conditioning on the full exterior can only increase this squared norm.

For the upper bound use a smooth vector field \(X\) of the interior group links, in left-invariant link coordinates. The compact Haar integration by parts gives the exact Wilson identity
\[
 k_X=-\operatorname{div}X+\beta X\!\cdot\!\nabla S,
 \qquad \mathbb E[k_X\mid\mathcal E_r]=0,\qquad
 \|\mathbb E[F\mid\mathcal E_r]\|_2^2\le\mathbb E|F-k_X|^2 .
 \tag{O.80}
\]
For each cube plaquette \(p\), truncate \(d^*\Delta^{-1}e_p\) on the infinite lattice by a cutoff supported strictly inside the box, equal to one on its inner half. Denote this link array by \(v_p\), set \(w_p=dv_p\), and write \(P\) for the infinite curl projection. The lattice Green estimates give \(\|P e_p-w_p\|_2^2\le C r^{-4}\), \(\|v_p\|_1\le Cr\) and \(\|w_p\|_1\le C\log(2+r)\). Indeed the uncut link field decays as distance to the inverse third power and its curl as the inverse fourth. The derivative of the cutoff costs \(C/r\) on an annulus of volume \(O(r^4)\).

Transport the scaled traceless skew plaquettes \(Y_q=\sqrt\beta\,\Pi_{\mathfrak{su}(N)}(U_q-I)\) to a common point by paths of length \(O(r)\) inside the box. Set \(\xi_p=\sum_q w_p(q)Y_q\). On link \(b\) sum \(\beta^{-1/2}v_p(b)(Y_p-\xi_p/2)\) with the actual cube face weights \(a_p\), transporting each velocity back to the initial vertex of \(b\). This gives a polynomial in the original link entries and their conjugates. In the Gaussian comparison the action drift for one color is \(\xi_p(Y_p-\xi_p/2)\) and its divergence is \(w_p(p)-\|w_p\|_2^2/2\). The resulting centered residual is half the sum of the weighted centered squares of \(Y_p-\xi_p\). Its variance is
\[
  \frac{d_A}{2}\sum_{p,p'} a_pa_{p'}
       \langle P e_p-w_p,P e_{p'}-w_{p'}\rangle^2
       \ \le\ C r^{-8}.
\]
For the actual compact Wilson density every derivative hits the plaquette or one of the occurrences of its link in the transport paths. A path derivative costs at most \(Cr/\sqrt\beta\) times one scaled plaquette per coefficient. In a common weak box the paths can be removed with an error bounded by their length times the link deviation in that frame. The square of the remaining expression uses joint moments of at most four scaled plaquettes. The coefficient sums above, the uniform moment comparison of §22, and the \(O(r/l)\) fraction of tiling offsets at which the stencil straddles a side-\(l\) box give a total error at most \(C r^7\beta^{-\epsilon/6}\). This is \(o(r^{-8})\) on the stated window. At a fixed physical radius the same calculation at the largest admitted inner radius gives \(O(\beta^{-8\epsilon/100})\) for the squared score, by projection. The needed order \(a^8\) at that physical radius remains open. Original-link vector field, moment estimates and signs

The same upper and lower orders hold uniformly in the 21-dimensional coefficient space of §29. For a finitely supported real combination of based plaquette fluxes with weights \(u\), take \(v_u=\sum_p u_p v_p\), \(w_u=dv_u\) and replace \(Y_p,\xi_p\) in the velocity by \(Y_u,\sum_q w_u(q)Y_q\). Twice the resulting field gives the divergence for a full squared norm. Polarization gives inner products of two clovers. Each oriented term keeps its actual based loop, signs and transport, and the trace part of the tensor uses its own finite sum of Wilson energies. Finite-dimensional linearity bounds the squared residual by \(C\operatorname{tr}(H^2)r^{-8}\) for \(F=\beta(A_H-\mathbb EA_H)\). For the lower bound project onto the two exterior cuts. By (O.43) the limiting rescaled Gram matrix on those cuts has quadratic form \(2d_A c^2\operatorname{tr}(H^2)\), strictly positive for every nonzero symmetric coefficient. Convergence of this fixed-size matrix gives a common lower constant. Thus the full exterior has squared response of order \(\operatorname{tr}(H^2)r^{-8}\), including the pseudoscalar. This uses the bare lattice placements and the same growing microscopic window as (O.79).

### 63. Simultaneously retaining the interiors of the original success tests

In §60 the estimate of mass lost at the hard cutoffs can be extended through the entire first successful erasure window of length \(N=k-h\). Fix its real terminal data, residual histories and original represented charts. Let \(m\) be the number of active eligible rectangles and \(u\) the number of tagged ones. At each original step \(j\), the earlier preliminary log activities in LFR I (1.61) obey (1.70) on the descended domain. By (1.63) each carrier meets its birth annulus. At native age \(a\) the union of the rectangles supplies at most \(C_M(1+m)(1+R_k)^4 L^{4(k-a)}\) touching cells. The remaining carrier sum at a fixed cell is bounded by spare tree decay. Sum over the at most \((1+N)^2\) birth and age choices, then apply Cauchy on a single fluctuation disk of radius \(c\min(1,r_j)\). In the ordinary action's quadratic terms the two sums over current bond indices each cost at most \(C_M L^{4N}\) times a carrier polynomial. The one-bond radial slope \(D_j\) is consequently bounded by \(C_M(1+m)L^{12N}\) times a fixed polynomial in the local cutoff parameters, their inverse disk margins, \(R_k\) and \(1+N\). This estimates a sum in the original smooth *logarithm* before exponential weighting.

To count the loss, reveal the original innovations in reverse order, starting at \(k-1\) and ending at \(h\). Both the full and the regional center of the \(j\)-test are known before its innovation is revealed. All uneliminated previous determining coordinates are already in the fixed residual data. If their maximum center displacement is \(t_j\) in the original scaled coordinates, failure of the narrower regional test on an originally successful link requires that link to lie in its true inner annulus of width \(2t_j\). The one-link bound of §59 and the number of tag bonds give, for the mass \(K_{\rm deep}\) passing all the narrower tests on the same original parent of mass \(K\),
\[
  0\le K-K_{\rm deep}\le K\min\!\left(1,
   C u(1+R_k)^4\sum_{j=h}^{k-1} L^{4(k-j)}t_j(D_j+r_j^{-1})\right).
  \tag{O.81}
\]
This bound also weights a bounded marked numerator by its original mass and mark supremum. On a comparable weak corridor with \(N\le C_N R_k\), the explicit factor \(L^{16N}\) is absorbed if the native collar satisfies \(\delta M>16C_N\log L\). The logarithm of the local cutoff polynomial is \(o(R_k)\), and the first line of (1.57) contributes at least \(e^{-\delta M R_k}\). For any fixed \(0<c<\delta M-16C_N\log L\), lowering the weak ceiling yields a relative loss bounded by \(\min(1,C_{M,L}u(1+m)e^{-cR_k})\). Formula (O.81) remains the bound when that numerical margin has not been checked. This establishes simultaneous mass control on the actual original successful window. Construction of the normalized source on the independent background charts of a partially integrated window remains open. Carrier count, reveal order and explicit constants

### 64. A physical-length thin Wilson transport has exponentially small normalized response

Let \(\pi\) be a fixed nontrivial irreducible unitary representation of \(SU(N)\). Take a path containing \(m\) distinct links traversed once, with no plaquette incident to two of the selected links. Let \(\eta\) reveal all other links. Conditional on \(\eta\) the selected links are independent under the actual Wilson measure. Denote the ordered path transport by \(G\). For an arbitrary complement-measurable matrix \(M(\eta)\), put \(h=\operatorname{tr}(M(\eta)\pi(G))\). For \(m\ge C_\pi(1+\beta)\) one has
\[
  |\mathbb E[F h]|\le C_\pi e^{-c_\pi m/(1+\beta)}
                                \|F\|_2\|h\|_2,
       \qquad F\in L^2(\eta).
  \tag{O.82}
\]
This permits intermediate transports and either orientation on each selected link. A simple path of length \(s\) admits such a set of size at least \(c s\), by coloring the bounded-degree plaquette incidence graph. The projection from a centered bulk cube onto this class of thin-path boundary tests is therefore exponentially small at physical length \(s\asymp a^{-1}\). The representation and path are fixed for this normalized test bound. General square-integrable boundary observables can use multiple paths and singlet channels.

For a single selected link write its conditional density as \(p(U)\propto e^{-\beta S_b(U)}\). Its action has uniformly bounded first and second invariant derivatives for every exterior, since only a fixed number of plaquettes meet the link. Integration by parts gives \(\beta\mathbb E_b|\nabla S_b|^2=\mathbb E_b\Delta S_b\le C_N\), with \(\Delta\) the sum of squares of invariant derivatives. Thus a set of conditional probability at least one half has \(\beta|\nabla S_b|\le C\sqrt{1+\beta}\). On that set \(p(e^XU)\ge c p(U)\) for \(|X|\le c'(1+\beta)^{-1/2}\), by the uniform second derivative bound. The conditional law consequently dominates a fixed positive fraction of its restriction to this set convolved on the left with the normalized central Haar ball of that radius. In representation \(\pi\) the ball average is \(\kappa I\). The second-order character expansion at identity and the positive quadratic Casimir give \(1-|\kappa|^2\ge c_\pi/(1+\beta)\) for a sufficiently small fixed \(c'\). For a unit vector \(v\), the variance of \(\pi(U)v\) on the original law dominates the integrated conditional variance from this ball. Hence \(\|\mathbb E_b\pi(U)\|\le1-c_\pi/(1+\beta)\) uniformly in the exterior.

Conditional independence multiplies these contractions along the path; all intervening group matrices are unitary. For the conditional second moment apply the same argument to each nontrivial irreducible summand of \(\pi\otimes\bar\pi\). The trivial summand gives exactly the Haar value \(\|M\|_{\rm HS}^2/\dim\pi\) by Schur orthogonality. The remaining contribution has absolute value at most \(C_\pi e^{-c_\pi m/(1+\beta)}\|M\|_{\rm HS}^2\), including when \(M\) depends on the entire complement. At the stated lengths the resulting conditional second moment is bounded below by half its Haar value. The conditional first-moment estimate, the tower law and Cauchy–Schwarz give (O.82).

### 65. Identifying the fixed-box exterior response from Wilson loops on its boundary

At a fixed box radius in §62 the whole exterior response has an explicit weak-coupling limit. Let \(I\) be the hidden links, \(d_I\) their zero-extended lattice curl, and \(P_I\) the orthogonal projection onto its finite-dimensional range. For each source plaquette choose \(v_p\) supported in \(I\) with \(w_p=dv_p=P_I e_p\). Use these coefficients in the vector field of (O.80). The real one-chain
\[
           J_p=d^*(e_p-w_p)
\]
has zero divergence by the chain identity. It vanishes on the hidden links by the normal equations. Its remaining support lies at the box boundary for an interior source. On the connected visible graph in a fixed collar, choose a spanning tree and decompose \(J_p\) into fundamental cycles. A coefficient is just the oriented current on the corresponding edge outside the tree, since subtracting these cycles leaves a divergence-free flow on a tree. The visible paths tying the cycles to a common basepoint give an actual observable of the exterior links.

For each based cycle \(\gamma\) in this construction, take the traceless skew part of its Wilson holonomy times \(\sqrt\beta\), and let \(Z_p\) be the sum with the cycle coefficients of \(J_p\). Put \(D_r=\tfrac12\sum_p a_p(\|Z_p\|^2-\mathbb E\|Z_p\|^2)\), with the original cube weights. On a prescribed weak sequence with fixed \(r\),
\[
 \|\mathbb E[F\mid\mathcal E_r]-D_r\|_2\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \|\mathbb E[F\mid\mathcal E_r]\|_2^2\longrightarrow
 \frac{d_A}{2}\sum_{p,p'}a_pa_{p'}
      \langle(P-P_I)e_p,(P-P_I)e_{p'}\rangle^2 . \tag{O.83}
\]
Indeed each fixed cycle can be filled by finitely many oriented plaquettes in a common contractible patch. An ordered product of the transported filling plaquettes equals its based holonomy. In the local weak limit its scaled skew part has the same leading flux as the sum of their linear Gaussian curls. Products and the transport back to the common base can be compared in the common frame of §22. At fixed radius the local energy moments and unitary telescoping bound the moments of these finite products. The total flat linear variable is \(J_p\cdot A=(e_p-w_p)\cdot dA\) by discrete summation by parts, independently of the filling. Thus \(F-k_X\) and \(D_r\) have the same leading centered quadratic polynomial on the same local fluctuations. Their squared difference tends to zero by the joint moment comparison. Projecting onto the exterior annihilates \(k_X\) and retains \(D_r\). Wick contraction and \(P w_p=w_p\) give the second limit in (O.83). Filling a cycle in this argument computes its moments; measurement of \(D_r\) uses its exterior links alone. Passing (O.83) to physical radii would require control of the error in this identification for long Wilson loops.

### 66. Size of the explicit boundary-loop observable at physical radius

For a cubical box, choose a spanning tree on its visible boundary graph. Write \(F_x\) for tree transport from a common root to \(x\). A boundary edge \(e=(x,y)\) outside the tree determines the fundamental holonomy \(F_x U_e F_y^{-1}\); let \(K_e\) be its traceless skew part. For real coefficients \(j_e\) and a box contained in a single torus lift, the Wilson law satisfies
\[
 \mathbb E\Big\|\sqrt\beta\sum_e j_e K_e\Big\|^4
        \le C_N\beta^2(1+\beta)^6\Big(\sum_e |j_e|^2\Big)^2 .
 \tag{O.84}
\]
This uses the actual measure at every radius. The power six comes from two summations over a three-dimensional surface.

To see the correlation estimate behind (O.84), fix four chord occurrences, allowing repetitions. Retain the part of the tree joining their endpoints and the root. Suppressing vertices of degree two leaves a bounded number of segments. Each segment transport occurs in a tensor product of at most eight fundamental or dual representations. Decompose its action into invariant and nontrivial summands. Use the full isotypic projector for each irreducible type. It can be written as a central character integral of an auxiliary group element acting on that segment. Holding the nontrivial auxiliary elements fixed, the invariant projector equals independent Haar integration of its segment transport. In a fixed assignment remove these invariant segments, then join the endpoints of each of the four labeled chords by its own edge. If a component contains just one chord occurrence, the tensor contraction vanishes. In fact the Haar cuts permit independent rotation of the absolute rooted frames of the resulting tree pieces. Rotate all pieces meeting that chord component together. Its rooted traceless matrix transforms by conjugation and the other chord matrices keep their values. If the root is in that component, rotate the complementary pieces and use invariance of the full scalar contraction. Haar averaging of a single traceless matrix gives zero. This argument keeps the chord matrices fixed and uses invariant projectors only on the cut segments.

For every surviving assignment, choose a plaquette-independent set of links on the nontrivial segments, omitting chords. Its size is bounded below by a fixed fraction of their total length. Conditional on the other links, each segment in its assigned nontrivial representation carries the product contraction of §64. Decomposition into the fixed finite set of representations costs a constant independent of the tree length. Including the four unit chord edges, the remaining graph joins the four occurrences into groups of size at least two. Some pairing of the four locations has the sum of its two distances bounded by a constant times this graph length, by traversing spanning trees of the components. The absolute fourth-order contraction is therefore bounded by a constant times the sum over pairings of \(\exp[-c(d_{ij}+d_{kl})/(1+\beta)]\). On a fixed-width cubical boundary collar each resulting exponential row has sum at most \(C(1+\beta)^3\). Summation with \(|j_e|\) and Cauchy–Schwarz gives (O.84).

Apply this bound to the actual observable \(D_r\) in §65. The chord coefficients are a subset of the entries of \(J_p=d^*(Pe_p-P_Ie_p)\). The cutoff comparison of §62 implies \(\|J_p\|_2^2\le Cr^{-4}\), since \(P_Ie_p\) is the best interior curl approximation to \(Pe_p\). Thus, without a weak-box approximation at the radius of observation,
\[
                     \|D_r\|_2^2\le C_N\beta^2(1+\beta)^6 r^{-8}.
 \tag{O.85}
\]
At physical \(r\asymp a^{-1}\) on the matched trajectory the explicit coupling factors cost powers of \(\log r\). Equation (O.85) bounds a specified boundary observable in the original Wilson law. The needed upper bound on the full conditional mean additionally requires its squared distance to this observable, or a different estimate for the unresolved part of that mean.

For the centered relative cubical box there is a sharper conclusion. Use the conventional relative complex with its tangential boundary links fixed and normal interior-to-boundary bonds included in the hidden variables. The change of convention at a one-link collar does not affect (O.79). On two-forms the relative Hodge Laplacian separates on the coordinate intervals. Extend edge coordinates evenly and vertex coordinates oddly across the ends to the torus of doubled side. The interior solution can be taken as \(v_p=d_I^*\Delta_{2,\rm rel}^{-1}e_p\), so the velocity and its curl are sums of a bounded number of signed periodic image kernels of orders minus one and zero. On a frequency annulus of width \(s\), discrete summation by parts bounds those kernels by \(C_Bs^{4-m}(1+s\,\mathrm{dist})^{-B}\), with \(m=1,0\) respectively. The last annulus at inverse side length has a bounded number of modes. The zero mode drops out of the derivatives. Every image of a central source is at distance of order \(r\) from the boundary in at least one coordinate. Thus the boundary entries of \(w_p\) are \(O(r^{-4})\). There are \(O(r^3)\) possible current edges and \(J_p\) at each is a sum over a bounded number of incident faces. This gives \(\sum_e |j_e|^2\le Cr^{-5}\) and hence
\[
              \|D_r\|_2^2\le C_N\beta^2(1+\beta)^6 r^{-10}.
\]
In particular \(r^8\|D_r\|_2^2\to0\) for \(r/\beta^4\to\infty\). This includes fixed positive physical radii on the matched sequence.

For this same box, the identification of the full score by \(D_r\) also holds on the entire quantified microscopic window. The image estimates give \(\|v_p\|_1\le Cr\), \(\|w_p\|_1\le C\log(2+r)\), and \(\|J_p\|_1\le C/r\). Take a boundary tree with paths to the basepoint of length \(O(r)\). Each fundamental loop has an ordered filling by \(O(r^2)\) plaquettes transported along paths of length \(O(r)\), by successively moving its coordinate steps into fixed order. In the scaled skew holonomy, replacing this product by its linear sum of transported scaled plaquettes costs \(O(r^4/\sqrt\beta)\) in fourth mean. This follows by telescoping the unitary factors and applying the uniform plaquette moments to the pair errors. Summing with the chord coefficients gives \(O(r^3/\sqrt\beta)\) and a total linear coefficient norm \(O(r)\). The common-frame weak-box comparison from §62 now applies to the square of the residual \(F-k_X-D_r\). Its flat quadratic is zero, since its exterior filling and \(e_p-w_p\) have the same boundary current and the same curl projection. With the path, coefficient and offset bounds of §62 its squared norm is at most \(Cr^7\beta^{-\epsilon/6}=o(r^{-8})\) for \(1\ll r\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\). Thus the explicit predictor accounts for the full exterior response there. Its smaller size on physical boxes, proved by a different estimate, leaves the physical-size conditional remainder undetermined. Relative Hodge images and microscopic error · Fourth moment on the actual physical boundary

### 67. Exact partial integration through a finite successful window near a real background

Work on a finite first-erasure geometry in §63. Require the regional determining stencils and constrained blocks to split between the tagged and untagged components, and fix the incident coarse data and uneliminated determining coordinates. Retain the independent raw group bonds \(W_{j,T}\) and historical real algebra variables in each tag. The other bonds in its blocks obey their original constraints. Take a raw record for which every regional success test lies inside its ball by more than twice the displacement allowance of §63. The full domain of the remaining real innovations then has product support. Indeed start at the final real state and reconstruct backward. The regional center is already fixed by the record. At any realized later state the full center is within the uniform displacement of this regional center before the current innovation is chosen. The small logarithm of the fixed raw link relative to the full center is therefore in the original successful ball for every choice of the other independent innovations. The original success implication supplies the next admitted real state. This induction requires the actual separation of the determining data across the constrained blocks.

Let \(e_j\) denote the untagged real innovations, \(\Phi_j^T\) the original reconstruction with those raw tag constraints, and \(w_j\) the true normalized step density, including the Jacobian for expressing the tagged free coordinates in raw Haar variables. The partial density of the retained record obeys
\[
 S_h=1,\qquad S_{j+1}(v)=
 \int w_j(v,e_j)S_j(\Phi_j^T(v,e_j))\,de_j . \tag{O.86}
\]
Consequently the integrand divided by \(S_{j+1}(v)\) is the normalized one-step conditional density of the untagged innovations. The original tag marginal is retained outside this conditional operation. Bounded measurable functions of the literally fixed raw records can be inserted, including the Wilson trace of an ordinary tagged loop. A test of a dependent bond uses its reconstructed observable and corresponding regularity.

There is a zero-free complex neighborhood for (O.86) in both the independent background and current variables. Require a positive analytic margin for the smooth maps on the closure of the whole untagged real integration support in the chosen represented charts. At original stage \(j\) let \(d_j>0\) be an available inner chart radius, \(A_j\ge1\) a bound for the derivative of its raw-tag reconstruction, and \(G_j\) a bound for the derivative of \(\log w_j\) there. Use a smaller radius if needed for uniformity over those real innovations and the permitted historical parameters. For a terminal stage \(s\) put \(P_{j,s}=\prod_{i=j+1}^{s}A_i\). With \(0<\gamma<\log2\), take
\[
 \rho_s=\tfrac12\min\!\left\{\min_{h\le j\le s}d_j/P_{j,s},
                   \frac{\gamma}{1+\sum_{j=h}^sG_jP_{j,s}}\right\}.
\]
Each perturbed orbit for the same real innovations moves within the available charts and the sum of absolute changes of its log weights is at most \(\gamma\). Integrating against the entire original positive product at the real base \(v_*\) yields
\[
 \left|S_{s+1}(z)/S_{s+1}(v_*)-1\right|\le e^\gamma-1,
 \qquad \|\mathcal P^T_{h:s}(z)\|_{\rm TV}
                 \le e^\gamma/(2-e^\gamma). \tag{O.87}
\]
This estimate is relative to the true mass even for a rare tag. It holds on the indicated finite-volume neighborhood, with the minimum of the suffix radii if every intermediate step is required. Its radius may shrink with the geometry, depth and raw record. A domain and spatial decay strong enough to insert this normalized partial density at subsequent renormalization scales are still needed for the general source transport. Raw tag constraints, Jacobians and radius estimate, (C.55)–(C.59)

### 68. The variance retained when the exterior radius doubles

Let \(F\) be the centered, scaled Wilson cube of §62 and let \(Q_r F\) be its conditional mean given the entire exterior of the concentric relative cubical box of radius \(r\). For the prescribed sequences in §22 and \(r\to\infty\) with \(2r\le\beta^{\epsilon/100}\), there is a constant \(b_N>0\) for the specified cube convention such that
\[
  \|Q_r F\|_2^2=(b_N+o(1))r^{-8},\qquad
  \frac{\|Q_{2r}F\|_2^2}{\|Q_rF\|_2^2}\longrightarrow\frac1{256}.
  \tag{O.88}
\]
Section 66 gives the actual Wilson variance to error \(o(r^{-8})\) from the Gaussian residual in (O.83). For central plaquette faces the entries of \(r^4(P-P_I)\) have limits in the doubled-box image formula. In the direct image the equal-face discrepancy at doubled side \(n'\) is \(1/(2n'^4)\), by the rank-three curl symbol and its omitted zero mode. A bounded shift changes this discrepancy by \(O(n'^{-5})\), since the lattice first difference of the kernel is absolutely summable and periodizes exactly. Reflections control the off-diagonal orientations. For the other images, which stay a positive rescaled distance from the source, finite Fourier modes converge to those of the continuum torus and dyadic summation by parts gives a uniformly vanishing tail. This yields a common limiting variance coefficient, positive by the two-cut lower bound in §62. The exact conditional projection identity \(Q_{2r}Q_r=Q_{2r}\) then gives \(\|Q_rF-Q_{2r}F\|_2^2/\|Q_rF\|_2^2\to255/256\). Both radii in this result shrink in physical units. Nested-box estimate and the cubical constant

At a fixed physical box size a separate estimate controls every nonlinear function of a coarse grid of straight boundary transports. Each path has a set of at least \(m\) exclusive singly traversed links, with no plaquette incident to two selected links over the whole grid. The one-link argument in §64 has a gap uniform over nontrivial representations: compare translations by elements of a small ball to its mean-square displacement, and telescope \(O(1/s)\) such translations to cover the group for ball radius \(s\). The one-link conditional density has sup norm \(O((1+\beta)^{d_A/2})\) by its bounded log Hessian at a maximum. The first and last convolutions along a path use the corresponding square-integrable densities; the intermediate links contract every centered Haar mode. Each transport thus has conditional density within \(\delta_m=C(1+\beta)^{d_A/2}e^{-c(m-2)/(1+\beta)}\) of one, uniformly in its complement. For \(M\) paths the conditional law factors, and the true marginal also differs from product Haar by \(O(M\delta_m)\) when that quantity is small. On a boundary of side \(O(e^{C\beta})\), a compatible straight grid of mesh of order \(\beta^3\) gives \(\|\mathbb E[F\mid\text{coarse transports}]\|_2\le C e^{-c\beta^2}\|F\|_2\). The remaining conditional mean on the fine boundary at fixed coarse transports is still unbounded at the required physical rate. Joint nonlinear conditional estimate

### 69. Localizing eligible plaquette sources on the full successful branch

Fix a compatible real first-erasure history with eligible rectangular components, common exterior and the protected first-scale stencils specified in the source note. The whole copied plaquette stencil, its old real fluctuation and its frame must belong to its receiving component. For each eligible crossing plaquette \(p\) there is a bounded observable \(b_{p,T(p)}\) of that component and the fixed exterior. If \(G_{\rm br}\) is the true normalized branch generating function, then on a fixed small disk \(t=\sum_p|z_p|\),
\[
 \left|\log G_{\rm br}(z)-
   \log\mathbb E_{\rm br,0}\exp\!\left(\sum_p z_p b_{p,T(p)}\right)\right|
        \le C E_* t . \tag{O.89}
\]
The reference expectation uses the original branch law, and the bound is uniform in the number of components. In an unnormalized integral the true branch mass multiplies the error. The error \(E_*\) includes the small first-step fluctuation, the sum over fresh ordinary source generations, the displacement of the copied plaquette through the preliminary steps, and \(C(1+R_k)^4e^{-cR_k^3}\) from terms reaching beyond its component. For the last term the linear terminal localization retains the original plaquette anchor, so each such term pays for the exposed face strip in §58. The component term is bounded on the real support by the original Wilson trace bound plus this error. Cauchy on the common disk controls joint connected source derivatives. This is an absolute comparison and its error still lacks the decay with separation needed for the physically normalized two-point function.

For a protected first-scale coefficient the independent background and current can also be tested through one actual integration at the full allowed successful fluctuation amplitudes. Turning off any one of a sequence of disjoint separating walls in the propagator expansion disconnects the forcing from the complete projected stencil. Repeated Schwarz bounds on the wall variables then pay exponentially for the number of walls. The check must include the change of frame of the independent current, \((\operatorname{Ad}_u-1)J\), and the net perturbation must fit the old coefficient's analytic radius. A newly generated coefficient may depend on a larger region and additional real historical variables. Propagating the required separation estimate through that generation remains open. Generating functions, stencil hypotheses and independent-current check, (C.60)–(C.68)
